Exponential ergodicity and finite-dimensional approximation for Markovian lifts of stochastic Volterra equations
Yushi Hamaguchi

Abstract
This paper investigates the long-time asymptotics and the existence of stationary solutions for a class of stochastic Volterra equations (SVEs). To address the non-Markovian nature of SVEs, we employ a Markovian lifting technique, formulating a Markovian lift as the solution to a stochastic evolution equation (SEE) on a Gelfand triplet. Our main objective is to establish the ergodicity of this Markovian lift via the generalized Harris’ theorem, which in turn yields the asymptotic results for the original SVE. Despite the challenges posed by the highly degenerate, infinite-dimensional nature of the SEE, we achieve this by constructing a generalized coupling and a distance function that exploit the structural properties arising from the non-local operators in its coefficients. Furthermore, we prove that the invariant probability measure and, more generally, the stationary law on the path space of the SEE can be weakly approximated by those of finite-dimensional SDEs. This yields a novel approximation result for the stationary solution of the original SVE, while offering a rigorous mathematical framework that supports the validity of the Markovian embedding concept widely utilized in statistical physics.



Keywords:
Stochastic Volterra equation; stochastic evolution equation; Markovian lift; ergodicity.

2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
: 37A25; 60H15; 45D05; 60G22.

1
Introduction

The analysis of the long-time asymptotic behavior and the existence of stationary solutions for stochastic differential equations (SDEs) is a fundamental problem in stochastic analysis. In this paper, we investigate these properties for
stochastic Volterra equations
(SVEs) of the following form:

X_{t}=x(t)+\int^{t}_{0}K_{b}(t-s)b(X_{s})\,\mathrm{d}s+\int^{t}_{0}K_{\sigma}(t-s)\sigma(X_{s})\,\mathrm{d}W_{s},\ \ t>0.

(1.1)

Here,
b:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\sigma:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
denote the drift and diffusion coefficients, respectively,
W
is a
d
-dimensional Brownian motion, and
x
is a given
\mathbb{R}^{n}
-valued function called the forcing term, which is possibly random but independent of the Brownian motion. Introducing the (matrix-valued) functions
K_{b},K_{\sigma}:(0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
, which are called kernels, allows us to model dynamics exhibiting both sample path roughness as well as memory effects that cannot be captured by SDEs. Prominent examples include the rough volatility model in mathematical finance
[
6
]
and the (over-damped) generalized Langevin equation in statistical physics
[
41
,
45
,
64
]
. However, the inherent non-Markovian nature of the SVE (
1.1
) poses significant challenges for the analysis of long-time asymptotics, as standard results on the ergodicity of Markov processes—which are central to the study of SDEs—cannot be directly applied. Furthermore, the solution
X
to (
1.1
) typically falls outside the framework of semimartingales, which presents another significant challenge, as the classical Itô calculus is no longer directly available.

In order to overcome the difficulties arising from these non-Markovian and non-semimartingale features, various “Markovian lifting” techniques have been developed in the literature. By introducing suitable Markovian dynamics called
Markovian lifts
, which typically evolve in infinite-dimensional spaces, one can represent the solution of an SVE as a projection of such a lift. There are at least two well-established procedures for constructing Markovian lifts of SVEs. The first is based on representing the kernels by the Laplace transforms of certain measures, an approach adopted, for example, in
[
13
,
14
,
32
,
33
,
36
]
across various frameworks. This approach is related to the Markovian embedding for generalized Langevin equations
[
25
,
43
]
and multi-factor approximation of rough volatility models
[
1
,
3
,
5
]
; see also
[
2
,
34
]
for applications to optimal control problems of SVEs. The second is based on the analysis of the dynamics of auxiliary processes, such as conditional future paths or the past paths, as seen in
[
8
,
20
,
27
,
60
]
. We also refer to the recent work
[
9
]
, where an abstract framework is introduced to unify the two distinct approaches in
[
8
,
32
]
. Notably, the approach established in our previous works
[
32
,
33
]
allows for the study of Markovian lifts within the framework of stochastic evolution equations (SEEs) on Gelfand triplets. This framework is particularly tractable, by virtue of the Hilbert space structure and a connection to well-established theory of monotone stochastic partial differential equations (SPDEs) initiated by Pardoux
[
53
,
54
]
and further developed by Krylov and Rozovskiĭ
[
40
]
; see also the monographs
[
21
,
47
,
55
]
for a comprehensive treatment of monotone SPDEs.

In the present paper, we adopt and further generalize the methodology of
[
32
,
33
]
. Specifically, under the assumption that the kernels
K_{b}
and
K_{\sigma}
admit the representations
K_{b}(t)=\int_{[0,\infty)}e^{-\theta t}M_{b}(\theta)\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)
and
K_{\sigma}(t)=\int_{[0,\infty)}e^{-\theta t}M_{\sigma}(\theta)\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)
for some Borel measure
\mu
on
[0,\infty)
and measurable maps
M_{b},M_{\sigma}:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
satisfying suitable integrability conditions, we transform the SVE (
1.1
) into the following SEE:

\mathrm{d}Y_{t}(\theta)=-\theta Y_{t}(\theta)\,\mathrm{d}t+M_{b}(\theta)b(\mu[Y_{t}])\,\mathrm{d}t+M_{\sigma}(\theta)\sigma(\mu[Y_{t}])\,\mathrm{d}W_{t},\ \ t\geq 0,\ \theta\in[0,\infty).

(1.2)

Here,
\mu[Y_{t}]
represents a non-local term defined by the integral
\mu[Y_{t}]=\int_{[0,\infty)}Y_{t}(\theta)\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)
. The SEE is rigorously formulated within a Gelfand triplet of Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{V}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{H}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{V}^{*}
, which consists of weighted
L^{2}
spaces with respect to the measure
\mu
. The weighted integrability structure of this Gelfand triplet accounts for the singularity of the kernels
K_{b}(t)
and
K_{\sigma}(t)
at
t=0
. In this paper, we assume these kernels to be merely
L^{1}
and
L^{2}
near the origin, respectively, thereby allowing for their divergence as
t\downarrow 0
. This choice of integrability is minimal, as it corresponds to the necessary conditions for the Lebesgue and stochastic integrals in (
1.1
) to be well-defined. The resulting solution
Y=(Y_{t})_{t\geq 0}
to the SEE (
1.2
) is a time-homogeneous Markov process on the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
, from which the solution to the original SVE (
1.1
) is recovered via the formula
X_{t}=\mu[Y_{t}]
. In this sense, the infinite-dimensional process
Y
serves as a Markovian lift of
X
. For a more detailed exposition of this framework, the reader is referred to
Section

2
.

Our primary objective is to establish the ergodicity of this Markovian lift. Specifically, our main result (
3.8
) provides a spectral gap-type estimate and the exponential weak convergence of transition probabilities associated with the SEE (
1.2
) toward its unique invariant probability measure. These results, in turn, ensure the long-time asymptotics and the existence of stationary solutions for the original SVE (
1.1
), as shown in
3.10
. To the best of our knowledge, the only existing work addressing the ergodicity of such a lift for SVEs is the preprint
[
9
]
; see also
[
8
]
for a study on the long-time limiting distributions of SVEs using an alternative class of lifts based on conditional future paths, while assuming strong regularity for the kernels. In
[
9
]
, the authors introduce an abstract framework of Markovian lifts to prove a form of ergodicity without relying on the specific structure of the SEE. However, such generality necessitates stringent technical assumptions on the Lipschitz constants and growth conditions of the coefficients (similar constraints are also imposed in
[
10
]
where limit distributions for SVEs are studied without resorting to Markovian lifts), and a spectral gap cannot be expected therein. In contrast, the present paper significantly relaxes these constraints by employing more natural Lyapunov-type conditions and instead assuming uniform ellipticity for the diffusion coefficient
\sigma
, while providing not only exponential weak ergodicity but also a spectral gap-type estimate for the SEE (
1.2
). The spectral gap is crucial in its own right, as it ensures the stability of invariant probability measures, as will be elaborated below. Our results are also comparable to the works
[
7
,
18
,
37
]
, where ergodicity for a particular class of SVEs called affine Volterra processes is investigated by relying on the analysis of Volterra-type Riccati equations arising from the specific affine structure. We also refer to the recent preprint
[
24
]
, which establishes the so-called “fake stationarity” for SVEs with a linear drift coefficient
b
. For a given solution process, this concept refers to the invariance under the time-shifts of merely marginal distributions or moments up to a certain degree. In contrast, we do not assume a linear or affine structure for the coefficients, and the resulting stationary solution for the original SVE (
1.1
) possesses strict stationarity, meaning that all finite-dimensional distributions are invariant under the time-shifts. Detailed statements of our main results are provided in
Section

3
.

The proof of our main result is based on the
generalized Harris’ theorem
developed by Hairer, Mattingly, and Scheutzow
[
29
]
and further extended in
[
11
,
16
]
; see also the monograph
[
42
]
. This theorem extends the classical Harris’ theorem (see, e.g.,
[
28
,
48
]
), originally developed to establish ergodicity with respect to the total variation distance, to a broader setting involving weighted Wasserstein-type distances. Such an extension is crucial in our framework, as the Markovian lift generally lacks the strong Feller property (see
[
32
, Theorem 3.4]
); consequently, ergodicity with respect to the total variation distance is not to be expected. We review the precise statement of the generalized Harris’ theorem from
[
29
]
in
Section

2
. Within this framework, the most critical and challenging step is the construction of a suitable distance(-like) function
d
that satisfies the “contraction” and the “
d
-smallness” conditions required in this theorem. In
[
12
]
, the authors provide a set of verifiable sufficient conditions for the assumptions of the generalized Harris’ theorem in terms of the notion of a
generalized coupling
between transition probabilities. Accordingly, our task reduces to the construction of an appropriate generalized coupling and a corresponding distance-like function. However, this construction is still non-trivial, as it requires a deep analysis tailored to the specific structure of the model. A particularly formidable challenge we have to overcome is that the SEE (
1.2
) is a
highly degenerate, infinite-dimensional system
, meaning that the state space
\mathcal{H}
is typically infinite-dimensional while the noise, represented by the Brownian motion
W
, is finite-dimensional. In fact, the driving noise
W
can be as low as one-dimensional if the original SVE (
1.1
) is so, whereas the state space of the SEE (
1.2
) remains infinite-dimensional. This stands in stark contrast to some existing works on ergodicity for “effectively elliptic” degenerate SPDEs via the generalized coupling approach (e.g.,
[
12
,
23
,
26
,
29
,
52
]
), where the noise dimension is assumed to be sufficiently large to ensure that the determining modes are adequately excited. Despite these challenges, we succeed in constructing a generalized coupling and a distance function satisfying the required conditions in the general results of
[
12
,
29
]
by exploiting the specific structural properties of the SEE (
1.2
) induced by the non-local operator
\mu[\cdot]
in its coefficients. These specific structural features are typically lost in abstract settings, such as in
[
9
]
, where ergodicity for an abstract Markovian lift of an SVE is obtained via direct estimates of the
synchronous coupling
between transition probabilities under much more restrictive conditions mentioned above. It is also worth noting that, as a byproduct of our proof, we derive the
asymptotic log-Harnack inequality
for the SEE (
1.2
). This result extends our previous work
[
32
, Section 4]
, which was limited to scalar kernels, to the more general setting involving matrix-valued kernels, where
K_{b}
and
K_{\sigma}
may differ and
K_{b}
is not necessarily square-integrable. This inequality is of independent interest, as it implies several key properties for the associated Markov semigroup, including the
asymptotic strong Feller property
,
asymptotic irreducibility
, and
uniqueness of the invariant probability measure
, while providing a
gradient estimate
and an
asymptotic heat kernel estimate
; we refer to
[
4
, Theorem 2.1]
for these concepts and general results. The detailed proofs of our main results are deferred to
Section

4
.

As an application of the aforementioned spectral gap result,
5.3
demonstrates that the invariant probability measure and, more generally, the stationary law on the path space for the SEE (
1.2
) can be weakly approximated by those of finite-dimensional Markovian SDEs. These results translate to the original SVE (
1.1
) via
5.6
, extending previous studies on Markovian approximations of SVEs, such as
[
1
,
3
,
5
]
. While those works focus on the finite-dimensional approximation of solutions to SVEs over finite time horizons, the approximation of stationary solutions has remained an open problem. Furthermore, our approximation result provides a rigorous justification for the underlying principle of the “Markovian embedding” concept, a heuristic yet powerful idea in statistical physics that dates back to the pioneering works of Mori
[
51
]
and Zwanzig
[
63
]
and has been widely utilized in the analysis of generalized Langevin equations (see, e.g.,
[
25
,
43
]
and references therein). Specifically, this approach involves representing a non-Markovian process as a projection of a higher-dimensional Markovian system through the introduction of auxiliary variables. This procedure typically assumes that the kernels are expressed as finite sums of exponentials, in which case the associated auxiliary Markovian dynamics are finite-dimensional. In contrast, for general kernels (such as the power-law kernels discussed below), the associated Markovian dynamics become infinite-dimensional and are typically interpreted as “formal limits” in the physics literature
[
19
,
43
]
. While the ergodicity of such infinite-dimensional systems has been rigorously established in specific settings
[
22
]
, the connection between the stationary solutions of finite-dimensional approximations and those of the limiting infinite-dimensional system has not been fully explored. Although our Markovian lift adopts a slightly different formulation from the classical Mori–Zwanzig approach employed in
[
22
]
, our results provide a rigorous justification for the convergence of stationary solutions associated with these approximations. In this way, our work provides a rigorous mathematical framework to support the validity of Markovian embedding-type procedures across a wide range of physical contexts. In particular, our results are expected to provide a solid mathematical basis for related concepts such as the so-called functional Fokker–Planck equation formally derived in
[
38
]
in the context of statistical physics (see also
[
35
]
and
[
19
, Chapter 13]
for formal developments in the SPDE literature). Detailed statements of our approximation results are provided in
Section

5
.

To conclude this introductory section, let us discuss the applicability and limitations of our results, with a view toward applications to the theory of generalized Langevin equations, a central topic in statistical physics. Such equations describe the dynamics of a particle in contact with a heat bath where the friction exhibits a memory effect
[
41
,
64
]
. In
[
45
]
, the authors derive a one-dimensional over-damped generalized Langevin equation with a power-law kernel. By normalizing physical constants such as the temperature and the Boltzmann constant, the resulting dimensionless equation is expressed as:

X_{t}=X_{0}-\frac{1}{\Gamma(\alpha)}\int^{t}_{0}(t-s)^{\alpha-1}V^{\prime}(X_{s})\,\mathrm{d}s+G_{t},\ \ t>0,

(1.3)

where
\alpha\in(0,1)
is a constant,
V:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
is an external potential, and
G
is a centered Gaussian process. To satisfy the fluctuation–dissipation theorem (a fundamental principle in statistical physics establishing a precise relationship between the friction kernel and the random force, required for the system to reach thermal equilibrium
[
64
]
), the process
G
must be a fractional Brownian motion with Hurst parameter
\alpha/2
, scaled by the constant factor
\sqrt{2/\Gamma(1+\alpha)}
(see
[
45
, Sections 2 and 3]
). In this case, by virtue of the Mandelbrot–van Ness representation of fractional Brownian motion (see, e.g.,
[
49
, Theorem 1.3.1]
), the equation (
1.3
) can be rewritten as

X_{t}=x(t)-\frac{1}{\Gamma(\alpha)}\int^{t}_{0}(t-s)^{\alpha-1}V^{\prime}(X_{s})\,\mathrm{d}s+\frac{\sqrt{2\sin\frac{\pi\alpha}{2}}}{\Gamma(\frac{1+\alpha}{2})}\int^{t}_{0}(t-s)^{\frac{\alpha-1}{2}}\,\mathrm{d}W_{s},\ \ t>0,

(1.4)

with the forcing term

x(t)=X_{0}+\frac{\sqrt{2\sin\frac{\pi\alpha}{2}}}{\Gamma(\frac{1+\alpha}{2})}\int^{0}_{-\infty}\left\{(t-s)^{\frac{\alpha-1}{2}}-(-s)^{\frac{\alpha-1}{2}}\right\}\,\mathrm{d}W_{s},\ \ t>0,

where
W=(W_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
is a two-sided Brownian motion. This model corresponds to the SVE (
1.1
) with fractional (or power-law) kernels
K_{b}(t)=\frac{1}{\Gamma(\alpha_{b})}t^{\alpha_{b}-1}
and
K_{\sigma}(t)=\frac{1}{\Gamma(\alpha_{\sigma})}t^{\alpha_{\sigma}-1}
, where the exponents
\alpha_{b}\in(0,1)
and
\alpha_{\sigma}\in(\frac{1}{2},1)
are related by
\alpha_{b}=2\alpha_{\sigma}-1=\alpha
. Notably, the two kernels
K_{b}
and
K_{\sigma}
are distinct, and both exhibit a singularity at
t=0
. More importantly, for
\alpha\leq 1/2
, a gap in regularity emerges:
K_{b}
is only locally integrable, while
K_{\sigma}
is locally square-integrable near the origin. While existing works
[
8
,
9
,
13
,
14
,
20
,
36
,
60
]
, including our own
[
32
,
33
]
, typically require both kernels to be locally square-integrable, the general framework of Markovian lifts established in this paper (
Section

2
) removes this limitation. Specifically, by extending
[
32
,
33
]
, it allows for a locally
L^{1}
drift kernel
K_{b}
, which is essential for the analysis of the over-damped generalized Langevin equation but remains beyond the reach of prior methodologies. It should be noted, however, that our main results on ergodicity in
Section

3
and
Section

5
require the kernels
K_{b}(t)
and
K_{\sigma}(t)
to decay exponentially as
t\to\infty
, thereby excluding the pure power-law kernels mentioned above. Instead, our main results apply to tempered fractional kernels (also known as gamma-fractional kernels)
K_{b}(t)=\frac{1}{\Gamma(\alpha_{b})}t^{\alpha_{b}-1}e^{-\kappa_{b}t}
and
K_{\sigma}(t)=\frac{1}{\Gamma(\alpha_{\sigma})}t^{\alpha_{\sigma}-1}e^{-\kappa_{\sigma}t}
with
\alpha_{b}\in(0,1)
,
\alpha_{\sigma}\in(\frac{1}{2},1)
and
\kappa_{b},\kappa_{\sigma}>0
. Such kernels arise in dynamics exhibiting the subdiffusive–diffusive crossover behavior, characterized by subdiffusive behavior in the short-time regime followed by a transition to normal diffusion in the long-time limit
[
46
,
50
,
58
]
.

The remainder of this paper is structured as follows.
Section

2
provides the necessary preliminaries on the generalized Harris’ theorem and the Markovian lifting framework. Our main results on ergodicity and their associated consequences are stated in
Section

3
, while the key steps of the proofs for these results are deferred to
Section

4
.
Section

5
is dedicated to the detailed analysis of the finite-dimensional approximation of stationary solutions. Finally, some technical lemmas are collected in the
Appendix
.

2
Preliminaries

This section provides the necessary preliminaries for our analysis. First, we summarize the notation used throughout this paper. Next, we recall the generalized Harris’ theorem developed in
[
29
]
and introduce the Markovian lifting framework for SVEs by following and further extending the approach in
[
32
,
33
]
; these two frameworks serve as the main tools in the subsequent sections.

Throughout this paper, the standard Euclidean norm and inner product on a finite-dimensional Euclidean space are denoted by
|\cdot|
and
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
, respectively. For a matrix
A
,
A^{\top}
denotes its transpose, and
|A|_{\mathrm{op}}
denotes its operator norm. For a square matrix
A
,
\mathrm{tr}[A]
denotes its trace. We denote the
n\times n
identity matrix by
I_{n\times n}
.

For a separable Banach space
(\mathcal{X},\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{X}})
and each
T>0
, we denote by
C([0,T];\mathcal{X})
the separable Banach space of
\mathcal{X}
-valued continuous functions on
[0,T]
with the norm
\|f\|_{C([0,T];\mathcal{X})}:=\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|f(t)\|_{\mathcal{X}}
. Similarly, we define
L^{2}(0,T;\mathcal{X})
as the separable Banach space of
\mathrm{d}t
-equivalence classes of square-integrable functions on
(0,T)
with the norm
\|f\|_{L^{2}(0,T;\mathcal{X})}:=(\int^{T}_{0}\|f(t)\|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t)^{1/2}
. Based on these, we denote by
C([0,\infty);\mathcal{X})
the set of
\mathcal{X}
-valued continuous functions on
[0,\infty)
, equipped with the metric

d_{C([0,\infty);\mathcal{X})}(f,g):=\sum_{T\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{1}{2^{T}}\left\{\|f-g\|_{C([0,T];\mathcal{X})}\wedge 1\right\},

(2.1)

which makes it a complete separable metric space. Similarly,
L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(0,\infty;\mathcal{X})
denotes the set of
\mathrm{d}t
-equivalence classes of Borel measurable maps
f:(0,\infty)\to\mathcal{X}
that are square-integrable on every finite interval
(0,T)
. This space is equipped with the metric

d_{L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(0,\infty;\mathcal{X})}(f,g):=\sum_{T\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{1}{2^{T}}\left\{\|f-g\|_{L^{2}(0,T;\mathcal{X})}\wedge 1\right\}.

(2.2)

This also makes
L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(0,\infty;\mathcal{X})
a complete separable metric space. Throughout this paper, we strictly distinguish individual functions from their equivalence classes when considering their distributions.

For normed spaces
\mathcal{X}_{1},\mathcal{X}_{2}
,
L(\mathcal{X}_{1},\mathcal{X}_{2})
denotes the set of bounded linear operators from
\mathcal{X}_{1}
to
\mathcal{X}_{2}
. It is equipped with the operator norm
\|\cdot\|_{L(\mathcal{X}_{1},\mathcal{X}_{2})}
. When
\mathcal{X}_{1}
and
\mathcal{X}_{2}
are separable Hilbert spaces,
L_{2}(\mathcal{X}_{1},\mathcal{X}_{2})\subset L(\mathcal{X}_{1},\mathcal{X}_{2})
denotes the space of Hilbert–Schmidt operators from
\mathcal{X}_{1}
to
\mathcal{X}_{2}
, which is a separable Hilbert space equipped with the Hilbert–Schmidt norm
\|\cdot\|_{L_{2}(\mathcal{X}_{1},\mathcal{X}_{2})}
.

For each measurable space
(E,\mathcal{E})
,
\mathcal{P}(E)
denotes the set of all probability measures on
E
. For each
x\in E
,
\delta_{x}\in\mathcal{P}(E)
denotes the Dirac measure at the point
x
. For each
A\in\mathcal{E}
,
\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{A}:E\to\{0,1\}
denotes the indicator function of
A
. For
\nu_{1},\nu_{2}\in\mathcal{P}(E)
,
\mathscr{C}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})\subset\mathcal{P}(E\times E)
denotes the set of couplings between
\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{2}
; that is,
\nu\in\mathscr{C}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})
if
\nu
is a probability measure on the product space
(E\times E,\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathcal{E})
whose first and second marginals are
\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{2}
, respectively. For
\nu_{1},\nu_{2}\in\mathcal{P}(E)
,
d_{\mathrm{TV}}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2}):=\sup_{A\in\mathcal{E}}|\nu_{1}(A)-\nu_{2}(A)|
denotes the total variation distance, which is known to admit the following coupling representation:

d_{\mathrm{TV}}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})=\inf_{\nu\in\mathscr{C}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}\int_{E\times E}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{\{x_{1}\neq x_{2}\}}\,\nu(\mathrm{d}x_{1},\mathrm{d}x_{2}).

For
E
-valued random variable
\xi
on a probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
, we denote its law under
\mathbb{P}
by
\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(\xi):=\mathbb{P}\circ\xi^{-1}\in\mathcal{P}(E)
. The expectation under
\mathbb{P}
is denoted by
\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}[\cdot]
, or simply by
\mathbb{E}[\cdot]
if the underlying probability measure
\mathbb{P}
is clear from the context. For each probability kernel
P:E\times\mathcal{E}\to[0,1]
on
E
and each bounded measurable function
f:E\to\mathbb{R}
, we define
Pf:E\to\mathbb{R}
by
(Pf)(x):=\int_{E}f(x^{\prime})\,P(x,\mathrm{d}x^{\prime})
for
x\in E
. This definition extends to measurable functions
f:E\to[0,\infty]
, in which case
Pf
may take values in
[0,\infty]
. Furthermore, for each
\nu\in\mathcal{P}(E)
, we define
P^{*}\nu\in\mathcal{P}(E)
by
(P^{*}\nu)(A):=\int_{E}P(x,A)\,\nu(\mathrm{d}x)
for
A\in\mathcal{E}
. When
E
is a topological space, it is always assumed to be equipped with the Borel
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{B}(E)
.

2.1
Generalized Harris’ theorem

We now recall the generalized Harris’ theorem developed by Hairer, Mattingly, and Scheutzow
[
29
]
. This theorem provides a general criterion for spectral gap-type estimates and the weak convergence of Markov semigroups toward their unique invariant probability measures.

Let
E
be a Polish space. As in
[
29
]
, we call a function
d:E\times E\to[0,\infty)
a
distance-like function
if it is symmetric, lower semi-continuous, and such that
d(x_{1},x_{2})=0
if and only if
x_{1}=x_{2}
. Given a distance-like function
d:E\times E\to[0,\infty)
, we define
\mathbb{W}_{d}:\mathcal{P}(E)\times\mathcal{P}(E)\to[0,\infty]
by

\mathbb{W}_{d}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2}):=\inf_{\nu\in\mathscr{C}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}\int_{E\times E}d(x_{1},x_{2})\,\nu(\mathrm{d}x_{1},\mathrm{d}x_{2}),\ \ \nu_{1},\nu_{2}\in\mathcal{P}(E).

(2.3)

Note that if
d
is a distance function on
E
,
\mathbb{W}_{d}
coincides with the usual
L^{1}
-Wasserstein metric. On the other hand, if
d(x_{1},x_{2})=\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{\{x_{1}\neq x_{2}\}}
,
\mathbb{W}_{d}
coincides with the total variation distance. Following
[
29
]
, we adopt the following pair of notions which are central to our analysis.

Definition 2.1
.

Let
P:E\times\mathcal{B}(E)\to[0,1]
be a probability kernel on a Polish space
E
, and let
d:E\times E\to[0,1]
be a distance-like function (bounded by
1
).

•

We say that the distance-like function
d
is
contracting
for
P
if there exists a constant
\alpha\in(0,1)
such that

\mathbb{W}_{d}(P(x_{1},\cdot),P(x_{2},\cdot))\leq\alpha d(x_{1},x_{2})

for any
x_{1},x_{2}\in E
with
d(x_{1},x_{2})<1
.

•

A set
B\subset E
is said to be
d
-small
for
P
if there exists a constant
\varepsilon>0
such that

\mathbb{W}_{d}(P(x_{1},\cdot),P(x_{2},\cdot))\leq 1-\varepsilon

for any
x_{1},x_{2}\in B
.

Remark 2.2
.

The contractivity condition above imposes no restrictions on pairs
(x_{1},x_{2})
with
d(x_{1},x_{2})=1
, even though this set might be large. Notably, if we take
d(x_{1},x_{2})=\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{\{x_{1}\neq x_{2}\}}
, which corresponds to the total variation distance, the contractivity is trivially satisfied by
any
probability kernel since the condition
d(x_{1},x_{2})<1
implies
x_{1}=x_{2}
. In this setting, the notion of a
d
-small set coincides with the notion of a “small set”, one of the crucial assumptions in the classical Harris’ theorem on ergodicity with respect to the total variation distance (see, e.g.,
[
28
,
48
]
). For more detailed discussions, see
[
29
, Section 4]
. Furthermore,
[
11
, Example 3.1]
provides a simple example of a probability kernel
P
on
([0,1),\mathcal{B}([0,1)))
where the entire state space
[0,1)
is
d
-small with respect to the Euclidean metric
d
, whereas there are no non-trivial small sets in the classical sense.

Let
\{P_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
be a Markov semigroup, that is, a family of probability kernels on a Polish space
E
such that
P_{0}f=f
and
P_{s+t}f=P_{s}P_{t}f
for any
s,t\geq 0
and any bounded measurable functions
f:E\to\mathbb{R}
. Below we summarize some fundamental terminology used throughout this paper:

•

We say that
\{P_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
satisfies the
Feller property
if the map
E\ni x\mapsto P_{t}f(x)
is continuous for any
t\geq 0
and any bounded continuous function
f:E\to\mathbb{R}
.

•

We say that
\{P_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
is
measurable
if the map
[0,\infty)\times E\ni(t,x)\mapsto P_{t}f(x)
is
\mathcal{B}([0,\infty))\otimes\mathcal{B}(E)
-measurable for any bounded measurable function
f:E\to\mathbb{R}
. This condition is clearly satisfied if
\{P_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
is stochastically continuous and has the Feller property; here,
stochastic continuity
means that
\lim_{t\downarrow 0}P_{t}f(x)=f(x)
for all
x\in E
and every bounded continuous function
f:E\to\mathbb{R}
. Indeed, these properties in conjunction with the semigroup property imply that
(t,x)\mapsto P_{t}f(x)
is jointly measurable for every bounded continuous function
f:E\to\mathbb{R}
, which extends to all bounded measurable functions by a standard monotone class argument.

•

A probability measure
\pi\in\mathcal{P}(E)
is called an
invariant probability measure
for
\{P_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
if
P^{*}_{t}\pi=\pi
for any
t\geq 0
.

•

A measurable function
V:E\to[0,\infty)
is called a
Lyapunov function
for
\{P_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
if there exist constants
\gamma_{V},C_{V},K_{V}>0
such that the bound

P_{t}V(x)\leq C_{V}e^{-\gamma_{V}t}V(x)+K_{V}

(2.4)

holds for any
x\in E
and any
t\geq 0
.

Theorem 2.3

(The generalized Harris’ theorem;
[
29
, Theorem 4.8 and Corollary 4.11]
)
.

Let
\{P_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
be a measurable Markov semigroup on a Polish space
E
satisfying the Feller property
1
1

1

In
[
29
, Corollary 4.11]
, the measurability of
\{P_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
is implicitly assumed.
. Assume that
\{P_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
admits a continuous Lyapunov function
V:E\to[0,\infty)
. Suppose further that there exists a distance-like function
d:E\times E\to[0,1]
satisfying the following conditions:

•

d_{0}\leq\sqrt{d}
for some compatible metric
d_{0}
on
E
.

•

There exists
t_{1}>0
such that, for every
t\geq t_{1}
, the distance-like function
d
is contracting for
P_{t}
.

•

There exists
t_{2}>0
such that, for every
t\geq t_{2}
, the level set
\{x\in E\,|\,V(x)\leq 4K_{V}\}
is
d
-small for
P_{t}
, where
K_{V}>0
is the constant appearing in (
2.4
).

Then,
\{P_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
possesses a unique invariant probability measure
\pi\in\mathcal{P}(E)
. Furthermore, there exist constants
r>0
and
t_{0}>0
such that the following spectral gap-type estimate holds:

\mathbb{W}_{d_{V}}\big(P_{t}^{*}\nu_{1},P_{t}^{*}\nu_{2}\big)\leq e^{-rt}\mathbb{W}_{d_{V}}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})

(2.5)

for all
\nu_{1},\nu_{2}\in\mathcal{P}(E)
and all
t\geq t_{0}
. Here,
d_{V}:E\times E\to[0,\infty)
is a distance-like function defined by

d_{V}(x_{1},x_{2}):=\sqrt{d(x_{1},x_{2})\big(1+V(x_{1})+V(x_{2})\big)}

for
x_{1},x_{2}\in E
.

Remark 2.4
.

(i)

If the distance-like function
d:E\times E\to[0,1]
itself is a compatible metric on
E
, then the first assumption trivially holds.

(ii)

If
V
is a Lyapunov function for
\{P_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
, then every invariant probability measure
\pi
must satisfy
\int_{E}V(x)\,\pi(\mathrm{d}x)<\infty
; see
[
11
, Lemma 4.1]
. Hence, taking
\nu_{1}=\delta_{x}
and
\nu_{2}=\pi
in (
2.5
) immediately yields the following exponential ergodicity: there exists a constant
C>0
such that

\mathbb{W}_{d_{V}}\big(P_{t}(x,\cdot),\pi\big)\leq C\big(1+V(x)^{1/2}\big)e^{-rt}

for all
x\in E
and all
t\geq 0
.

(iii)

As shown in
[
30
, Proposition 2.8]
, the estimate (
2.5
) yields the
L^{2}
-spectral gap estimate

\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}f\in L^{2}(E,\pi)\\
f\neq\pi f\end{subarray}}\frac{\|P_{t}f-\pi f\|_{L^{2}(E,\pi)}}{\|f-\pi f\|_{L^{2}(E,\pi)}}\leq e^{-rt},

provided that the set of bounded,
d_{V}
-Lipschitz functions is dense in
L^{2}(E,\pi)
and the Markov semigroup
\{P_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
is reversible with respect to
\pi
.
2
2

2

Note that the density of the set of bounded,
d_{V}
-Lipschitz functions in
L^{2}(E,\pi)
is automatically satisfied whenever
d_{0}\leq\sqrt{d}
for some compatible metric
d_{0}
on
E
.
Here,
(L^{2}(E,\pi),\|\cdot\|_{L^{2}(E,\pi)})
denotes the Hilbert space of (equivalence classes of)
\pi
-square-integrable real-valued functions, and
\pi f:=\int_{E}f(x)\,\pi(\mathrm{d}x)
.

In order to apply the generalized Harris’ theorem, the crucial and practically challenging step is to construct a distance(-like) function
d
which satisfies all the requirements in
2.3
; the construction of a Lyapunov function
V
is equally essential and is typically addressed separately. In
[
12
]
, the authors provide a set of verifiable sufficient conditions for the required assumptions in
2.3
in terms of the so-called
generalized coupling
between the transition probabilities
P_{t}(x_{1},\cdot)
and
P_{t}(x_{2},\cdot)
with
x_{1},x_{2}\in E
. Hence, the problem we have to address reduces to how to construct a “good” generalized coupling and a distance(-like) function
d
. However, this latter task remains non-trivial, as it requires a deep analysis depending on each model. We address this issue within our framework of Markovian lifts of SVEs, to be introduced in the next subsection, where the specific structure of the lifted SEE (
1.2
) plays a crucial role. For more details, see the discussion in
Section

4.1
.

2.2
Markovian lifts of SVEs

In this subsection, we recall and further generalize the Markovian lifting framework developed in our previous works
[
32
,
33
]
(see also
[
34
]
). Before doing so, let us present the following standard well-posedness result for the SVE (
1.1
).

Proposition 2.5
.

Let
K_{b},K_{\sigma}:(0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
be measurable maps such that
\int^{T}_{0}|K_{b}(t)|_{\mathrm{op}}\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty
and
\int^{T}_{0}|K_{\sigma}(t)|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty
for any
T>0
. Suppose that
b:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\sigma:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
are measurable maps such that
|b(x)-b(x^{\prime})|\leq C_{b,\mathrm{Lip}}|x-x^{\prime}|
and
|\sigma(x)-\sigma(x^{\prime})|\leq C_{\sigma,\mathrm{Lip}}|x-x^{\prime}|
for all
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
with some constants
C_{b,\mathrm{Lip}},C_{\sigma,\mathrm{Lip}}>0
. Assume we are given a filtered probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
satisfying the usual conditions, a
d
-dimensional
\mathbb{F}
-Brownian motion
W
, and an
\mathcal{F}_{0}\otimes\mathcal{B}((0,\infty))
-measurable map
x:\Omega\times(0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
\int^{T}_{0}|x(t)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty
a.s. for any
T>0
. Then, there exists a unique progressively measurable process
X=(X_{t})_{t>0}
satisfying
\int^{T}_{0}|X_{t}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty
a.s. for any
T>0
such that the equality in (
1.1
) holds for
\mathrm{d}t\otimes\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}
-a.e.
(t,\omega)\in(0,\infty)\times\Omega
. The uniqueness is understood in the sense that any two such solutions coincide
\mathrm{d}t\otimes\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}
-a.e. on
(0,\infty)\times\Omega
.

The above result can be proved by applying
[
31
, Proposition 2.4]
(see also
[
62
, Theorem 3.1]
) in conjunction with a standard localization argument with respect to the forcing term
x
; we omit the details here for brevity as the procedure is standard in the context of stochastic analysis. We view the solution space of the SVE (
1.1
) as the Polish space
L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(0,\infty;\mathbb{R}^{n})
equipped with the complete metric defined by (
2.2
). By virtue of the Yamada–Watanabe-type result in
[
44
]
, uniqueness in law holds for the SVE (
1.1
) under the setting of
2.5
. Consequently, the law of the
\mathrm{d}t
-equivalence class of the solution
X
on
L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(0,\infty;\mathbb{R}^{n})
is uniquely determined by the law of the
\mathrm{d}t
-equivalence class of the forcing term
x
.

Remark 2.6
.

In the setting of
2.5
where the kernels
K_{b}
and
K_{\sigma}
are assumed to be merely (locally)
L^{1}
and
L^{2}
, we cannot expect pathwise time regularity for the solution
X
. To recover pathwise regularity, additional conditions on the kernels
K_{b}
and
K_{\sigma}
are required; see, e.g.,
[
62
, Theorem 3.3]
.

We now present a Markovian lifting framework. First, let us introduce the following definition.

Definition 2.7
.

We call a triplet
(\mu,M_{b},M_{\sigma})
a
lifting basis
if
\mu
is a Borel measure on
[0,\infty)
,
M_{b},M_{\sigma}:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
are matrix-valued Borel measurable maps, and they satisfy

\displaystyle\int_{[0,\infty)}(1+\theta)^{-1/2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)<\infty,

(2.6)

\displaystyle\int_{[0,\infty)}(1+\theta)^{-3/2}|M_{b}(\theta)|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)<\infty\ \ \text{and}

(2.7)

\displaystyle\int_{[0,\infty)}(1+\theta)^{-1/2}|M_{\sigma}(\theta)|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)<\infty.

(2.8)

We say that a pair of kernels
K_{b},K_{\sigma}:(0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is
liftable
if there exists a lifting basis
(\mu,M_{b},M_{\sigma})
such that

K_{b}(t)=\int_{[0,\infty)}e^{-\theta t}M_{b}(\theta)\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)\ \ \text{and}\ \ K_{\sigma}(t)=\int_{[0,\infty)}e^{-\theta t}M_{\sigma}(\theta)\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)

(2.9)

for any
t\in(0,\infty)
. In this case, we say that the lifting basis
(\mu,M_{b},M_{\sigma})
generates the pair
(K_{b},K_{\sigma})
.

Remark 2.8
.

(i)

A slight modification of
[
34
, Lemma 4.1]
shows that a pair
(K_{b},K_{\sigma})
of kernels is liftable if and only if each of the matrix components
K_{b}^{i,j},K_{\sigma}^{i,j}:(0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
for
i,j\in\{1,\dots,n\}
is of the form

K_{b}^{i,j}=K_{b}^{i,j,+}-K_{b}^{i,j,-}\ \ \text{and}\ \ K_{\sigma}^{i,j}=K_{\sigma}^{i,j,+}-K_{\sigma}^{i,j,-}

for some completely monotone functions
K_{b}^{i,j,\pm},K_{\sigma}^{i,j,\pm}:(0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
such that

\int^{T}_{0}K_{b}^{i,j,\pm}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty\ \ \text{and}\ \ \int^{T}_{0}t^{-1/2}K_{\sigma}^{i,j,\pm}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty

(2.10)

for any
T\in(0,\infty)
. In particular, by virtue of
[
32
, Lemma 2.1]
, every liftable pair
(K_{b},K_{\sigma})
satisfies

\int^{T}_{0}|K_{b}(t)|_{\mathrm{op}}\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty\ \ \text{and}\ \ \int^{T}_{0}|K_{\sigma}(t)|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty

(2.11)

for any
T\in(0,\infty)
.

(ii)

If
(K_{b,1},K_{\sigma,1})
and
(K_{b,2},K_{\sigma,2})
are liftable, then for any
A_{b,1},A_{b,2},A_{\sigma,1},A_{\sigma,2}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
, the pair
(A_{b,1}K_{b,1}+A_{b,2}K_{b,2},A_{\sigma,1}K_{\sigma,1}+A_{\sigma,2}K_{\sigma,2})
is also liftable. Indeed, given a lifting basis
(\mu_{i},M_{b,i},M_{\sigma,i})
which generates
(K_{b,i},K_{\sigma,i})
for each
i\in\{1,2\}
, the lifting basis
(\mu,M_{b},M_{\sigma})
given by

\mu=\mu_{1}+\mu_{2},\ \ M_{b}=\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}}{\mathrm{d}\mu}A_{b,1}M_{b,1}+\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2}}{\mathrm{d}\mu}A_{b,2}M_{b,2},\ \ M_{\sigma}=\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}}{\mathrm{d}\mu}A_{\sigma,1}M_{\sigma,1}+\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu_{2}}{\mathrm{d}\mu}A_{\sigma,2}M_{\sigma,2},

generates
(A_{b,1}K_{b,1}+A_{b,2}K_{b,2},A_{\sigma,1}K_{\sigma,1}+A_{\sigma,2}K_{\sigma,2})
.

(iii)

For a given liftable pair
(K_{b},K_{\sigma})
of kernels, a lifting basis
(\mu,M_{b},M_{\sigma})
generating it is not unique. Indeed, there is flexibility in the choice of the measure
\mu
; however, once a measure
\mu
is given, the maps
M_{b}
and
M_{\sigma}
satisfying (
2.9
) are determined up to a
\mu
-null set.

(iv)

The seemingly technical set of integrability conditions (
2.6
), (
2.7
) and (
2.8
) for a lifting basis
(\mu,M_{b},M_{\sigma})
relates to the conditions (
2.10
) and (
2.11
) for the generated kernels
K_{b}
and
K_{\sigma}
. This set of conditions is natural when we consider the case where
K_{b}=K_{\sigma}=KI_{n\times n}
for some scalar completely monotone function
K:(0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
, a case adopted in
[
32
,
33
]
. Indeed, in this case, Bernstein’s theorem ensures that there exists a
unique
Borel measure
\mu
on
[0,\infty)
such that
K(t)=\int_{[0,\infty)}e^{-\theta t}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)
for any
t>0
. By
[
32
, Lemma 2.1]
, the condition
\int^{T}_{0}t^{-1/2}K(t)\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty
for any
T\in(0,\infty)
is equivalent to the integrability condition (
2.6
) for the measure
\mu
; furthermore, both conditions imply that
\int^{T}_{0}K(t)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty
for any
T\in(0,\infty)
, which is a necessary condition for the Volterra-type stochastic integral
\int^{t}_{0}K(t-s)\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}
to be well-defined. In this case, the triplet
(\mu,I_{n\times n},I_{n\times n})
is a lifting basis which generates
(KI_{n\times n},KI_{n\times n})
in the sense of
2.7
. Our set of integrability conditions (
2.6
), (
2.7
) and (
2.8
) thus serves as a natural generalization of the benchmark case to settings where the two matrix-valued kernels
K_{b}
and
K_{\sigma}
may differ and
K_{b}
is only (locally)
L^{1}
. Similar integrability conditions are also employed in
[
34
]
.

Example 2.9
.

(i)

(Sum-of-exponentials type kernels)
Consider the kernels

K^{\exp}_{b}(t)=\sum^{N}_{i=1}e^{-\kappa_{i}t}M_{b,i},\ \ K^{\exp}_{\sigma}(t)=\sum^{N}_{i=1}e^{-\kappa_{i}t}M_{\sigma,i},\ \ t>0,

where
M_{b,i},M_{\sigma,i}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
for each
i\in\{1,\dots,N\}
with
N\in\mathbb{N}
, and
\kappa_{i}\geq 0
are distinct constants. Such kernels are frequently employed to approximate more general kernels, as seen in the studies of the Markovian embedding for generalized Langevin equations
[
25
,
43
]
and multi-factor approximation of rough volatility models
[
1
,
3
,
5
]
. Clearly, the pair of kernels
(K^{\exp}_{b},K^{\exp}_{\sigma})
above is liftable. As a lifting basis
(\mu,M_{b},M_{\sigma})
which generates this pair, we can take

\mu=\sum^{N}_{i=1}\delta_{\kappa_{i}},\ \ M_{b}(\theta)=\sum^{N}_{i=1}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{\{\kappa_{i}\}}(\theta)M_{b,i},\ \ M_{\sigma}(\theta)=\sum^{N}_{i=1}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{\{\kappa_{i}\}}(\theta)M_{\sigma,i}.

In this case, we have
{\mathrm{supp}\,\mu}=\{\kappa_{i}\}^{N}_{i=1}
.

(ii)

(Tempered fractional kernels, also known as gamma-fractional kernels)
Consider the kernels

K^{\mathrm{frac}}_{b}(t)=\frac{1}{\Gamma(\alpha_{b})}t^{\alpha_{b}-1}e^{-\kappa_{b}t}I_{n\times n},\ \ K^{\mathrm{frac}}_{\sigma}(t)=\frac{1}{\Gamma(\alpha_{\sigma})}t^{\alpha_{\sigma}-1}e^{-\kappa_{\sigma}t}I_{n\times n},\ \ t>0,

where
\alpha_{b}\in(0,1)
,
\alpha_{\sigma}\in(\frac{1}{2},1)
and
\kappa_{b},\kappa_{\sigma}\in[0,\infty)
. The case
\kappa_{b}=\kappa_{\sigma}=0
corresponds to the fractional kernels appearing in the Riemann–Liouville and Caputo fractional derivatives
[
57
]
, which arise in the over-damped generalized Langevin equation (
1.4
). In contrast, the case where
\kappa_{b}
and/or
\kappa_{\sigma}
are positive corresponds to exponentially tempered fractional kernels. Such kernels arise in dynamics exhibiting the subdiffusive–diffusive crossover behavior
[
46
,
50
,
58
]
. Notably, unlike
\alpha_{\sigma}
, we allow
\alpha_{b}
to be smaller than
1/2
, so that
K^{\mathrm{frac}}_{b}
is not necessarily locally square-integrable. This is crucial in application to the over-damped generalized Langevin equation (
1.4
) derived in
[
45
]
, where the exponents
\alpha_{b}
and
\alpha_{\sigma}
are related by
\alpha_{b}=2\alpha_{\sigma}-1\in(0,1)
in view of the fluctuation–dissipation theorem. The pair of kernels
(K^{\mathrm{frac}}_{b},K^{\mathrm{frac}}_{\sigma})
above is liftable. To see this, let
\gamma_{b}
and
\gamma_{\sigma}
be constants such that

(2\alpha_{b}-1)\vee\frac{1}{2}<\gamma_{b}<\left(2\alpha_{b}+\frac{1}{2}\right)\wedge 1\ \ \text{and}\ \ (2\alpha_{\sigma}-1)\vee\frac{1}{2}<\gamma_{\sigma}<\left(2\alpha_{\sigma}-\frac{1}{2}\right)\wedge 1.

For example, we can take
\gamma_{b}=\frac{\alpha_{b}+1}{2}
and
\gamma_{\sigma}=\alpha_{\sigma}
, but now we keep the above flexibility. Set

\displaystyle\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)=\left\{(\theta-\kappa_{b})^{-\gamma_{b}}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{(\kappa_{b},\infty)}(\theta)+(\theta-\kappa_{\sigma})^{-\gamma_{\sigma}}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{(\kappa_{\sigma},\infty)}(\theta)\right\}\,\mathrm{d}\theta,

\displaystyle M_{b}(\theta)=\Gamma(\alpha_{b})^{-1}\Gamma(1-\alpha_{b})^{-1}\left\{(\theta-\kappa_{b})^{-\gamma_{b}}+(\theta-\kappa_{\sigma})^{-\gamma_{\sigma}}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{(\kappa_{\sigma},\infty)}(\theta)\right\}^{-1}(\theta-\kappa_{b})^{-{\alpha_{b}}}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{(\kappa_{b},\infty)}(\theta)I_{n\times n},

\displaystyle M_{\sigma}(\theta)=\Gamma(\alpha_{\sigma})^{-1}\Gamma(1-\alpha_{\sigma})^{-1}\left\{(\theta-\kappa_{b})^{-\gamma_{b}}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{(\kappa_{b},\infty)}(\theta)+(\theta-\kappa_{\sigma})^{-\gamma_{\sigma}}\right\}^{-1}(\theta-\kappa_{\sigma})^{-{\alpha_{\sigma}}}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{(\kappa_{\sigma},\infty)}(\theta)I_{n\times n}.

By a direct computation, one can show that
(\mu,M_{b},M_{\sigma})
is a lifting basis which generates
(K^{\mathrm{frac}}_{b},K^{\mathrm{frac}}_{\sigma})
in the sense of
2.7
. In this case, we have
{\mathrm{supp}\,\mu}=[\kappa_{b}\wedge\kappa_{\sigma},\infty)
.

Let
\mu
be a Borel measure on
[0,\infty)
satisfying (
2.6
). We denote by
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_{\mu}
the set of all
\mu
-equivalence classes of Borel measurable maps
y:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that

\|y\|_{\mathcal{H}}:=\left(\int_{[0,\infty)}(1+\theta)^{-1/2}|y(\theta)|^{2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)\right)^{1/2}<\infty.

We equip
\mathcal{H}
with the inner product

\langle y_{1},y_{2}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}:=\int_{[0,\infty)}(1+\theta)^{-1/2}\langle y_{1}(\theta),y_{2}(\theta)\rangle\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta),\ \ y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathcal{H},

which induces the norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{H}}
on
\mathcal{H}
. Then,
(\mathcal{H},\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{H}},\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathcal{H}})
is a separable Hilbert space. We identify the topological dual
\mathcal{H}^{*}
of
\mathcal{H}
with
\mathcal{H}
itself by the Riesz isomorphism
y\mapsto\langle y,\cdot\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
. Also, we denote by
\mathcal{V}=\mathcal{V}_{\mu}
the set of all
\mu
-equivalence classes of Borel measurable maps
y:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that

\|y\|_{\mathcal{V}}:=\left(\int_{[0,\infty)}(1+\theta)^{1/2}|y(\theta)|^{2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)\right)^{1/2}<\infty.

We equip
\mathcal{V}
with the norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{V}}
. Then
(\mathcal{V},\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{V}})
is a separable and reflexive Banach space, and the embedding
\mathcal{V}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{H}
is continuous and dense. Under the identification of
\mathcal{H}^{*}
with
\mathcal{H}
, we have a continuous and dense embedding
\mathcal{H}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{V}^{*}
, where the topological dual
\mathcal{V}^{*}
of
\mathcal{V}
is now identified with the space of all
\mu
-equivalence classes of Borel measurable maps
y:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that

\|y\|_{\mathcal{V}^{*}}:=\left(\int_{[0,\infty)}(1+\theta)^{-3/2}|y(\theta)|^{2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)\right)^{1/2}<\infty.

Note that the duality pairing
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathcal{V}^{*},\mathcal{V}}
between
\mathcal{V}^{*}
and
\mathcal{V}
is compatible with the inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
on
\mathcal{H}
in the sense that
\langle y_{1},y_{2}\rangle_{\mathcal{V}^{*},\mathcal{V}}=\langle y_{1},y_{2}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
whenever
y_{1}\in\mathcal{H}\subset\mathcal{V}^{*}
and
y_{2}\in\mathcal{V}\subset\mathcal{H}
. Hence, we have a
Gelfand triplet

\mathcal{V}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{H}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{V}^{*}
.

As shown in
[
32
, Lemma 2.5]
, the space
\mathcal{V}
is continuously embedded into
L^{1}(\mu)=L^{1}(\mu;\mathbb{R}^{n})
, but there is no relation between
\mathcal{H}
and
L^{1}(\mu)
in general. For each
y\in\mathcal{V}
, we set

\mu[y]:=\int_{[0,\infty)}y(\theta)\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta).

Then, the map
\mu[\cdot]:\mathcal{V}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a bounded linear operator. Moreover, as shown in
[
32
, Lemma 2.5]
, the following holds:

Lemma 2.10
.

For any
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a constant
C_{\mu,\varepsilon}>0
such that

\big|\mu[y]\big|^{2}\leq\varepsilon\|y\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}+C_{\mu,\varepsilon}\|y\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}

for any
y\in\mathcal{V}
.

Remark 2.11
.

(i)

Since
\|y_{1}+y_{2}\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}+\|y_{1}-y_{2}\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}=2(\|y_{1}\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}+\|y_{2}\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2})
for any
y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathcal{V}
, the Banach space
(\mathcal{V},\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{V}})
becomes a Hilbert space equipped with the inner product which induces the norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{V}}
. Similarly, the Banach space
(\mathcal{V}^{*},\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{V}^{*}})
becomes a Hilbert space with the inner product which induces the norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{V}^{*}}
.

(ii)

We set
\|y\|_{\mathcal{V}}=\infty
for each
y\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\mathcal{V}
. Then, the function
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{V}}:\mathcal{H}\to[0,\infty]
is lower semi-continuous and hence
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
-measurable (see, e.g.,
[
47
, Exercise 4.2.3]
).

(iii)

We have
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{V})=\{A\cap\mathcal{V}\,|\,A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})\}\subset\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
and
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})=\{A\cap\mathcal{H}\,|\,A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{V}^{*})\}\subset\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{V}^{*})
, where the Borel
\sigma
-algebras are generated by their respective norm topology. Indeed, since the natural embedding
i:\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{H}
is continuous and injective, the Lusin–Suslin theorem (see, e.g.,
[
39
, Theorem 15.1]
) implies that
B=i(B)\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
for any
B\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{V})
, and hence
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{V})\subset\{A\cap\mathcal{V}\,|\,A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})\}\subset\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
. On the other hand, again by the continuity of
i:\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{H}
, we have
\{A\cap\mathcal{V}\,|\,A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})\}\subset\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{V})
, and hence
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{V})=\{A\cap\mathcal{V}\,|\,A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})\}\subset\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
. The relations
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})=\{A\cap\mathcal{H}\,|\,A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{V}^{*})\}\subset\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{V}^{*})
can be proved similarly.

Let
(\mu,M_{b},M_{\sigma})
be a lifting basis, and let
b:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\sigma:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
be Borel measurable maps. We regard (
1.2
) as an SEE on the Gelfand triplet
\mathcal{V}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{H}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{V}^{*}
specified above. Before defining the notion of a solution to the SEE (
1.2
), let us make some remarks on the coefficients.

Remark 2.12
.

(i)

By the integrability condition (
2.7
), the map
\mathcal{M}_{b}:x\mapsto(\theta\mapsto M_{b}(\theta)x)
is a bounded linear operator from
\mathbb{R}^{n}
to
\mathcal{V}^{*}
, and we have

\|\mathcal{M}_{b}\|_{L(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathcal{V}^{*})}\leq\left(\int_{[0,\infty)}(1+\theta)^{-3/2}|M_{b}(\theta)|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)\right)^{1/2}<\infty.

Similarly, by (
2.8
), the map
\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}:x\mapsto(\theta\mapsto M_{\sigma}(\theta)x)
is a bounded linear operator from
\mathbb{R}^{n}
to
\mathcal{H}
, and we have

\|\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}\|_{L(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathcal{H})}\leq\left(\int_{[0,\infty)}(1+\theta)^{-1/2}|M_{\sigma}(\theta)|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)\right)^{1/2}<\infty.

(ii)

For each
t\geq 0
, define
\mathcal{S}(t):\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{H}
by
(\mathcal{S}(t)y)(\theta):=e^{-\theta t}y(\theta)
,
\theta\in[0,\infty)
, for
y\in\mathcal{H}
. As shown in
[
32
, Lemma 2.5]
,
\{S(t)\}_{t\geq 0}
is a contraction semigroup on
\mathcal{H}
, and the associated infinitesimal generator
\mathcal{A}:\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})\subset\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{H}
is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})=\left\{y\in\mathcal{H}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\int_{[0,\infty)}(1+\theta)^{3/2}|y(\theta)|^{2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)<\infty\right\},

\displaystyle(\mathcal{A}y)(\theta)=-\theta y(\theta),\ \ \theta\in[0,\infty),\ \ \text{for $y\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})$}.

Moreover, it holds that

\langle\mathcal{A}y,y\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}=\|y\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}-\|y\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\leq 0

(2.12)

for any
y\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})
. Furthermore,
\mathcal{S}(t)
is a bounded linear operator from
\mathcal{H}
to
\mathcal{V}
for any
t>0
, and
\mathcal{S}(\cdot):y\mapsto(\mathcal{S}(t)y)_{t\in(0,T]}
is a bounded linear operator from
\mathcal{H}
to
L^{2}(0,T;\mathcal{V})
for any
T>0
. For more details, see
[
32
, Section 2]
.

(iii)

For each
t\geq 0
, it is easy to see that
\|\mathcal{S}(t)y\|_{\mathcal{V}^{*}}\leq\|y\|_{\mathcal{V}^{*}}
for any
y\in\mathcal{H}
. Consequently, the operator
\mathcal{S}(t):\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{H}
(viewed as a map into
\mathcal{V}^{*}
) uniquely extends to a contraction linear operator
\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}(t):\mathcal{V}^{*}\to\mathcal{V}^{*}
. The family
\{\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}(t)\}_{t\geq 0}
then forms a contraction semigroup on the larger Hilbert space
\mathcal{V}^{*}
. Following the same argument as in the proof of
[
32
, Lemma 2.5]
, we can show that the infinitesimal generator
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}:\mathcal{D}(\widetilde{\mathcal{A}})\subset\mathcal{V}^{*}\to\mathcal{V}^{*}
of
\{\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}(t)\}_{t\geq 0}
is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(\widetilde{\mathcal{A}})=\left\{y\in\mathcal{V}^{*}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\int_{[0,\infty)}(1+\theta)^{-3/2}\theta^{2}|y(\theta)|^{2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)<\infty\right\},

\displaystyle(\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}y)(\theta)=-\theta y(\theta),\ \ \theta\in[0,\infty),\ \ \text{for $y\in\mathcal{D}(\widetilde{\mathcal{A}})$}.

Note that
\mathcal{D}(\widetilde{\mathcal{A}})=\mathcal{V}
with the norm equivalence
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\|y\|_{\mathcal{V}}\leq\|y\|_{\mathcal{D}(\widetilde{\mathcal{A}})}\leq\|y\|_{\mathcal{V}}
for any
y\in\mathcal{V}
, where
\|y\|_{\mathcal{D}(\widetilde{\mathcal{A}})}:=(\|\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}y\|_{\mathcal{V}^{*}}^{2}+\|y\|_{\mathcal{V}^{*}}^{2})^{1/2}
. The operator
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}:\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{V}^{*}
is the unique extension of the operator
\mathcal{A}:\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})\to\mathcal{H}
(viewed as a map into
\mathcal{V}^{*}
). Combining the equality (
2.12
), the density of
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})
in
\mathcal{V}
, and the compatibility of the duality pairing
{}_{\mathcal{V}^{*}}\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathcal{V}}
with the inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
in
\mathcal{H}
, we obtain

\langle\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}y,y\rangle_{\mathcal{V}^{*},\mathcal{V}}=\|y\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}-\|y\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\leq 0

(2.13)

for any
y\in\mathcal{V}
. For simplicity, in what follows, the operators
\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}(t)
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}
will be denoted again by
\mathcal{S}(t)
and
\mathcal{A}
, respectively.

Definition 2.13
.

Let
(\mu,M_{b},M_{\sigma})
be a lifting basis, and let
b:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\sigma:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
be Borel measurable maps. Suppose that we are given a
d
-dimensional Brownian motion
W
on a complete probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
, with respect to a filtration
\mathbb{F}=(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
satisfying the usual conditions. We say that an
\mathcal{H}
-valued adapted process
Y
is a solution of the SEE (
1.2
) if the following hold:

(i)

The sample path
t\mapsto Y_{t}
is strongly continuous in
\mathcal{H}
a.s., and satisfies
\int^{T}_{0}\|Y_{t}\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty
for any
T>0
a.s.

(ii)

For any
T>0
,
\int^{T}_{0}\big\{|b(\mu[Y_{t}])|+|\sigma(\mu[Y_{t}])|^{2}\big\}\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty
a.s.

(iii)

The following equality holds in
\mathcal{V}^{*}
for any
t\geq 0
a.s.:

Y_{t}=Y_{0}+\int^{t}_{0}\big\{\mathcal{A}Y_{s}+\mathcal{M}_{b}b(\mu[Y_{s}])\big\}\,\mathrm{d}s+\int^{t}_{0}\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}\sigma(\mu[Y_{s}])\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}.

(2.14)

Remark 2.14
.

(i)

By the progressive measurability of the
\mathcal{H}
-valued process
Y
and the integrability condition in
2.13
(i), combined with the fact that
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{V})=\{A\cap\mathcal{V}\,|A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})\}\subset\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
, the process
\widetilde{Y}:=Y\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{\mathcal{V}}(Y)
is a
\mathcal{V}
-valued progressively measurable process such that
Y_{t}=\widetilde{Y}_{t}
in
\mathcal{H}
for
\mathrm{d}t\otimes\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}
-a.e.
(t,\omega)\in[0,\infty)\times\Omega
. The processes
\mu[Y_{t}]
and
\mathcal{A}Y_{t}
are then defined by
\mu[\widetilde{Y}_{t}]
and
\mathcal{A}\widetilde{Y}_{t}
, which are
\mathbb{R}^{n}
-valued and
\mathcal{V}^{*}
-valued progressively measurable processes, respectively. For simplicity, we denote this version
\widetilde{Y}
again by
Y
when no confusion can arise.

(ii)

In the right-hand side of (
2.14
), the integral with respect to
\mathrm{d}s
is understood as a Bochner integral in
\mathcal{V}^{*}
, while the integral with respect to
\mathrm{d}W_{s}
is understood as a stochastic integral in
\mathcal{H}
. These integrals are well-defined thanks to the integrability conditions (i) and (ii) in
2.13
; see also
2.12
. The solution
Y
is an
\mathcal{H}
-valued continuous adapted process, but it is not necessarily a semimartingale in
\mathcal{H}
; it can only be regarded as a semimartingale when viewed as a process in
\mathcal{V}^{*}
. This is because the
\mathcal{V}^{*}
-valued Bochner integral in (
2.14
) (which indeed takes values in
\mathcal{H}
) is not necessarily of bounded variation as an
\mathcal{H}
-valued process.

(iii)

The definition of the solution to the SEE (
1.2
) follows the standard “variational approach” for monotone SPDEs on a Gelfand triplet, a framework initiated by Pardoux
[
53
,
54
]
and further developed by Krylov and Rozovskiĭ
[
40
]
; see also the monographs
[
21
,
47
,
55
]
. Indeed, as demonstrated in the proof of
2.15
below, the SEE (
1.2
) with Lipschitz continuous coefficients
b:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\sigma:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
satisfies the standard “monotonicity conditions” on the Gelfand triplet
\mathcal{V}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{H}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{V}^{*}
. Solutions in the sense of
2.13
are commonly referred to as “variational solutions” in the literature. Furthermore, such solutions can be interpreted as “analytically strong solutions” on the largest Hilbert space
\mathcal{V}^{*}
by viewing the operator
\mathcal{A}:\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{V}^{*}
as the infinitesimal generator of the contraction semigroup
\{\mathcal{S}(t)\}_{t\geq 0}
on
\mathcal{V}^{*}
. In this setting, the coefficients
y\mapsto\mathcal{M}_{b}b(\mu[y\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{\mathcal{V}}(y)])
and
y\mapsto\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}\sigma(\mu[y\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{\mathcal{V}}(y)])
are regarded as measurable maps from
\mathcal{V}^{*}
to
\mathcal{V}^{*}
and
L_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathcal{V}^{*})
, respectively. However, it should be noted that these coefficients are generally not continuous as maps from
\mathcal{V}^{*}
(or even from
\mathcal{H}
) to their respective target spaces.

Proposition 2.15
.

Suppose that we are given a
d
-dimensional Brownian motion
W
defined on a complete probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
, with respect to a filtration
\mathbb{F}=(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
satisfying the usual conditions. Let
(\mu,M_{b},M_{\sigma})
be a lifting basis, and let
b:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\sigma:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
be measurable maps. Assume that there exist constants
C_{b,\mathrm{Lip}},C_{\sigma,\mathrm{Lip}}>0
such that
|b(x)-b(x^{\prime})|\leq C_{b,\mathrm{Lip}}|x-x^{\prime}|
and
|\sigma(x)-\sigma(x^{\prime})|\leq C_{\sigma,\mathrm{Lip}}|x-x^{\prime}|
for all
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Then, the following assertions hold:

(i)

For any
\mathcal{H}
-valued,
\mathcal{F}_{0}
-measurable random variable
Y_{0}
, there exists a unique solution
Y=(Y_{t})_{t\geq 0}
to the SEE (
1.2
) with the prescribed initial condition
Y_{0}
. Furthermore, this solution forms a time-homogeneous Markov process on
\mathcal{H}
.

(ii)

There exists a constant
C>0
, which depends only on the lifting basis
(\mu,M_{b},M_{\sigma})
and the constants
C_{b,\mathrm{Lip}}
and
C_{\sigma,\mathrm{Lip}}
, such that, for any two solutions
Y
and
Y^{\prime}
of the SEE (
1.2
) driven by the same
d
-dimensional Brownian motion
W
, we have

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|Y_{t}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+\int^{T}_{0}\|Y_{t}\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\mathcal{F}_{0}\right]\leq Ce^{CT}\left(1+\|Y_{0}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right)

(2.15)

and

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|Y_{t}-Y^{\prime}_{t}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+\int^{T}_{0}\|Y_{t}-Y^{\prime}_{t}\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\mathcal{F}_{0}\right]\leq Ce^{CT}\|Y_{0}-Y^{\prime}_{0}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}

(2.16)

for any
T>0
a.s.

(iii)

Denote by
(K_{b},K_{\sigma})
the liftable pair of kernels generated by
(\mu,M_{b},M_{\sigma})
. Let
Y
be a solution of the SEE (
1.2
). Then, the
\mathbb{R}^{n}
-valued progressively measurable process
X_{t}:=\mu[Y_{t}]
,
t\geq 0
, is the solution of the SVE (
1.1
) with the forcing term given by
x(t)=\mu[\mathcal{S}(t)Y_{0}]=\int_{[0,\infty)}e^{-\theta t}Y_{0}(\theta)\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)
for
t>0
.

Proof.

To establish the well-posedness stated in assertion (i), we apply a general result concerning the well-posedness of monotone SPDEs (see, e.g.,
[
47
, Theorem 4.2.4]
), combined with a standard localization technique. To this end, we verify the following conditions (H1)–(H4) required in
[
47
, Theorem 4.2.4]
(with
\alpha=2
in their notation):

(H1)

(
Hemicontinuity
)
For any
y_{1},y_{2},y_{3}\in\mathcal{V}
, the function

\mathbb{R}\ni\lambda\mapsto\big\langle\mathcal{A}(y_{1}+\lambda y_{2})+\mathcal{M}_{b}b\big(\mu[y_{1}+\lambda y_{2}]\big),y_{3}\big\rangle_{\mathcal{V}^{*},\mathcal{V}}\in\mathbb{R}

is continuous.

(H2)

(
Weak monotonicity
)
There exists a constant
C_{\mathrm{H}2}\in\mathbb{R}
such that, for any
y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathcal{V}
, it holds that

\displaystyle 2\big\langle\mathcal{A}(y_{1}-y_{2})+\mathcal{M}_{b}\big(b(\mu[y_{1}])-b(\mu[y_{2}])\big),y_{1}-y_{2}\big\rangle_{\mathcal{V}^{*},\mathcal{V}}+\big\|\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}\big(\sigma(\mu[y_{1}])-\sigma(\mu[y_{2}])\big)\big\|_{L_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathcal{H})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\mathrm{H}2}\|y_{1}-y_{2}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}.

(H3)

(
Coercivity
)
There exist constants
C_{\mathrm{H}3,1}\in\mathbb{R}
,
C_{\mathrm{H}3,2}>0
and
C_{\mathrm{H}3,3}>0
such that, for any
y\in\mathcal{V}
, it holds that

2\big\langle\mathcal{A}y+\mathcal{M}_{b}b(\mu[y]),y\big\rangle_{\mathcal{V}^{*},\mathcal{V}}+\big\|\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}\sigma(\mu[y])\big\|_{L_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathcal{H})}^{2}\leq C_{\mathrm{H}3,1}\|y\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}-C_{\mathrm{H}3,2}\|y\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}+C_{\mathrm{H}3,3}.

(H4)

(
Boundedness
)
There exists a constant
C_{\mathrm{H}4}>0
such that, for any
y\in\mathcal{V}
, it holds that

\big\|\mathcal{A}y+\mathcal{M}_{b}b(\mu[y])\big\|_{\mathcal{V}^{*}}\leq C_{\mathrm{H}4}\big(1+\|y\|_{\mathcal{V}}\big).

First, since
\mathcal{A}:\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{V}^{*}
,
\mu[\cdot]:\mathcal{V}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
b:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\mathcal{M}_{b}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathcal{V}^{*}
are (strongly) continuous, we see that condition (H1) (hemicontinuity) holds. Second, observe that, for any
y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathcal{V}
,

\displaystyle 2\big\langle\mathcal{A}(y_{1}-y_{2})+\mathcal{M}_{b}\big(b(\mu[y_{1}])-b(\mu[y_{2}])\big),y_{1}-y_{2}\big\rangle_{\mathcal{V}^{*},\mathcal{V}}+\big\|\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}\big(\sigma(\mu[y_{1}])-\sigma(\mu[y_{2}])\big)\big\|_{L_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathcal{H})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\langle\mathcal{A}(y_{1}-y_{2}),y_{1}-y_{2}\rangle_{\mathcal{V}^{*},\mathcal{V}}+2\|\mathcal{M}_{b}\|_{L(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathcal{V}^{*})}C_{b,\mathrm{Lip}}\big|\mu[y_{1}-y_{2}]\big|\|y_{1}-y_{2}\|_{\mathcal{V}}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\|\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}\|_{L(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathcal{H})}^{2}C_{\sigma,\mathrm{Lip}}^{2}\big|\mu[y_{1}-y_{2}]\big|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\|y_{1}-y_{2}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}-\frac{3}{2}\|y_{1}-y_{2}\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}+\big(2\|\mathcal{M}_{b}\|_{L(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathcal{V}^{*})}^{2}C_{b,\mathrm{Lip}}^{2}+\|\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}\|_{L(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathcal{H})}^{2}C_{\sigma,\mathrm{Lip}}^{2}\big)\big|\mu[y_{1}-y_{2}]\big|^{2},

where we used (
2.13
) and Young’s inequality in the last line. By using
2.10
with
\varepsilon>0
given by
\varepsilon=2^{-1}(2\|\mathcal{M}_{b}\|_{L(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathcal{V}^{*})}^{2}C_{b,\mathrm{Lip}}^{2}+\|\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}\|_{L(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathcal{H})}^{2}C_{\sigma,\mathrm{Lip}}^{2})^{-1}
, we obtain

\begin{split}&2\big\langle\mathcal{A}(y_{1}-y_{2})+\mathcal{M}_{b}\big(b(\mu[y_{1}])-b(\mu[y_{2}])\big),y_{1}-y_{2}\big\rangle_{\mathcal{V}^{*},\mathcal{V}}+\big\|\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}\big(\sigma(\mu[y_{1}])-\sigma(\mu[y_{2}])\big)\big\|_{L_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathcal{H})}^{2}\\
&\leq\widetilde{C}_{\mathrm{H}2}\|y_{1}-y_{2}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}-\|y_{1}-y_{2}\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2},\end{split}

(2.17)

where
\widetilde{C}_{\mathrm{H}2}:=2+C_{\mu,\varepsilon}(2\|\mathcal{M}_{b}\|_{L(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathcal{V}^{*})}^{2}C_{b,\mathrm{Lip}}^{2}+\|\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}\|_{L(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathcal{H})}^{2}C_{\sigma,\mathrm{Lip}}^{2})
with
C_{\mu,\varepsilon}>0
arising from
2.10
. In particular, condition (H2) (weak monotonicity) is satisfied. Similarly, noting the linear growth properties of
b:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\sigma:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
, a calculation analogous to the one above confirms that condition (H3) (coercivity) also holds. Lastly, since
\mathcal{A}:\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{V}^{*}
,
\mathcal{M}_{b}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathcal{V}^{*}
and
\mu[\cdot]:\mathcal{V}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
are bounded linear operators, and
b:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
exhibits linear growth, we conclude that condition (H4) (boundedness) is satisfied.

Based on conditions (H1)–(H4) established above,
[
47
, Theorem 4.2.4]
implies that, provided the
\mathcal{H}
-valued,
\mathcal{F}_{0}
-measurable initial condition
Y_{0}
is square-integrable, the SEE (
1.2
) admits a (unique) solution
Y
satisfying

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|Y_{t}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+\int^{T}_{0}\|Y_{t}\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\right]<\infty

for any
T>0
. For a general
Y_{0}
that is not necessarily square-integrable, we consider the
\mathcal{F}_{0}
-measurable partition
\{A_{N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
of
\Omega
defined by
A_{N}:=\{N-1\leq\|Y_{0}\|_{\mathcal{H}}<N\}
. Denoting by
Y^{N}=(Y^{N}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
the solution to the SEE (
1.2
) with the initial condition
Y_{0}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{A_{N}}
and setting
Y_{t}:=\sum^{\infty}_{N=1}Y^{N}_{t}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{A_{N}}
for
t\geq 0
, we see that
Y=(Y_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is a solution to the SEE (
1.2
) in the sense of
2.13
with the initial condition
Y_{0}
. Uniqueness of the solution follows from the estimate (
2.16
) in assertion (ii), which is established below. The fact that the solution to the SEE (
1.2
) forms a time-homogeneous Markov process follows, once again, from the general result
[
47
, Proposition 4.3.5]
on monotone SPDEs.

Next, we prove assertion (ii). Specifically, we focus on the proof of estimate (
2.16
), as estimate (
2.15
) can be derived in an analogous manner. Let
Y
and
Y^{\prime}
be two solutions of (
1.2
). Applying Itô’s formula for the squared norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}
(see
[
47
, Theorem 4.2.5]
or
2.19
below) to the
\mathcal{H}
-valued process
Y-Y^{\prime}
yields

\displaystyle\big\|Y_{t}-Y^{\prime}_{t}\big\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\big\|Y_{0}-Y^{\prime}_{0}\big\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+2\int^{t}_{0}\Big\langle Y_{s}-Y^{\prime}_{s},\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}\big(\sigma(\mu[Y_{s}])-\sigma(\mu[Y^{\prime}_{s}])\big)\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle\hskip 5.69046pt+\int^{t}_{0}\left\{2\Big\langle\mathcal{A}(Y_{s}-Y^{\prime}_{s})+\mathcal{M}_{b}\big(b(\mu[Y_{s}])-b(\mu[Y^{\prime}_{s}])\big),Y_{s}-Y^{\prime}_{s}\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{V}^{*},\mathcal{V}}+\Big\|\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}\big(\sigma(\mu[Y_{s}])-\sigma(\mu[Y^{\prime}_{s}])\big)\Big\|_{L_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathcal{H})}^{2}\right\}\,\mathrm{d}s

for any
t\geq 0
a.s. By the monotonicity estimate (
2.17
), we have

\begin{split}&\big\|Y_{t}-Y^{\prime}_{t}\big\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+\int^{t}_{0}\big\|Y_{s}-Y^{\prime}_{s}\big\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\\
&\leq\big\|Y_{0}-Y^{\prime}_{0}\big\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+\widetilde{C}_{\mathrm{H}2}\int^{t}_{0}\big\|Y_{s}-Y^{\prime}_{s}\big\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s+2\int^{t}_{0}\Big\langle Y_{s}-Y^{\prime}_{s},\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}\big(\sigma(\mu[Y_{s}])-\sigma(\mu[Y^{\prime}_{s}])\big)\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\end{split}

(2.18)

for any
t\geq 0
a.s. For each
N\in\mathbb{N}
, set
\tau_{N}:=\inf\{t\geq 0\,|\,\|Y_{t}-Y^{\prime}_{t}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}>N\,\text{or}\,\int^{t}_{0}\|Y_{s}-Y^{\prime}_{s}\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s>N\}
. Since
Y
and
Y^{\prime}
are
\mathcal{H}
-valued continuous adapted processes such that
\int^{T}_{0}\|Y_{t}\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty
and
\int^{T}_{0}\|Y^{\prime}_{t}\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty
a.s. for any
T>0
,
\{\tau_{N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
is an increasing sequence of stopping times such that
\lim_{N\to\infty}\tau_{N}=\infty
a.s. Fix
N\in\mathbb{N}
. Since
\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}\in L(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathcal{H})
,
\sigma:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
is Lipschitz continuous, and
\mu[\cdot]\in L(\mathcal{V};\mathbb{R}^{n})
, by the definition of the stopping time
\tau_{N}
, we see that the stochastic integral in the right-hand side of (
2.18
) stopped at
\tau_{N}
is a martingale. Hence, taking the conditional expectations
\mathbb{E}[\cdot|\mathcal{F}_{0}]
on both sides of (
2.18
), we obtain

\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{t\wedge\tau_{N}}_{0}\big\|Y_{s}-Y^{\prime}_{s}\big\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\mathcal{F}_{0}\right]\leq\big\|Y_{0}-Y^{\prime}_{0}\big\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+\widetilde{C}_{\mathrm{H}2}\int^{t}_{0}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{r\in[0,s\wedge\tau_{N}]}\big\|Y_{r}-Y^{\prime}_{r}\big\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\mathcal{F}_{0}\right]\,\mathrm{d}s

(2.19)

for any
t\geq 0
a.s. Furthermore, applying the conditional Burkholder--Davis--Gundy inequality
3
3

3

For any one-dimensional continuous martingale
M=(M_{t})_{t\geq 0}
on a filtered probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
and any
A\in\mathcal{F}_{0}
, applying the standard Burkholder–Davis–Gundy inequality to the martingale
(M_{t}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{A})_{t\geq 0}
yields that
\mathbb{E}[\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|M_{t}|\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{A}]\leq C_{\mathrm{BDG}}\mathbb{E}[\langle M\rangle_{T}^{1/2}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{A}]
, which implies that
\mathbb{E}[\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|M_{t}||\mathcal{F}_{0}]\leq C_{\mathrm{BDG}}\mathbb{E}[\langle M\rangle_{T}^{1/2}|\mathcal{F}_{0}]
a.s. for any
T>0
.
to (
2.18
) shows that, for some universal constant
C_{\mathrm{BDG}}>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{s\in[0,t\wedge\tau_{N}]}\big\|Y_{s}-Y^{\prime}_{s}\big\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\mathcal{F}_{0}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\big\|Y_{0}-Y^{\prime}_{0}\big\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+\widetilde{C}_{\mathrm{H}2}\int^{t}_{0}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{r\in[0,s\wedge\tau_{N}]}\big\|Y_{r}-Y^{\prime}_{r}\big\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\mathcal{F}_{0}\right]\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+2C_{\mathrm{BDG}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int^{t\wedge\tau_{N}}_{0}\big\|Y_{s}-Y^{\prime}_{s}\big\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\big\|\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}\big(\sigma(\mu[Y_{s}])-\sigma(\mu[Y^{\prime}_{s}])\big)\big\|_{L_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathcal{H})}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\right)^{1/2}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\mathcal{F}_{0}\right]

for any
t\geq 0
a.s. Since
\mathbb{E}[\sup_{s\in[0,t\wedge\tau_{N}]}\|Y_{s}-Y^{\prime}_{s}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}|\mathcal{F}_{0}]<\infty
a.s. by the definition of the stopping time
\tau_{N}
, the above estimate and Young’s inequality yield

\begin{split}\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{s\in[0,t\wedge\tau_{N}]}\big\|Y_{s}-Y^{\prime}_{s}\big\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\mathcal{F}_{0}\right]&\leq\big\|Y_{0}-Y^{\prime}_{0}\big\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+\widetilde{C}_{\mathrm{H}2}\int^{t}_{0}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{r\in[0,s\wedge\tau_{N}]}\big\|Y_{r}-Y^{\prime}_{r}\big\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\mathcal{F}_{0}\right]\,\mathrm{d}s\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt+2C_{\mathrm{BDG}}^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{t\wedge\tau_{N}}_{0}\big\|\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}\big(\sigma(\mu[Y_{s}])-\sigma(\mu[Y^{\prime}_{s}])\big)\big\|_{L_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathcal{H})}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\mathcal{F}_{0}\right]\end{split}

(2.20)

for any
t\geq 0
a.s. Concerning the last conditional expectation in the right-hand side of (
2.20
), since
\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}\in L(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathcal{H})
,
\sigma:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
is Lipschitz continuous, and
\mu[\cdot]\in L(\mathcal{V};\mathbb{R}^{n})
, there exists a constant
\widetilde{C}>0
, which depends only on
\mu,M_{\sigma}
and the Lipschitz constant
C_{\sigma,\mathrm{Lip}}
of
\sigma
, such that

\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{t\wedge\tau_{N}}_{0}\big\|\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}\big(\sigma(\mu[Y_{s}])-\sigma(\mu[Y^{\prime}_{s}])\big)\big\|_{L_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathcal{H})}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\mathcal{F}_{0}\right]\leq\widetilde{C}\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{t\wedge\tau_{N}}_{0}\big\|Y_{s}-Y^{\prime}_{s}\big\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\mathcal{F}_{0}\right]

(2.21)

for any
t\geq 0
a.s. Combining (
2.19
), (
2.20
) and (
2.21
), we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{s\in[0,t\wedge\tau_{N}]}\big\|Y_{s}-Y^{\prime}_{s}\big\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+\int^{t\wedge\tau_{N}}_{0}\big\|Y_{s}-Y^{\prime}_{s}\big\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\mathcal{F}_{0}\right]

\displaystyle\leq C\big\|Y_{0}-Y^{\prime}_{0}\big\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+C\int^{t}_{0}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{r\in[0,s\wedge\tau_{N}]}\big\|Y_{r}-Y^{\prime}_{r}\big\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\mathcal{F}_{0}\right]\,\mathrm{d}s

for any
t\geq 0
a.s. with a constant
C>0
depending only on the lifting basis
(\mu,M_{b},M_{\sigma})
and the constants
C_{b,\mathrm{Lip}}
and
C_{\sigma,\mathrm{Lip}}
. Thus, Gronwall’s inequality implies that

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,T\wedge\tau_{N}]}\big\|Y_{t}-Y^{\prime}_{t}\big\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+\int^{T\wedge\tau_{N}}_{0}\big\|Y_{t}-Y^{\prime}_{t}\big\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\mathcal{F}_{0}\right]\leq Ce^{CT}\big\|Y_{0}-Y^{\prime}_{0}\big\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}

for any
T>0
a.s. Then, taking the limit
N\to\infty
and using the conditional monotone convergence theorem yield the desired estimate (
2.16
). The estimate (
2.15
) can be proved in the same manner as above by using the coercivity (H3) instead of the monotonicity (
2.17
); hence, its proof is omitted.

Now we prove assertion (iii). Let
Y=(Y_{t})_{t\geq 0}
be a solution to the SEE (
1.2
) in the sense of
2.13
. By virtue of a standard localization technique with respect to the initial condition
Y_{0}
, without loss of generality, we may assume that
\mathbb{E}[\|Y_{0}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}]<\infty
. In this case, by (
2.15
), we have

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|Y_{t}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+\int^{T}_{0}\|Y_{t}\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\right]<\infty.

(2.22)

Note that the solution
Y
to the SEE (
1.2
) in the sense of
2.13
can be regarded as an analytically strong solution on
\mathcal{V}^{*}
; see
2.14
. Hence, the arguments in
[
15
, Chapter 5]
show that it is also an analytically mild solution on
\mathcal{V}^{*}
in the sense that

Y_{t}=\mathcal{S}(t)Y_{0}+\int^{t}_{0}\mathcal{S}(t-s)\mathcal{M}_{b}b(\mu[Y_{s}])\,\mathrm{d}s+\int^{t}_{0}\mathcal{S}(t-s)\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}\sigma(\mu[Y_{s}])\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}\ \ \text{in $\mathcal{V}^{*}$}

(2.23)

for any
t\geq 0
a.s.; see also
[
17
, Proposition 2.9 and Proposition 2.10]
for more details on relationships between analytically strong, weak and mild solutions for SEEs on separable Hilbert spaces. Let
m\in\mathbb{N}
, and define maps
\mu_{m}[\cdot]:\mathcal{V}^{*}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\overline{\mu}_{m}[\cdot]:\mathcal{V}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
by

\displaystyle\mu_{m}[y]:=\int_{[0,m)}y(\theta)\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)\ \ \text{for $y\in\mathcal{V}^{*}$},\ \ \text{and}

\displaystyle\overline{\mu}_{m}[y]:=\mu[y]-\mu_{m}[y]=\int_{[m,\infty)}y(\theta)\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)\ \ \text{for $y\in\mathcal{V}$}.

Clearly,
\mu_{m}[\cdot]
is a bounded linear operator from
\mathcal{V}^{*}
to
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and
\overline{\mu}_{m}[\cdot]
is a bounded linear operator from
\mathcal{V}
to
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Furthermore, by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, we have

\big\|\overline{\mu}_{m}[\cdot]\big\|_{L(\mathcal{V};\mathbb{R}^{n})}=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\in\mathcal{V}\\
\|y\|_{\mathcal{V}}\leq 1\end{subarray}}\big|\overline{\mu}_{m}[y]\big|\leq\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\in\mathcal{V}\\
\|y\|_{\mathcal{V}}\leq 1\end{subarray}}\int_{[m,\infty)}|y(\theta)|\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)\leq\left(\int_{[m,\infty)}(1+\theta)^{-1/2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)\right)^{1/2}.

Noting the integrability condition (
2.6
) for the measure
\mu
, the dominated convergence theorem yields

\lim_{m\to\infty}\big\|\overline{\mu}_{m}[\cdot]\big\|_{L(\mathcal{V};\mathbb{R}^{n})}=0.

(2.24)

We apply the operator
\mu_{m}[\cdot]\in L(\mathcal{V}^{*};\mathbb{R}^{n})
to both sides of (
2.23
). Since any bounded linear operator from
\mathcal{V}^{*}
to
\mathbb{R}^{n}
commutes with Bochner and stochastic integrals on
\mathcal{V}^{*}
(see, e.g.,
[
15
, Proposition 1.6 and Proposition 4.30]
), we obtain

\mu_{m}[Y_{t}]=\mu_{m}[\mathcal{S}(t)Y_{0}]+\int^{t}_{0}\mu_{m}[\mathcal{S}(t-s)\mathcal{M}_{b}]b(\mu[Y_{s}])\,\mathrm{d}s+\int^{t}_{0}\mu_{m}[\mathcal{S}(t-s)\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}]\sigma(\mu[Y_{s}])\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}\ \ \text{in $\mathbb{R}^{n}$}

(2.25)

a.s. for any
t\geq 0
. We take the limit
m\to\infty
in each term of (
2.25
). Recalling that
x(t)=\mu[\mathcal{S}(t)Y_{0}]
,
K_{b}(t)=\mu[\mathcal{S}(t)\mathcal{M}_{b}]
and
K_{\sigma}(t)=\mu[\mathcal{S}(t)\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}]
for a.e.
t\geq 0
, to establish assertion (iii), it suffices to show that, for any
T>0
,

\displaystyle\lim_{m\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T}_{0}\big|\overline{\mu}_{m}[Y_{t}]\big|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\right]=0,

(2.26)

\displaystyle\lim_{m\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T}_{0}\big|\overline{\mu}_{m}[\mathcal{S}(t)Y_{0}]\big|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\right]=0,

(2.27)

\displaystyle\lim_{m\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T}_{0}\left|\int^{t}_{0}\overline{\mu}_{m}[\mathcal{S}(t-s)\mathcal{M}_{b}]b(\mu[Y_{s}])\,\mathrm{d}s\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\right]=0,\ \ \text{and}

(2.28)

\displaystyle\lim_{m\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T}_{0}\left|\int^{t}_{0}\overline{\mu}_{m}[\mathcal{S}(t-s)\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}]\sigma(\mu[Y_{s}])\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\right]=0.

(2.29)

First, (
2.26
) follows from (
2.22
) and (
2.24
). Similarly, since the map
\mathcal{S}(\cdot):y\mapsto(\mathcal{S}(t)y)_{t\in(0,T]}
is a bounded linear operator from
\mathcal{H}
to
L^{2}(0,T;\mathcal{V})
, again by (
2.24
) we obtain (
2.27
). As for the expectation in (
2.28
), by Young’s convolution inequality, we have
