\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T}_{0}\left|\int^{t}_{0}\overline{\mu}_{m}[\mathcal{S}(t-s)\mathcal{M}_{b}]b(\mu[Y_{s}])\,\mathrm{d}s\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\right]\leq\left(\int^{T}_{0}\big|\overline{\mu}_{m}[\mathcal{S}(t)\mathcal{M}_{b}]\big|_{\mathrm{op}}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{2}\,\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T}_{0}\big|b(\mu[Y_{t}])\big|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\right].

Since
b:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
exhibits linear growth and
\mu[\cdot]\in L(\mathcal{V};\mathbb{R}^{n})
, it follows from (
2.22
) that
\mathbb{E}[\int^{T}_{0}|b(\mu[Y_{t}])|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t]<\infty
. Furthermore, observe that

\displaystyle\int^{T}_{0}\big|\overline{\mu}_{m}[\mathcal{S}(t)\mathcal{M}_{b}]\big|_{\mathrm{op}}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\int^{T}_{0}\int_{[m,\infty)}e^{-\theta t}|M_{b}(\theta)|_{\mathrm{op}}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\int_{[m,\infty)}\theta^{-1}|M_{b}(\theta)|_{\mathrm{op}}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int_{[m,\infty)}\theta^{-1/2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)\right)^{1/2}\left(\int_{[m,\infty)}\theta^{-3/2}|M_{b}(\theta)|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)\right)^{1/2},

where we used Tonelli’s theorem and the elementary inequality
\int^{T}_{0}e^{-\theta t}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq\theta^{-1}
in the second inequality, and the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality in the third inequality. Recalling the integrability conditions (
2.6
) and (
2.7
) on
\mu
and
M_{b}
, the dominated convergence theorem ensures that the last term in the above estimate converges to zero as
m\to\infty
. Hence, we obtain (
2.28
). It remains to prove (
2.29
). By Itô’s isometry and Young’s convolution inequality, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T}_{0}\left|\int^{t}_{0}\overline{\mu}_{m}[\mathcal{S}(t-s)\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}]\sigma(\mu[Y_{s}])\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T}_{0}\int^{t}_{0}\big|\overline{\mu}_{m}[\mathcal{S}(t-s)\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}]\sigma(\mu[Y_{s}])\big|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}t\right]

\displaystyle\leq\int^{T}_{0}\big|\overline{\mu}_{m}[\mathcal{S}(t)\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}]\big|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\,\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T}_{0}\big|\sigma(\mu[Y_{t}])\big|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\right]

for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
. Since
\sigma:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
exhibits linear growth and
\mu[\cdot]\in L(\mathcal{V};\mathbb{R}^{n})
, it follows from (
2.22
) that
\mathbb{E}[\int^{T}_{0}|\sigma(\mu[Y_{t}])|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t]<\infty
. Together with the facts that
\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}\in L(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathcal{H})
and
\mathcal{S}(\cdot)\in L(\mathcal{H};L^{2}(0,T;\mathcal{V}))
, along with the convergence given in (
2.24
), this implies that (
2.29
) holds. Consequently, by taking the limit
m\to\infty
in (
2.25
), we conclude that assertion (iii) is satisfied. This completes the proof.
∎

Define

\Lambda:=\left\{\eta:[0,\infty)\to\mathcal{H}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\text{$t\mapsto\eta_{t}$ is strongly continuous in $\mathcal{H}$ and}\ \int^{T}_{0}\|\eta_{t}\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty\ \text{for any $T>0$}\right\}

and

d_{\Lambda}(\eta,\eta^{\prime}):=\sum_{T\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{1}{2^{T}}\big\{\|\eta-\eta^{\prime}\|_{\Lambda_{T}}\wedge 1\big\}\ \ \text{for $\eta,\eta^{\prime}\in\Lambda$},

(2.30)

where

\|\eta\|_{\Lambda_{T}}:=\left(\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\eta_{t}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+\int^{T}_{0}\|\eta_{t}\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{1/2}\ \ \text{for $\eta\in\Lambda$}.

(2.31)

Then,
(\Lambda,d_{\Lambda})
is a complete separable metric space; see
A.3
. A sequence
\{\eta^{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
in
\Lambda
converges to an element
\eta\in\Lambda
with respect to the metric
d_{\Lambda}
if and only if
\lim_{k\to\infty}\|\eta_{k}-\eta\|_{\Lambda_{T}}=0
for any
T>0
. By virtue of the final assertion in
A.3
(see also
A.4
(ii)), for each solution
Y
to the SEE (
1.2
) on a complete probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
, the sample-path map
\Omega\ni\omega\mapsto(Y_{t}(\omega))_{t\geq 0}
defines a
\Lambda
-valued Borel measurable random variable. Under the setting in
2.15
, the law of the path of a solution
Y
on
\Lambda
is determined by the distribution of the initial condition
Y_{0}
on
\mathcal{H}
. This follows from the uniqueness in law for the SEE (
1.2
), which is ensured by the pathwise uniqueness and a Yamada–Watanabe-type result for general SEEs (see, e.g.,
[
47
, Appendix E]
). When
Y_{0}
is distributed according to a prescribed initial distribution
\nu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})
, we denote the law of the solution
Y
on the path space
\Lambda
by
\mathbf{P}^{\nu}\in\mathcal{P}(\Lambda)
. The Markov semigroup
\{P_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
on
\mathcal{H}
associated with the SEE (
1.2
) is then given by

P_{t}(y,A):=\mathbf{P}^{\delta_{y}}\big(\left\{\eta\in\Lambda\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\eta_{t}\in A\right\}\big),\ \ y\in\mathcal{H},\ A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}),\ t\geq 0.

The estimate (
2.16
) in particular implies that the Markov semigroup
\{P_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
on
\mathcal{H}
satisfies the Feller property. Furthermore, the Markov semigroup
\{P_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
is clearly stochastically continuous, and hence it is measurable.

Remark 2.16
.

(i)

More generally than the Feller property of
\{P_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
, the estimate (
2.16
) implies that the map
\nu\mapsto\mathbf{P}^{\nu}
is continuous from
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})
to
\mathcal{P}(\Lambda)
, both of which are equipped with their respective topology of weak convergence of probability measures. For each
\tau\in[0,\infty)
, denote by
\mathscr{S}_{\tau}:\Lambda\to\Lambda
the time-shift operator on
\Lambda
, which is defined by
\mathscr{S}_{\tau}\eta:=(\eta_{t+\tau})_{t\geq 0}
for each
\eta\in\Lambda
. The time-homogeneous Markov property implies that
\mathbf{P}^{\nu}\circ\mathscr{S}_{\tau}^{-1}=\mathbf{P}^{P_{\tau}^{*}\nu}
.

(ii)

Consider the SVE (
1.1
) with the liftable pair of kernels
(K_{b},K_{\sigma})
generated by a lifting basis
(\mu,M_{b},M_{\sigma})
. Given a free term
x
of the form
x(t)=\mu[\mathcal{S}(t)Y_{0}]
for some
\mathcal{H}
-valued random variable
Y_{0}
distributed according to a prescribed probability measure
\nu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})
, we denote the law of the
\mathrm{d}t
-equivalence class of the solution
X=(X_{t})_{t>0}
on
L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(0,\infty;\mathbb{R}^{n})
by
\mathbf{Q}^{\nu}\in\mathcal{P}(L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(0,\infty;\mathbb{R}^{n}))
. By
2.15
(iii), we have
\mathbf{Q}^{\nu}=\mathbf{P}^{\nu}\circ\mu[\cdot]^{-1}
. Here, with a slight abuse of notation,
\mu[\cdot]
is understood as a map from
\Lambda
to
L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(0,\infty;\mathbb{R}^{n})
, which maps each
\eta\in\Lambda
to the
\mathrm{d}t
-equivalence class of
(\mu[\eta_{t}])_{t\geq 0}
. Since
\mathcal{V}\ni y\mapsto\mu[y]\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a bounded linear operator, by the definition of
\Lambda
, the map
\mu[\cdot]:\Lambda\to L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(0,\infty;\mathbb{R}^{n})
is continuous. Consequently, the continuity of
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})\ni\nu\mapsto\mathbf{P}^{\nu}\in\mathcal{P}(\Lambda)
implies the continuity of
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})\ni\nu\mapsto\mathbf{Q}^{\nu}\in\mathcal{P}(L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(0,\infty;\mathbb{R}^{n}))
with respect to the topology of weak convergence of probability measures. With a slight abuse notation, we again denote the time-shift operator
\xi\mapsto\xi_{\cdot+\tau}
on
L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(0,\infty;\mathbb{R}^{n})
by
\mathscr{S}_{\tau}
for each
\tau\in[0,\infty)
. Then, we have
\mathbf{Q}^{\nu}\circ\mathscr{S}_{\tau}^{-1}=\mathbf{Q}^{P_{\tau}^{*}\nu}
.

To apply the generalized Harris’ theorem of
[
29
]
(see
2.3
), the construction of a suitable distance(-like) function is essential. To this end, a “change-of-norm” technique plays a crucial role in our framework. More precisely, we introduce the following definition.

Definition 2.17
.

Let
(\mu,M_{b},M_{\sigma})
be a lifting basis. We call a measurable map
\Phi:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
an
admissible weight function
if the matrix
\Phi(\theta)\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is symmetric, positive definite and satisfies

|\Phi(\theta)|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq C_{\Phi}(1+\theta)^{-1/2}\ \ \text{and}\ \ \big|\Phi(\theta)^{-1}\big|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq C_{\Phi}(1+\theta)^{1/2}

(2.32)

for
\mu
-a.e.
\theta\in[0,\infty)
for some constant
C_{\Phi}>0
. For each admissible weight function
\Phi
, define

\displaystyle\|y\|_{\Phi}:=\left(\int_{[0,\infty)}\langle\Phi(\theta)y(\theta),y(\theta)\rangle\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)\right)^{1/2}\ \ \text{for $y\in\mathcal{H}$},

\displaystyle\mathopen{|\mkern-1.5mu|\mkern-1.5mu|}y\mathclose{|\mkern-1.5mu|\mkern-1.5mu|}_{\Phi}:=\left(\int_{[0,\infty)}\theta\langle\Phi(\theta)y(\theta),y(\theta)\rangle\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)\right)^{1/2}\ \ \text{for $y\in\mathcal{V}$},

\displaystyle\mu_{b,\Phi}[y]:=\int_{[0,\infty)}M_{b}(\theta)^{\top}\Phi(\theta)y(\theta)\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)\ \ \text{for $y\in\mathcal{V}$},

\displaystyle\mu_{\sigma,\Phi}[y]:=\int_{[0,\infty)}M_{\sigma}(\theta)^{\top}\Phi(\theta)y(\theta)\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)\ \ \text{for $y\in\mathcal{H}$},\ \ \text{and}

\displaystyle Q_{\sigma,\Phi}:=\int_{[0,\infty)}M_{\sigma}(\theta)^{\top}\Phi(\theta)M_{\sigma}(\theta)\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta).

Furthermore, define a map
d_{\Phi}:\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{H}\to[0,1]
by

d_{\Phi}(y_{1},y_{2}):=\|y_{1}-y_{2}\|_{\Phi}\wedge 1\ \ \text{for $y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathcal{H}$.}

Remark 2.18
.

(i)

Notice that
\|y\|_{\mathcal{H}}=\|y\|_{\Phi_{0}}
and
\|y\|_{\mathcal{V}}=(\|y\|_{\Phi_{0}}^{2}+\mathopen{|\mkern-1.5mu|\mkern-1.5mu|}y\mathclose{|\mkern-1.5mu|\mkern-1.5mu|}_{\Phi_{0}}^{2})^{1/2}
for the admissible weight function
\Phi_{0}(\theta)=(1+\theta)^{-1/2}I_{n\times n}
.

(ii)

For every admissible weight function
\Phi
, the map
y\mapsto\|y\|_{\Phi}
defines an equivalent norm in
\mathcal{H}
. This norm is induced by the inner product

\langle y_{1},y_{2}\rangle_{\Phi}:=\int_{[0,\infty)}\langle\Phi(\theta)y_{1}(\theta),y_{2}(\theta)\rangle\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta),\ \ y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathcal{H}.

The map
d_{\Phi}:\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{H}\to[0,1]
is a distance function on
\mathcal{H}
which is compatible to the original norm-topology of
\mathcal{H}
. Similarly, the map
y\mapsto(\|y\|_{\Phi}^{2}+\mathopen{|\mkern-1.5mu|\mkern-1.5mu|}y\mathclose{|\mkern-1.5mu|\mkern-1.5mu|}_{\Phi}^{2})^{1/2}
defines an equivalent norm in
\mathcal{V}
. If we identify
\mathcal{H}^{*}
with
\mathcal{H}
by the Riesz isomorphism
y\mapsto\langle y,\cdot\rangle_{\Phi}
with respect to the inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\Phi}
, which may be different from the isomorphism
y\mapsto\langle y,\cdot\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
, then the corresponding duality pairing
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathcal{V}^{*},\mathcal{V},\Phi}
between
\mathcal{V}^{*}
and
\mathcal{V}
is given as the unique extension of the bounded bilinear functional
\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{V}\ni(y_{1},y_{2})\mapsto\langle y_{1},y_{2}\rangle_{\Phi}\in\mathbb{R}
to
\mathcal{V}^{*}\times\mathcal{V}
.

(iii)

For each admissible weight function
\Phi
, it is clear from the definition that

\langle\mathcal{A}y,y\rangle_{\mathcal{V}^{*},\mathcal{V},\Phi}=-\mathopen{|\mkern-1.5mu|\mkern-1.5mu|}y\mathclose{|\mkern-1.5mu|\mkern-1.5mu|}_{\Phi}^{2}

(2.33)

for any
y\in\mathcal{V}
. The integrability condition (
2.7
) on
M_{b}
and the bound (
2.32
) for
\Phi
ensure that the map
\mu_{b,\Phi}[\cdot]
is a bounded linear operator from
\mathcal{V}
to
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. This is the adjoint operator of
\mathcal{M}_{b}\in L(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathcal{V}^{*})
with respect to the duality pairing
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathcal{V}^{*},\mathcal{V},\Phi}
, that is,

\langle\mathcal{M}_{b}x,y\rangle_{\mathcal{V}^{*},\mathcal{V},\Phi}=\int_{[0,\infty)}\big\langle\Phi(\theta)M_{b}(\theta)x,y(\theta)\big\rangle\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)=\langle x,\mu_{b,\Phi}[y]\rangle

(2.34)

for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
y\in\mathcal{V}
. Similarly, the integrability condition (
2.8
) on
M_{\sigma}
and the bound (
2.32
) for
\Phi
ensure that the map
\mu_{\sigma,\Phi}[\cdot]
is a bounded linear operator from
\mathcal{H}
to
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. This is the adjoint operator of
\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}\in L(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathcal{H})
with respect to the inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\Phi}
, that is,

\langle\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}x,y\rangle_{\Phi}=\int_{[0,\infty)}\big\langle\Phi(\theta)M_{\sigma}(\theta)x,y(\theta)\big\rangle\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)=\langle x,\mu_{\sigma,\Phi}[y]\rangle

(2.35)

for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
y\in\mathcal{H}
. Furthermore, again by (
2.8
) and (
2.32
), the matrix
Q_{\sigma,\Phi}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is well-defined. This matrix is symmetric and satisfies

\big\|\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}x\big\|_{L_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};(\mathcal{H},\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\Phi}))}^{2}=\mathrm{tr}\big[x^{\top}Q_{\sigma,\Phi}x\big]

(2.36)

for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
. Here,
\|\cdot\|_{L_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};(\mathcal{H},\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\Phi}))}
denotes the Hilbert–Schmidt norm for an operator from the Euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{d}
to the separable Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
equipped with the inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\Phi}
.

Applying
[
47
, Theorem 4.2.5]
(with
\alpha=2
in their notation)
4
4

4

Although
[
47
, Theorem 4.2.5]
assumes some additional integrability conditions with respect to
\mathbb{P}
, such conditions can be dropped by a standard localization argument. See also
[
55
, Lemma 2.14]
for a similar result (with simpler proof than
[
47
, Theorem 4.2.5]
) without any integrability assumptions with respect to
\mathbb{P}
.
to our setting, we obtain the following Itô’s formula for the squared norm
\|\cdot\|_{\Phi}^{2}
.

Lemma 2.19
.

Suppose that we are given a
d
-dimensional Brownian motion
W
defined on a complete probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
, with respect to a filtration
\mathbb{F}=(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
satisfying the usual conditions. Let
(\mu,M_{b},M_{\sigma})
be a lifting basis. Let
Y
be an
\mathcal{H}
-valued continuous adapted process, and let
B
and
\Sigma
be
\mathcal{V}^{*}
and
L_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathcal{H})
-valued progressively measurable processes, respectively. Assume that
\int^{T}_{0}\{\|Y_{t}\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}+\|B_{t}\|_{\mathcal{V}^{*}}^{2}+\|\Sigma_{t}\|_{L_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathcal{H})}^{2}\}\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty
a.s. for any
T>0
, and that
Y
satisfies

Y_{t}=Y_{0}+\int^{t}_{0}B_{s}\,\mathrm{d}s+\int^{t}_{0}\Sigma_{s}\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}

in
\mathcal{V}^{*}
for all
t\geq 0
a.s. Then, for any admissible weight function
\Phi
, it holds that

\|Y_{t}\|_{\Phi}^{2}=\|Y_{0}\|_{\Phi}^{2}+\int^{t}_{0}\Big\{2\langle B_{s},Y_{s}\rangle_{\mathcal{V}^{*},\mathcal{V},\Phi}+\|\Sigma_{s}\|_{L_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};(\mathcal{H},\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\Phi}))}^{2}\Big\}\,\mathrm{d}s+2\int^{t}_{0}\langle Y_{s},\Sigma_{s}\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}\rangle_{\Phi}

(2.37)

for all
t\geq 0
a.s.

Remark 2.20
.

As discussed previously, the pairing
\langle B_{s},Y_{s}\rangle_{\mathcal{V}^{*},\mathcal{V},\Phi}
in (
2.37
) is understood in the sense of
\langle B_{s},\widetilde{Y}_{s}\rangle_{\mathcal{V}^{*},\mathcal{V},\Phi}
, where
\widetilde{Y}:=Y\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{\mathcal{V}}(Y)
is a
\mathcal{V}
-valued progressively measurable version of
Y
; see
2.14
. It should be emphasized that the process
Y
is generally not a semimartingale on the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
; indeed, it can only be viewed as a semimartingale when considered as a process in the larger space
\mathcal{V}^{*}
. Nevertheless, the above Itô’s formula remains valid for the squared (equivalent) norm
\|\cdot\|_{\Phi}^{2}
on
\mathcal{H}
, and the process
(\|Y_{t}\|_{\Phi}^{2})_{t\geq 0}
is a nonnegative semimartingale.

3
Main results

We now state our main results. The proofs of the key assertions (
3.2
,
3.4
and
3.6
) in this section are deferred to
Section

4
, while the primary theorem on ergodicity (
3.8
) follows as a straightforward consequence of these results by virtue of the generalized Harris’ theorem recalled in
2.3
.

For a given lifting basis
(\mu,M_{b},M_{\sigma})
and measurable maps
b:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\sigma:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
, we introduce the following assumption.

Assumption 1
.

•

\kappa:=\inf{\mathrm{supp}\,\mu}>0
.

•

For
\mu
-a.e.
\theta\in[0,\infty)
, the matrix
M_{\sigma}(\theta)\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is symmetric and positive definite.

•

There exist constants
C_{b,\mathrm{Lip}},C_{\sigma,\mathrm{Lip}}>0
such that
|b(x)-b(x^{\prime})|\leq C_{b,\mathrm{Lip}}|x-x^{\prime}|
and
|\sigma(x)-\sigma(x^{\prime})|\leq C_{\sigma,\mathrm{Lip}}|x-x^{\prime}|
for all
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

•

There exists a constant
C_{\mathrm{UE}}>0
such that
\langle\sigma(x)\sigma(x)^{\top}\xi,\xi\rangle\geq\frac{|\xi|^{2}}{C_{\mathrm{UE}}}
for all
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Remark 3.1
.

(i)

In
1
, we do not impose any restrictions on the values of the Lipschitz constants
C_{b,\mathrm{Lip}}
and
C_{\sigma,\mathrm{Lip}}
of
b:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\sigma:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
. This stands in contrast to
[
8
,
9
,
10
]
, where various results on long-time asymptotics (without a spectral gap) for SVEs and their Markovian lifts are established under the assumption that the Lipschitz and growth constants are sufficiently small to make the systems dissipative. Instead, we require the uniform ellipticity of the diffusion coefficient
\sigma
. It is worth noting that this does not imply that the diffusion of the SEE (
1.2
) is uniformly elliptic on the entire state space
\mathcal{H}
. On the contrary, the system is
highly degenerate
, as only finite-dimensional noise
W
acts on the infinite-dimensional state space. Notably, the noise dimension
d
can be as small as one, provided that the dimension
n
of the original SVE (
1.1
) is one.

(ii)

For the lifting basis in
2.9
(i) generating the sum-of-exponentials type kernels
(K^{\exp}_{b},K^{\exp}_{\sigma})
, the first and second conditions in
1
are satisfied provided that
\kappa_{i}>0
and
M_{\sigma,i}
is symmetric and positive definite for each
i\in\{1,\dots,N\}
. In this case, we have
\kappa:=\inf{\mathrm{supp}\,\mu}=\min\{\kappa_{1},\dots,\kappa_{N}\}
. As for the lifting basis in
2.9
(ii) generating the tempered fractional kernels
(K^{\mathrm{frac}}_{b},K^{\mathrm{frac}}_{\sigma})
, the first and second conditions in
1
are satisfied provided that
0<\kappa_{\sigma}\leq\kappa_{b}
. In this case, we have
\kappa:=\inf{\mathrm{supp}\,\mu}=\kappa_{\sigma}
. It should be noted that the requirement
\kappa:=\inf{\mathrm{supp}\,\mu}>0
necessitates exponential tempering of the kernels.

The following theorem constitutes the key ingredient in applying the generalized Harris’ theorem of
[
29
]
(see also
2.3
). Furthermore, we establish an
asymptotic log-Harnack inequality
which is of independent interest. In particular, it guarantees the uniqueness of the invariant probability measure (see, e.g.,
[
4
, Theorem 2.1]
). In what follows, we use the notation introduced in
2.17
and (
2.3
).

Theorem 3.2
.

Suppose that a lifting basis
(\mu,M_{b},M_{\sigma})
and measurable maps
b:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
\sigma:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
satisfy
1
. Let
\{P_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
be the Markov semigroup associated with the SEE (
1.2
). Then, there exists an admissible weight function
\Phi:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
, depending only on
(\mu,M_{b},M_{\sigma})
and the constants
C_{b,\mathrm{Lip}},C_{\sigma,\mathrm{Lip}}
, and
C_{\mathrm{UE}}
, such that the following assertions hold:

(i)

For any
t\geq\frac{4\log 2}{\kappa}
, the distance function
d_{\Phi}
is contracting for
P_{t}
. Specifically, for any
y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathcal{H}
with
d_{\Phi}(y_{1},y_{2})<1
, we have

\mathbb{W}_{d_{\Phi}}(P_{t}(y_{1},\cdot),P_{t}(y_{2},\cdot))\leq\frac{3}{4}d_{\Phi}(y_{1},y_{2}).

(3.1)

(ii)

For any
R>0
and any
t\geq\frac{4R^{2}+2\log(8R)}{\kappa}
, the set
\overline{B}_{\Phi}(R):=\{y\in\mathcal{H}\,|\,\|y\|_{\Phi}\leq R\}
is
d_{\Phi}
-small for
P_{t}
. Specifically, for any
y_{1},y_{2}\in\overline{B}_{\Phi}(R)
, we have

\mathbb{W}_{d_{\Phi}}(P_{t}(y_{1},\cdot),P_{t}(y_{2},\cdot))\leq 1-\frac{1}{4}\exp(-2R^{2}).

(3.2)

(iii)

The following asymptotic log-Harnack inequality holds: for any
y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathcal{H}
, any
t\geq 0
and any bounded Borel measurable function
f:\mathcal{H}\to[1,\infty)
such that

\|\nabla_{\Phi}\log f\|_{\infty}:=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}y,y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}\\
y\neq y^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\frac{|\log f(y)-\log f(y^{\prime})|}{\|y-y^{\prime}\|_{\Phi}}<\infty,

it holds that

P_{t}\log f(y_{1})\leq\log P_{t}f(y_{2})+\frac{1}{2}\|y_{1}-y_{2}\|_{\Phi}^{2}+e^{-\kappa t/2}\|y_{1}-y_{2}\|_{\Phi}\|\nabla_{\Phi}\log f\|_{\infty}.

(3.3)

In particular, the Markov semigroup
\{P_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
possesses at most one invariant probability measure.

The proof of the above theorem is provided in
Section

4.1
.

Remark 3.3
.

In addition to the uniqueness of the invariant probability measure, the asymptotic log-Harnack inequality in assertion (iii) implies that the Markov semigroup
\{P_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
is
asymptotically strong Feller
and
asymptotically irreducible
. Furthermore, it provides a
gradient estimate
and an
asymptotic heat kernel estimate
; for further details, see
[
4
, Theorem 2.1]
. In our previous work
[
32
, Section 4]
, the asymptotic log-Harnack inequality was established for the Markovian lift of an SVE with a scalar completely monotone kernel, corresponding to the case
M_{b}=M_{\sigma}=I_{n\times n}
. The present assertion (iii) significantly extends this result to the case of matrix-valued kernels where
K_{b}
and
K_{\sigma}
may differ, allowing for cases where
K_{b}
is not necessarily square-integrable.

The construction of a Lyapunov function constitutes another important step in applying the generalized Harris’ theorem of
[
29
]
(see also
2.3
). The following assumption provides an abstract criterion for the existence of a Lyapunov function. For its statement, we again refer to the notation introduced in
2.17
.

Assumption 2
.

There exist an admissible weight function
\Psi:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
and constants
\delta\in(0,1)
,
\rho>0
and
C_{\mathrm{Lyap}}>0
such that

\big\langle b(\mu[y]),\mu_{b,\Psi}[y]\big\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}\big[\sigma(\mu[y])^{\top}Q_{\sigma,\Psi}\sigma(\mu[y])\big]\leq\delta\mathopen{|\mkern-1.5mu|\mkern-1.5mu|}y\mathclose{|\mkern-1.5mu|\mkern-1.5mu|}_{\Psi}^{2}-\rho\|y\|_{\Psi}^{2}+C_{\mathrm{Lyap}}

(3.4)

for any
y\in\mathcal{V}
.

Theorem 3.4
.

Suppose that a lifting basis
(\mu,M_{b},M_{\sigma})
and measurable maps
b:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
\sigma:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
satisfy
2
. Assume that the SEE (
1.2
) is weakly well-posed, and denote by
\{P_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
the associated measurable Markov semigroup on
\mathcal{H}
. Then, the function
V(y):=\|y\|_{\Psi}^{2}
is a Lyapunov function for
\{P_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
. Furthermore, if
\pi\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})
is an invariant probability measure for
\{P_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
, then the following estimate holds:

\int_{\mathcal{H}}\Big\{\rho\|y\|_{\Psi}^{2}+(1-\delta)\mathopen{|\mkern-1.5mu|\mkern-1.5mu|}y\mathclose{|\mkern-1.5mu|\mkern-1.5mu|}_{\Psi}^{2}\Big\}\,\pi(\mathrm{d}y)\leq C_{\mathrm{Lyap}}.

(3.5)

In particular, we have
\pi(\mathcal{V})=1
and
\int_{\mathcal{V}}\|y\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\,\pi(\mathrm{d}y)<\infty
.

The proof of the above theorem is provided in
Section

4.2
.

Remark 3.5
.

The condition in
2
is related to the Lyapunov-type condition in
[
21
, Chapter 7]
, where the ultimate boundedness for a general class of monotone SPDEs on an abstract Gelfand triplet
\mathcal{V}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{H}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{V}^{*}
is investigated. In that work, it is shown that the ultimate boundedness condition implies the existence of an invariant probability measure, provided that the embedding
\mathcal{V}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{H}
is compact. However, this classical result cannot be directly applied to our framework, since the embedding
\mathcal{V}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{H}
is not compact in typical cases of interest; see
A.1
for a characterization of the compactness of this embedding in our framework. This lack of compactness poses a technical challenge beyond the standard result on the general framework, as
2
alone is not sufficient to conclude the existence of an invariant probability measure; see
4.4
for more detailed discussions on this difficulty. To ensure the existence (as well as the uniqueness and the exponential ergodicity) of the invariant probability measure, it is essential to combine the Lyapunov approach in
3.4
with the contractivity and
d_{\Phi}
-smallness conditions established in
3.2
(i) and (ii) for a specific distance
d_{\Phi}
, as will be demonstrated in
3.8
below.

It is straightforward to show that
2
holds if
\kappa:=\inf{\mathrm{supp}\,\mu}>0
and the maps
b:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\sigma:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
exhibit sub-linear growth; in this case, the required condition is satisfied for
any
admissible weight function
\Psi
. The following proposition, the proof of which is also deferred to
Section

4.2
, provides another verifiable yet non-trivial sufficient condition for
2
. In this context, the flexibility in the choice of the weight function
\Psi
turns out to be crucial.

Proposition 3.6
.

For a lifting basis
(\mu,M_{b},M_{\sigma})
and measurable maps
b:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\sigma:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
,
2
is satisfied if the following conditions hold:

•

\kappa:=\inf{\mathrm{supp}\,\mu}>0
.

•

For
\mu
-a.e.
\theta\in[0,\infty)
, the matrix
M_{b}(\theta)\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is symmetric and nonnegative definite.

•

There exist constants
C_{b,\mathrm{LG}},C_{b,\mathrm{LG}}^{\prime}>0
and a constant
\gamma>0
satisfying
\gamma\int_{[\kappa,\infty)}\theta^{-1}|M_{b}(\theta)|_{\mathrm{op}}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)<1
such that, for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

|b(x)|\leq C_{b,\mathrm{LG}}(1+|x|)\ \ \text{and}\ \ \langle b(x),x\rangle\leq\gamma|x|^{2}+C_{b,\mathrm{LG}}^{\prime}.

•

There exist constants
p\in(0,1)
and
C_{\sigma,\mathrm{subLG}}>0
such that
|\sigma(x)|\leq C_{\sigma,\mathrm{subLG}}(1+|x|^{p})
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Remark 3.7
.

(i)

Within the aforementioned conditions, we do not impose any restriction on the linear growth constant of the drift coefficient
b
, other than the coercivity condition
\langle b(x),x\rangle\leq\gamma|x|^{2}+C_{b,\mathrm{LG}}^{\prime}
with the specific positive constant
\gamma
introduced above. This kind of coercivity condition often arises in Lyapunov-type estimates for SDEs. It is worth noting, however, that controlling the system under such an instantaneous condition is far from trivial in the context of the SVE (
1.1
), as the dynamics exhibit path-dependence due to the presence of the kernels. Similarly, it is also non-trivial from the viewpoint of the SEE (
1.2
), as its coefficients involve the non-local term
\mu[y]
with low regularity. Nevertheless,
3.4
and
3.6
establish suitable Lyapunov-type estimates. A key ingredient in these estimates is the construction of an admissible weight function
\Psi
that exploits the specific structure of the SEE (
1.2
) to satisfy the requirements in
2
. For more details, see
Section

4.2
.

(ii)

By (
2.6
), (
2.7
), and the assumption
\kappa:={\mathrm{supp}\,\mu}>0
, the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality yields

\int_{[\kappa,\infty)}\theta^{-1}|M_{b}(\theta)|_{\mathrm{op}}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)\leq\left(\int_{[\kappa,\infty)}\theta^{-1/2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)\right)^{1/2}\left(\int_{[\kappa,\infty)}\theta^{-3/2}|M_{b}(\theta)|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)\right)^{1/2}<\infty.

If
(K_{b},K_{\sigma})
is generated by the lifting basis
(\mu,M_{b},M_{\sigma})
, the quantity
\int_{[\kappa,\infty)}\theta^{-1}|M_{b}(\theta)|_{\mathrm{op}}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)
is closely related to the integral
\int^{\infty}_{0}|K_{b}(t)|_{\mathrm{op}}\,\mathrm{d}t
. Indeed, the representation
K_{b}(t)=\int_{[\kappa,\infty)}e^{-\theta t}M_{b}(\theta)\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)
and Tonelli’s theorem yield

\int^{\infty}_{0}|K_{b}(t)|_{\mathrm{op}}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq\int^{\infty}_{0}\int_{[\kappa,\infty)}e^{-\theta t}|M_{b}(\theta)|_{\mathrm{op}}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{[\kappa,\infty)}\theta^{-1}|M_{b}(\theta)|_{\mathrm{op}}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)<\infty.

Furthermore, by Fubini’s theorem, we have

\int^{\infty}_{0}K_{b}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t=\int^{\infty}_{0}\int_{[\kappa,\infty)}e^{-\theta t}M_{b}(\theta)\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{[\kappa,\infty)}\theta^{-1}M_{b}(\theta)\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta).

Thus, if there exists a vector
v\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{0\}
(independent of
\theta
) such that
M_{b}(\theta)v=|M_{b}(\theta)|_{\mathrm{op}}v
for
\mu
-a.e.
\theta\in[0,\infty)
, then the following identities hold:

\left|\int^{\infty}_{0}K_{b}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\right|_{\mathrm{op}}=\int^{\infty}_{0}|K_{b}(t)|_{\mathrm{op}}\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{[\kappa,\infty)}\theta^{-1}|M_{b}(\theta)|_{\mathrm{op}}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)=\left|\int_{[\kappa,\infty)}\theta^{-1}M_{b}(\theta)\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)\right|_{\mathrm{op}}.

(iii)

For the lifting basis in
2.9
(i) generating the sum-of-exponentials type kernels
(K^{\exp}_{b},K^{\exp}_{\sigma})
, the first and second conditions in
3.6
are satisfied provided that
\kappa_{i}>0
and
M_{b,i}
is symmetric and nonnegative definite for each
i\in\{1,\dots,N\}
. In this case, we have
\kappa:=\inf{\mathrm{supp}\,\mu}=\min\{\kappa_{1},\dots,\kappa_{N}\}
and
\int_{[\kappa,\infty)}\theta^{-1}|M_{b}(\theta)|_{\mathrm{op}}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)=\sum^{N}_{i=1}\frac{|M_{b,i}|_{\mathrm{op}}}{\kappa_{i}}
. As for the lifting basis in
2.9
(ii) generating the tempered fractional kernels
(K^{\mathrm{frac}}_{b},K^{\mathrm{frac}}_{\sigma})
, the first and second conditions in
3.6
are satisfied provided that
\kappa_{b},\kappa_{\sigma}>0
. In this case, we have
\kappa:=\inf{\mathrm{supp}\,\mu}=\kappa_{b}\wedge\kappa_{\sigma}
and
\int_{[\kappa,\infty)}\theta^{-1}|M_{b}(\theta)|_{\mathrm{op}}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)=\int^{\infty}_{0}|K_{b}(t)|_{\mathrm{op}}\,\mathrm{d}t=\kappa_{b}^{-\alpha_{b}}
. These observations are comparable with
3.1
(ii).

As a direct consequence of
3.2
and
3.4
, and by virtue of the generalized Harris’ theorem of
[
29
]
(see also
2.3
), we obtain the following main result regarding the exponential ergodicity of the Markovian lift.

Theorem 3.8
.

Suppose that a lifting basis
(\mu,M_{b},M_{\sigma})
and measurable maps
b:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
\sigma:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
satisfy both
1
and
2
. Then, for the Markov semigroup
\{P_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
associated with the SEE (
1.2
), the following assertions hold:

(i)

There exist constants
r,t_{0}>0
such that the following spectral gap-type estimate holds:

\mathbb{W}_{d_{\Phi,\Psi}}(P_{t}^{*}\nu_{1},P_{t}^{*}\nu_{2})\leq e^{-rt}\mathbb{W}_{d_{\Phi,\Psi}}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})

(3.6)

for all
t\geq t_{0}
and all
\nu_{1},\nu_{2}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})
, where
d_{\Phi,\Psi}:\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{H}\to[0,\infty)
is a distance-like function given by

d_{\Phi,\Psi}(y_{1},y_{2}):=\sqrt{\big(\|y_{1}-y_{2}\|_{\Phi}\wedge 1\big)\big(1+\|y_{1}\|_{\Psi}^{2}+\|y_{2}\|_{\Psi}^{2}\big)},\ \ y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathcal{H},

(3.7)

and
\Phi
and
\Psi
are the admissible weight functions provided in
3.2
and
2
, respectively.

(ii)

There exists a unique invariant probability measure
\pi\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})
for
\{P_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
, which satisfies

\pi(\mathcal{V})=1\ \ \text{and}\ \ \int_{\mathcal{V}}\|y\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\,\pi(\mathrm{d}y)<\infty.

(3.8)

Furthermore, there exists a constant
C>0
such that the following exponential ergodicity holds:

\mathbb{W}_{d_{\Phi,\Psi}}(P_{t}(y,\cdot),\pi)\leq C(1+\|y\|_{\Psi})e^{-rt}

(3.9)

for all
y\in\mathcal{H}
and all
t\geq 0
.

Remark 3.9
.

(i)

The estimate (
3.9
) immediately yields that
P_{t}(y,\cdot)
converges to the invariant probability measure
\pi
weakly in
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})
as
t\to\infty
for any
y\in\mathcal{H}
. Furthermore, note that there exists a constant
C>0
such that

\|y_{1}-y_{2}\|_{\mathcal{H}}\leq\sqrt{\big(\|y_{1}-y_{2}\|_{\mathcal{H}}\wedge 1\big)\big(1+2\|y_{1}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+2\|y_{2}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\big)}\leq Cd_{\Phi,\Psi}(y_{1},y_{2})

(3.10)

for all
y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathcal{H}
. Consequently, (
3.9
) yields the following exponential ergodicity in the
L^{1}
-Wasserstein distance:

\mathbb{W}_{\mathcal{H}}(P_{t}(y,\cdot),\pi)\leq C(1+\|y\|_{\mathcal{H}})e^{-rt}

(3.11)

for all
y\in\mathcal{H}
and all
t\geq 0
for some constant
C>0
, where
\mathbb{W}_{\mathcal{H}}:\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})\times\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})\to[0,\infty]
is the
L^{1}
-Wasserstein (extended) metric
5
5

5

The extended metric
\mathbb{W}_{\mathcal{H}}:\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})\times\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})\to[0,\infty]
is a metric on
\{\nu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})\,|\,\int_{\mathcal{H}}\|y\|_{\mathcal{H}}\,\nu(\mathrm{d}y)<\infty\}
, while it can take an infinite value outside this set.
defined by

\mathbb{W}_{\mathcal{H}}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2}):=\inf_{\nu\in\mathscr{C}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}\int_{\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{H}}\|y_{1}-y_{2}\|_{\mathcal{H}}\,\nu(\mathrm{d}y_{1},\mathrm{d}y_{2}),\ \ \nu_{1},\nu_{2}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}).

(3.12)

(ii)

The exponential weak ergodicity (
3.9
) (or (
3.11
)) has various applications to limit theorems, including the
law of large numbers
, the
central limit theorem
, the
averaging principle
and the
diffusion approximation
; see
[
42
, Chapters 5 and 6]
. We will study these topics in more detail in future research. On the other hand, the “spectral gap” result (
3.6
) by itself plays a crucial role in showing the stability of invariant probability measures; see
[
29
, Section 4.1]
. In
Section

5
, we exploit this spectral gap result to analyze a finite-dimensional approximation of the invariant probability measure
\pi\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})
.

Following the notation and observations in
2.16
, we obtain the following corollary as a direct consequence of
3.8
.

Corollary 3.10
.

Under the setting of
3.8
, the following assertions hold:

(i)

The probability measure
\mathbf{P}^{\pi}\in\mathcal{P}(\Lambda)
is invariant under the time-shifts on
\Lambda
, in the sense that
\mathbf{P}^{\pi}\circ\mathscr{S}_{\tau}^{-1}=\mathbf{P}^{\pi}
in
\mathcal{P}(\Lambda)
for all
\tau\geq 0
. Furthermore, for every
y\in\mathcal{H}
, the convergence
\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\mathbf{P}^{\delta_{y}}\circ\mathscr{S}_{\tau}^{-1}=\mathbf{P}^{\pi}
holds weakly in
\mathcal{P}(\Lambda)
.

(ii)

The probability measure
\mathbf{Q}^{\pi}\in\mathcal{P}(L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(0,\infty;\mathbb{R}^{n}))
is invariant under the time-shifts on
L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(0,\infty;\mathbb{R}^{n})
, in the sense that
\mathbf{Q}^{\pi}\circ\mathscr{S}_{\tau}^{-1}=\mathbf{Q}^{\pi}
in
\mathcal{P}(L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(0,\infty;\mathbb{R}^{n}))
for all
\tau\geq 0
. Furthermore, for every
y\in\mathcal{H}
, the convergence
\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\mathbf{Q}^{\delta_{y}}\circ\mathscr{S}_{\tau}^{-1}=\mathbf{Q}^{\pi}
holds weakly in
\mathcal{P}(L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(0,\infty;\mathbb{R}^{n}))
.

Proof.

Since
\pi\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})
is invariant with respect to
\{P_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
and
\lim_{t\to\infty}P_{t}(y,\cdot)=\pi
weakly in
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})
for any
y\in\mathcal{H}
, the assertions follow from the identities
\mathbf{P}^{\nu}\circ\mathscr{S}_{\tau}^{-1}=\mathbf{P}^{P_{\tau}^{*}\nu}
in
\mathcal{P}(\Lambda)
and
\mathbf{Q}^{\nu}\circ\mathscr{S}_{\tau}^{-1}=\mathbf{Q}^{P_{\tau}^{*}\nu}
in
\mathcal{P}(L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(0,\infty;\mathbb{R}^{n}))
for
\tau\geq 0
and
\nu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})
, combined with the continuity of
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})\ni\nu\mapsto\mathbf{P}^{\nu}\in\mathcal{P}(\Lambda)
and
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})\ni\nu\mapsto\mathbf{Q}^{\nu}\in\mathcal{P}(L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(0,\infty;\mathbb{R}^{n}))
in the weak sense (see
2.16
).
∎

Remark 3.11
.

Strictly speaking, the invariance of
\mathbf{Q}^{\pi}\in\mathcal{P}(L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(0,\infty;\mathbb{R}^{n}))
under the time-shifts on
L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(0,\infty;\mathbb{R}^{n})
established in
3.10
(ii) does not directly imply the stationarity of the finite-dimensional distributions of the process itself. Nevertheless, we can construct a stationary solution of the SVE (
1.1
) in the latter sense. Indeed, under the setting of
3.8
, the invariance of
\pi\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})
with respect to
\{P_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
and
\pi(\mathcal{V})=1
ensure that

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}^{\pi}\big(\left\{\eta\in\Lambda\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\eta_{t_{1}},\dots,\eta_{t_{\ell}}\in\mathcal{V}\right\}\big)=\int_{\mathcal{V}}\pi(\mathrm{d}y_{0})\int_{\mathcal{V}}P_{t_{1}}(y_{0},\mathrm{d}y_{1})\int_{\mathcal{V}}P_{t_{2}-t_{1}}(y_{1},\mathrm{d}y_{2})\cdots\int_{\mathcal{V}}P_{t_{\ell}-t_{\ell-1}}(y_{\ell-1},\mathrm{d}y_{\ell})=1

for any
t_{1},\dots,t_{\ell}\in[0,\infty)
.
6
6

6

Thanks to
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{V})=\{A\cap\mathcal{V}\,|\,A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})\}\subset\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
and
A.3
, we have
\{\eta\in\Lambda\,|\,\eta_{t_{1}},\dots,\eta_{t_{\ell}}\in\mathcal{V}\}\in\mathcal{B}(\Lambda)
, or more generally
\{\eta\in\Lambda\,|\,(\eta_{t_{1}},\dots,\eta_{t_{\ell}})\in A\}\in\mathcal{B}(\Lambda)
for any
A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{V}^{\ell})=\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{V})^{\otimes\ell}
. Note, however, that the set
\{\eta\in\Lambda\,|\,\eta_{t}\in\mathcal{V}\ \text{for any $t\geq 0$}\}
may not be a Borel set in
\Lambda
, as
t\mapsto\eta_{t}
is not necessarily continuous in
\mathcal{V}
for each
\eta\in\Lambda
.
Based on this observation, together with
\mu[\cdot]\in L(\mathcal{V};\mathbb{R}^{n})
, we can define a consistent family of finite-dimensional distributions
\widetilde{\mathbf{Q}}^{\pi}_{(t_{1},\dots,t_{\ell})}\in\mathcal{P}((\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\ell})
by

\widetilde{\mathbf{Q}}_{(t_{1},\dots,t_{\ell})}^{\pi}(A_{1}\times\cdots\times A_{\ell}):=\mathbf{P}^{\pi}\big(\left\{\eta\in\Lambda\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\eta_{t_{1}},\dots,\eta_{t_{\ell}}\in\mathcal{V}\ \text{and}\ \mu[\eta_{t_{1}}]\in A_{1},\dots,\mu[\eta_{t_{\ell}}]\in A_{\ell}\right\}\big)

for any
A_{1},\dots,A_{\ell}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
t_{1},\dots,t_{\ell}\in[0,\infty)
and
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{[0,\infty)}
be the space of all maps
\xi:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
, which is equipped with the
\sigma
-algebra generated by the family of cylinder sets
\{\{\xi\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{[0,\infty)}\,|\xi_{t}\in A\}\,|\,t\in[0,\infty),\,A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\}
. By the Kolmogorov extension theorem, there exists a unique probability measure
\widetilde{\mathbf{Q}}^{\pi}
on the measurable space
(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{[0,\infty)}
such that

\widetilde{\mathbf{Q}}^{\pi}\Big(\left\{\xi\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{[0,\infty)}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\big(\xi_{t_{1}},\dots,\xi_{t_{\ell}}\big)\in\cdot\right\}\Big)=\widetilde{\mathbf{Q}}^{\pi}_{(t_{1},\dots,t_{\ell})}\ \ \text{in $\mathcal{P}((\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\ell})$}

for any
t_{1},\dots,t_{\ell}\in[0,\infty)
and
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
. The probability measure
\widetilde{\mathbf{Q}}^{\pi}\in\mathcal{P}((\mathbb{R}^{n})^{[0,\infty)})
represents the law of the process
X:=\mu[Y]=(\mu[Y_{t}])_{t\geq 0}
itself (rather than its
\mathrm{d}t
-equivalence class as
\mathbf{Q}^{\pi}\in\mathcal{P}(L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(0,\infty;\mathbb{R}^{n}))
), where
Y
is a solution of the SEE (
1.2
) with the initial distribution
\pi\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})
. By
2.15
(iii),
X
solves the SVE (
1.1
). Furthermore,
3.10
(i) implies that the probability measure
\widetilde{\mathbf{Q}}^{\pi}\in\mathcal{P}((\mathbb{R}^{n})^{[0,\infty)})
is invariant under the time-shifts on
(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{[0,\infty)}
, in the sense that
\widetilde{\mathbf{Q}}^{\pi}\circ\mathscr{S}_{\tau}^{-1}=\widetilde{\mathbf{Q}}^{\pi}
in
\mathcal{P}((\mathbb{R}^{n})^{[0,\infty)})
for all
\tau\geq 0
. Here, with a slight abuse of notation, as in
2.16
, we again denote by
\mathscr{S}_{\tau}
the time-shift operator
(\xi_{t})_{t\geq 0}\mapsto(\xi_{t+\tau})_{t\geq 0}
on
(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{[0,\infty)}
. This shift-invariance of
\widetilde{\mathbf{Q}}^{\pi}
precisely means that the
\mathbb{R}^{n}
-valued process
X=\mu[Y]
is strictly stationary. We emphasize that
\mathbf{Q}^{\pi}\in\mathcal{P}(L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(0,\infty;\mathbb{R}^{n}))
and
\widetilde{\mathbf{Q}}^{\pi}\in\mathcal{P}((\mathbb{R}^{n})^{[0,\infty)})
are different objects; the former characterizes the law of the
\mathrm{d}t
-equivalence class of the process
X=\mu[Y]
, and the latter characterizes the finite-dimensional distributions of the process
X=\mu[Y]
itself (which can be interpreted as a “good representative” among the
\mathrm{d}t
-equivalence class). In contrast to
3.10
(ii), our current result does
not
imply the weak convergence of shifted finite-dimensional distributions, due to the general lack of continuity of the map
\Lambda\ni\eta\mapsto(\mu[\eta_{t_{1}}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{\mathcal{V}}(\eta_{t_{1}})],\dots,\mu[\eta_{t_{\ell}}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{\mathcal{V}}(\eta_{t_{\ell}})])\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\ell}
for fixed
t_{1},\dots,t_{\ell}\in[0,\infty)
.

Let us further examine the stationary distributions
\mathbf{Q}^{\pi}\in\mathcal{P}(L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(0,\infty;\mathbb{R}^{n}))
and
\widetilde{\mathbf{Q}}^{\pi}\in\mathcal{P}((\mathbb{R}^{n})^{[0,\infty)})
corresponding to the SVE (
1.1
). Admittedly, the state space
\mathcal{H}
, the Markov semigroup
\{P_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
on
\mathcal{H}
, and the invariant probability measure
\pi\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})
associated with the SEE (
1.2
) all depend on the specific choice of the lifting basis
(\mu,M_{b},M_{\sigma})
which generates the kernels
K_{b}
and
K_{\sigma}
. Nevertheless, it is natural to expect that the resulting stationary distributions for the original SVE (
1.1
) depend only on the kernels
K_{b},K_{\sigma}
and the coefficients
b,\sigma
, remaining independent of the particular choice of the lifting basis. The following theorem shows that this is indeed the case in some sense.

Theorem 3.12
.

Let
(K_{b},K_{\sigma})
be a liftable pair of kernels, and let
b:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\sigma:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
be measurable maps. Suppose that
(\mu_{1},M_{b,1},M_{\sigma,1})
and
(\mu_{2},M_{b,2},M_{\sigma,2})
are two lifting bases, both of which generate
(K_{b},K_{\sigma})
, such that
1
and
2
are satisfied for each. For
i\in\{1,2\}
, consider the SEE (
1.2
) on the Gelfand triplet
\mathcal{V}_{i}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{H}_{i}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{V}^{*}_{i}
associated with
(\mu_{i},M_{b,i},M_{\sigma,i})
, and denote by
\{\mathbf{P}_{i}^{\nu_{i}}\}_{\nu_{i}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{i})}\subset\mathcal{P}(\Lambda_{i})
,
\{P_{i,t}\}_{t\geq 0}
, and
\pi_{i}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{i})
the corresponding laws of the paths of the solutions on the path space
\Lambda_{i}
, the associated Markov semigroup, and its invariant probability measure, respectively. Furthermore, let
\mathbf{Q}_{i}^{\pi_{i}}\in\mathcal{P}(L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(0,\infty;\mathbb{R}^{n}))
and
\widetilde{\mathbf{Q}}_{i}^{\pi_{i}}\in\mathcal{P}((\mathbb{R}^{n})^{[0,\infty)})
be the stationary distributions constructed as in
2.16
(ii) and in
3.11
. Then, it holds that

\mathbf{Q}_{1}^{\pi_{1}}=\mathbf{Q}_{2}^{\pi_{2}}\ \ \text{in $\mathcal{P}\big(L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(0,\infty;\mathbb{R}^{n})\big)$}

(3.13)

and

\widetilde{\mathbf{Q}}_{1}^{\pi_{1}}=\widetilde{\mathbf{Q}}_{2}^{\pi_{2}}\ \ \text{in $\mathcal{P}\big((\mathbb{R}^{n})^{[0,\infty)}\big)$}.

(3.14)

Proof.

Since both
(\mu_{1},M_{b,1},M_{\sigma,1})
and
(\mu_{2},M_{b,2},M_{\sigma,2})
generate the same kernels
K_{b}
and
K_{\sigma}
, the characterization of Borel measures by their Laplace transforms implies that

M_{b,1}(\theta)\,\mu_{1}(\mathrm{d}\theta)=M_{b,2}(\theta)\,\mu_{2}(\mathrm{d}\theta)\ \ \text{and}\ \ M_{\sigma,1}(\theta)\,\mu_{1}(\mathrm{d}\theta)=M_{\sigma,2}(\theta)\,\mu_{2}(\mathrm{d}\theta).

Furthermore, since
M_{\sigma,i}(\theta)
is positive definite for
\mu_{i}
-a.e.
\theta\in[0,\infty)
by
1
for each
i\in\{1,2\}
, we see that the two measures
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
are equivalent. Set
\overline{\mu}:=\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}
. Clearly, we have
0<\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu_{i}}{\mathrm{d}\overline{\mu}}<1

\overline{\mu}
-a.e. for
i\in\{1,2\}
. Define

\overline{M}_{b}(\theta):=\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu_{i}}{\mathrm{d}\overline{\mu}}(\theta)M_{b,i}(\theta)\ \ \text{and}\ \ \overline{M}_{\sigma}(\theta):=\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu_{i}}{\mathrm{d}\overline{\mu}}(\theta)M_{\sigma,i}(\theta)

for each
\theta\in[0,\infty)
and
i\in\{1,2\}
. Note that
\overline{M}_{b}
and
\overline{M}_{\sigma}
are independent of
i\in\{1,2\}
. Moreover,
(\overline{\mu},\overline{M}_{b},\overline{M}_{\sigma})
constitutes a lifting basis in the sense of
2.7
and satisfies the first two conditions in
1
. For the given maps
b:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\sigma:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
, consider the SEE (
1.2
) on the Gelfand triplet
\overline{\mathcal{V}}\hookrightarrow\overline{\mathcal{H}}\hookrightarrow\overline{\mathcal{V}}^{*}
associated with
(\overline{\mu},\overline{M}_{b},\overline{M}_{\sigma})
, and denote the corresponding Markov semigroup on
\overline{\mathcal{H}}
by
\{\overline{P}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
. For each
\overline{\nu}\in\mathcal{P}(\overline{\mathcal{H}})
, denote by
\overline{\mathbf{P}}^{\overline{\nu}}
the law of the path of a solution with the initial distribution
\overline{\nu}
, which is a probability measure on the associated path space
\overline{\Lambda}
.

Let
i\in\{1,2\}
be fixed. Define
\Xi_{i}:\mathcal{V}_{i}^{*}\to\overline{\mathcal{V}}^{*}
by
\Xi_{i}y_{i}:=\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu_{i}}{\mathrm{d}\overline{\mu}}y_{i}
for each
y_{i}\in\mathcal{V}^{*}_{i}
. Clearly, this multiplication operator
\Xi_{i}
belongs to
L(\mathcal{V}^{*}_{i},\overline{\mathcal{V}}^{*})
,
L(\mathcal{H}_{i},\overline{\mathcal{H}})
and
L(\mathcal{V}_{i},\overline{\mathcal{V}})
. Furthermore, we have

\mu_{i}[y_{i}]=\overline{\mu}[\Xi_{i}y_{i}]\ \ \text{for any $y_{i}\in\mathcal{V}_{i}$}.

(3.15)

Thus, for each solution
Y_{i}=(Y_{i,t})_{t\geq 0}
of the SEE (
1.2
) corresponding to the lifting basis
(\mu_{i},M_{b,i},M_{\sigma,i})
with a given initial distribution
\nu_{i}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{i})
, the process
(\Xi_{i}Y_{i,t})_{t\geq 0}
is a solution of the SEE (
1.2
) corresponding to the lifting basis
(\overline{\mu},\overline{M}_{b},\overline{M}_{\sigma})
with the initial distribution
\nu_{i}\circ\Xi_{i}^{-1}\in\mathcal{P}(\overline{\mathcal{H}})
. Hence, we have

\mathbf{P}_{i}^{\nu_{i}}\circ\Xi_{i}^{-1}=\overline{\mathbf{P}}^{\nu_{i}\circ\Xi_{i}^{-1}}\ \ \text{in $\mathcal{P}\big(\overline{\Lambda}\big)$ for any $\nu_{i}\in\mathcal{P}\big(\mathcal{H}_{i}\big)$},

(3.16)

where, with a slight abuse of notation, we used the same symbol
\Xi_{i}
as before to represent the map
\Lambda_{i}\ni(\eta_{i,t})_{t\geq 0}\mapsto(\Xi_{i}\eta_{i,t})_{t\geq 0}\in\overline{\Lambda}
. In particular, we have
(P_{i,t}^{*}\nu_{i})\circ\Xi_{i}^{-1}=\overline{P}_{t}^{*}(\nu_{i}\circ\Xi_{i}^{-1})
in
\mathcal{P}(\overline{\mathcal{H}})
for any
\nu_{i}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{i})
and
t\geq 0
. Hence, for the invariant probability measure
\pi_{i}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{i})
with respect to the Markov semigroup
\{P_{i,t}\}_{t\geq 0}
, we have

\overline{P}_{t}^{*}\big(\pi_{i}\circ\Xi_{i}^{-1}\big)=(P_{i,t}^{*}\pi_{i})\circ\Xi_{i}^{-1}=\pi_{i}\circ\Xi_{i}^{-1}

in
\mathcal{P}(\overline{\mathcal{H}})
for any
t\geq 0
. This indicates that
\pi_{i}\circ\Xi_{i}^{-1}\in\mathcal{P}(\overline{\mathcal{H}})
is an invariant probability measure for the Markov semigroup
\{\overline{P}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
. This conclusion remains valid for each
i\in\{1,2\}
. However, since the lifting basis
(\overline{\mu},\overline{M}_{b},\overline{M}_{\sigma})
and the maps
b,\sigma
satisfy
1
,
3.2
(iii) shows that the Markov semigroup
\{\overline{P}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
on
\overline{\mathcal{H}}
has at most one invariant probability measure. Hence, it must hold that

\pi_{1}\circ\Xi_{1}^{-1}=\pi_{2}\circ\Xi_{2}^{-1}\ \ \text{in $\mathcal{P}\big(\overline{\mathcal{H}}\big)$}.

(3.17)

Observe that, for each
i\in\{1,2\}
,

\mathbf{Q}_{i}^{\pi_{i}}=\mathbf{P}_{i}^{\pi_{i}}\circ\mu_{i}[\cdot]^{-1}=\big(\mathbf{P}_{i}^{\pi_{i}}\circ\Xi_{i}^{-1}\big)\circ\overline{\mu}[\cdot]^{-1}=\overline{\mathbf{P}}^{\pi_{i}\circ\Xi_{i}^{-1}}\circ\overline{\mu}[\cdot]^{-1}

in
\mathcal{P}(L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(0,\infty;\mathbb{R}^{n}))
, where the first equality is precisely the definition of the probability measure
\mathbf{Q}_{i}^{\pi_{i}}\in\mathcal{P}(L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(0,\infty;\mathbb{R}^{n}))
, the second equality is due to (
3.15
), and the third equality is due to (
3.16
). From this, together with (
3.17
), we obtain the desired first equality (
3.13
). To prove the second equality (
3.14
), take arbitrary
t_{1},\dots,t_{\ell}\in[0,\infty)
and
A_{1},\dots,A_{\ell}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
. Fix
i\in\{1,2\}
. Define
B_{i},C_{i}\in\mathcal{B}(\Lambda_{i})
and
\overline{B},\overline{C}\in\mathcal{B}(\overline{\Lambda})
by

\displaystyle B_{i}:=\left\{(\eta_{i,t})_{t\geq 0}\in\Lambda_{i}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\eta_{i,t_{1}},\dots,\eta_{i,t_{\ell}}\in\mathcal{V}_{i}\right\},\ \ C_{i}:=\left\{(\eta_{i,t})_{t\geq 0}\in B_{i}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\mu_{i}[\eta_{i,t_{1}}]\in A_{1},\dots,\mu_{i}[\eta_{i,t_{\ell}}]\in A_{\ell}\right\},

\displaystyle\overline{B}:=\left\{(\overline{\eta}_{t})_{t\geq 0}\in\overline{\Lambda}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\overline{\eta}_{t_{1}},\dots,\overline{\eta}_{t_{\ell}}\in\overline{\mathcal{V}}\right\},\ \ \overline{C}:=\left\{(\overline{\eta}_{t})_{t\geq 0}\in\overline{B}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\overline{\mu}[\overline{\eta}_{t_{1}}]\in A_{1},\dots,\overline{\mu}[\overline{\eta}_{t_{\ell}}]\in A_{\ell}\right\}.

By (
3.15
), we see that
C_{i}=B_{i}\cap(\Xi_{i}^{-1}\overline{C})
. Furthermore, noting
3.11
, we have
\mathbf{P}_{i}^{\pi_{i}}(B_{i})=1
. Hence,

\widetilde{\mathbf{Q}}_{i}^{\pi_{i}}\left(\left\{(\xi_{t})_{t\geq 0}\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{[0,\infty)}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\xi_{t_{1}}\in A_{1},\dots,\xi_{t_{\ell}}\in A_{\ell}\right\}\right)\\
=\mathbf{P}_{i}^{\pi_{i}}(C_{i})=\mathbf{P}_{i}^{\pi_{i}}\big(\Xi_{i}^{-1}\overline{C}\big)=\overline{\mathbf{P}}^{\pi_{i}\circ\Xi_{i}^{-1}}\big(\overline{C}\big),

where the first equality is due to the definition of the probability measure
\widetilde{\mathbf{Q}}_{i}^{\pi_{i}}\in\mathcal{P}((\mathbb{R}^{n})^{[0,\infty)})
, the second equality is due to
C_{i}=B_{i}\cap(\Xi_{i}^{-1}\overline{C})
and
\mathbf{P}_{i}^{\pi_{i}}(B_{i})=1
, and the third equality is due to (
3.16
). The above and (
3.17
) ensure that every finite-dimensional distributions of
\widetilde{\mathbf{Q}}_{1}^{\pi_{1}}\in\mathcal{P}((\mathbb{R}^{n})^{[0,\infty)})
and
\widetilde{\mathbf{Q}}_{2}^{\pi_{2}}\in\mathcal{P}((\mathbb{R}^{n})^{[0,\infty)})
coincide, proving the desired second equality (
3.14
). This completes the proof.
∎

4
Proofs of the main results

In this section, we provide proofs of
3.2
,
3.4
and
3.6
. The most technically demanding part is the proof of
3.2
, where the “change-of-norm” technique and the “generalized coupling approach” play crucial roles; see
Section

4.1
. The “change-of-norm” technique is also employed to construct the Lyapunov function in
3.4
and
3.6
, as detailed in
Section

4.2
.

4.1
Proof of
3.2
: Contractivity,
d
-smallness, and the asymptotic log-Harnack inequality

As shown in the work
[
12
]
, in order to construct a “good” distance(-like) function satisfying the required conditions in the generalized Harris’ theorem of
[
29
]
(see
2.3
), a tractable strategy is to consider a
generalized coupling
of probability measures. We follow this idea to show
3.2
. The most important and technical point is to construct a suitable admissible weight function and a generalized coupling satisfying the required conditions in
[
12
]
; see
4.2
below.

As a first step, we present the following standard lemma, which reduces the proof of
3.2
to the case where the maps
b:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\sigma:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
are bounded.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
(\mu,M_{b},M_{\sigma})
be a lifting basis. Suppose that
b:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\sigma:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
are measurable maps satisfying
|b(x)-b(x^{\prime})|\leq C_{b,\mathrm{Lip}}|x-x^{\prime}|
and
|\sigma(x)-\sigma(x^{\prime})|\leq C_{\sigma,\mathrm{Lip}}|x-x^{\prime}|
for all
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, with some constants
C_{b,\mathrm{Lip}},C_{\sigma,\mathrm{Lip}}>0
. For each
N\in\mathbb{N}
, define the truncated maps
b^{N}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\sigma^{N}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
by

b^{N}(x):=\begin{cases}b(x)\ &\text{if $|x|\leq N$},\\
b\left(\frac{N}{|x|}x\right)\ &\text{if $|x|>N$},\end{cases}\ \ \text{and}\ \ \sigma^{N}(x):=\begin{cases}\sigma(x)\ &\text{if $|x|\leq N$},\\
\sigma\left(\frac{N}{|x|}x\right)\ &\text{if $|x|>N$}.\end{cases}

Let
\{P_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
and
\{P^{N}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
be the Markov semigroups associated with the SEEs (
1.2
) with coefficients
(b,\sigma)
and
(b^{N},\sigma^{N})
, respectively. Then, for any
t\geq 0
and any
y\in\mathcal{H}
, we have
\lim_{N\to\infty}P^{N}_{t}(y,\cdot)=P_{t}(y,\cdot)
weakly in
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})
.

Proof.

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
be fixed. Note that

|b^{N}(x)-b^{N}(x^{\prime})|\leq C_{b,\mathrm{Lip}}|x-x^{\prime}|\ \ \text{and}\ \ |\sigma^{N}(x)-\sigma^{N}(x^{\prime})|\leq C_{\sigma,\mathrm{Lip}}|x-x^{\prime}|

(4.1)

for all
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and

|b(x)-b^{N}(x)|\leq C_{b,\mathrm{Lip}}|x|\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{\{|x|>N\}}\ \ \text{and}\ \ |\sigma(x)-\sigma^{N}(x)|\leq C_{\sigma,\mathrm{Lip}}|x|\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{\{|x|>N\}}

(4.2)

for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Fix an arbitrary
y\in\mathcal{H}
. By
2.15
, there exist unique solutions
Y
and
Y^{N}
to the SEEs (
1.2
) with coefficients
(b,\sigma)
and
(b^{N},\sigma^{N})
, respectively, driven by a common
d
-dimensional Brownian motion
W
on a complete probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
, such that
Y_{0}=Y^{N}_{0}=y
. Note that
\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(Y_{t})=P_{t}(y,\cdot)
and
\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(Y^{N}_{t})=P^{N}_{t}(y,\cdot)
for any
t\geq 0
. Furthermore, by (
2.15
), we have

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\Big\{\big\|Y_{t}\big\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+\big\|Y^{N}_{t}\big\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\Big\}+\int^{T}_{0}\Big\{\big\|Y_{t}\big\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}+\big\|Y^{N}_{t}\big\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\Big\}\,\mathrm{d}t\right]<\infty

(4.3)

for any
T>0
.

Applying Itô’s formula for the squared norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}
in
2.19
to the
\mathcal{H}
-valued process
Y-Y^{N}
yields

\|Y_{t}-Y^{N}_{t}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}=\int^{t}_{0}F^{N}\big(Y_{s},Y^{N}_{s}\big)\,\mathrm{d}s+2\int^{t}_{0}\Big\langle Y_{s}-Y^{N}_{s},\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}\big(\sigma(\mu[Y_{s}])-\sigma^{N}(\mu[Y^{N}_{s}])\big)\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

(4.4)

for any
t\geq 0
a.s., where the function
F^{N}:\mathcal{V}\times\mathcal{V}\to\mathbb{R}
is given by

F^{N}(y_{1},y_{2}):=2\Big\langle\mathcal{A}(y_{1}-y_{2})+\mathcal{M}_{b}\big(b(\mu[y_{1}])-b^{N}(\mu[y_{2}])\big),y_{1}-y_{2}\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{V}^{*},\mathcal{V}}+\big\|\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}\big(\sigma(\mu[y_{1}])-\sigma^{N}(\mu[y_{2}])\big)\big\|_{L_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathcal{H})}^{2}

for
y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathcal{V}
. Analogously to the proof of
2.15
, it follows from (
4.1
), (
4.2
), and (
2.13
), together with
2.10
,
\mathcal{M}_{b}\in L(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathcal{V}^{*})
,
\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}\in L(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathcal{H})
, and Young’s inequality, that there exists a constant
C>0
depending only on the lifting basis
(\mu,M_{b},M_{\sigma})
and the constants
C_{b,\mathrm{Lip}}
and
C_{\sigma,\mathrm{Lip}}
such that

F^{N}(y_{1},y_{2})\leq C\big\{\|y_{1}-y_{2}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+\big|\mu[y_{1}]\big|^{2}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{\{|\mu[y_{1}]|>N\}}\big\}

(4.5)

for any
y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathcal{V}
. Furthermore, by (
4.1
), (
4.2
), and (
4.3
), along with the fact that
\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}\in L(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathcal{H})
, the stochastic integral in the right-hand side of (
4.4
) is a martingale under
\mathbb{P}
. Hence, by taking the expectations on both sides of (
4.4
), applying (
4.5
) and using Gronwall’s inequality, we obtain

\mathbb{E}\big[\|Y_{t}-Y^{N}_{t}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\big]\leq Ce^{Ct}\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{t}_{0}\big|\mu[Y_{s}]\big|^{2}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{\{|\mu[Y_{s}]|>N\}}\,\mathrm{d}s\right]

for any
t\geq 0
. Since
\mu[\cdot]\in L(\mathcal{V};\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
\mathbb{E}[\int^{t}_{0}\|Y_{s}\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s]<\infty
, by the dominated convergence theorem, the right-hand side above tends to zero as
N\to\infty
, and hence
\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}[\|Y_{t}-Y^{N}_{t}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}]=0
for any
t\geq 0
. This in particular implies that
\lim_{N\to\infty}P^{N}_{t}(y,\cdot)=P_{t}(y,\cdot)
weakly in
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})
for any
t\geq 0
. This completes the proof.
∎

Provided that
b:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\sigma:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
satisfy the conditions in
1
, the truncated maps
b^{N}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\sigma^{N}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
in
4.1
satisfy the same conditions with the same constants
C_{b,\mathrm{Lip}}
,
C_{\sigma,\mathrm{Lip}}
and
C_{\mathrm{UE}}
. Furthermore, for each admissible weight function
\Phi
, the map
\mathbb{W}_{d_{\Phi}}:\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})\times\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})\to[0,1]
is a metric on
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})
that generates the topology of weak convergence. Thus, in proving
3.2
, we may assume without loss of generality that the coefficients
b
and
\sigma
are bounded. The key is to construct an admissible weight function
\Phi
that depends only on the lifting basis
(\mu,M_{b},M_{\sigma})
and the constants
C_{b,\mathrm{Lip}}
,
C_{\sigma,\mathrm{Lip}}
and
C_{\mathrm{UE}}
, while remaining independent of the specific bounds of
b
and
\sigma
.

The following lemma constitutes the most crucial step in the proof of
3.2
. In what follows, for two probability measures
\nu_{1},\nu_{2}
on a measurable space
(E,\mathcal{E})
such that
\nu_{1}
is absolutely continuous with respect to
\nu_{2}
,
D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\nu_{1}\|\nu_{2}):=\int_{E}\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\nu_{1}}{\mathrm{d}\nu_{2}}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{1}
denotes the Kullback–Leibler divergence, also called relative entropy.

Lemma 4.2
.

Assume that a lifting basis
(\mu,M_{b},M_{\sigma})
and measurable maps
b:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
\sigma:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
satisfy
1
. Suppose further that the map
\sigma:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
is bounded. Then, there exist an admissible weight function
\Phi:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
, a family
\{(Y^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t},\widehat{Y}^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t})\}_{t\geq 0,\,y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathcal{H}}
of
\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{H}
-valued random variables
(Y^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t},\widehat{Y}^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t})
on a probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
, and a family
\{\widehat{\mathbb{P}}^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0,\,y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathcal{H}}
of probability measures on
(\Omega,\mathcal{F})
with
\widehat{\mathbb{P}}^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t}\sim\mathbb{P}
, such that the following properties hold for any
t\geq 0
and
y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathcal{H}
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(Y^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t})=P_{t}(y_{1},\cdot),

(4.6)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Law}_{\widehat{\mathbb{P}}^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t}}(\widehat{Y}^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t})=P_{t}(y_{2},\cdot),

(4.7)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}\left[\big\|Y^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t}-\widehat{Y}^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t}\big\|_{\Phi}\right]\leq e^{-\kappa t/2}\|y_{1}-y_{2}\|_{\Phi},

(4.8)

\displaystyle D_{\mathrm{KL}}\big(\mathbb{P}\big\|\widehat{\mathbb{P}}^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t}\big)\leq\frac{1}{2}\|y_{1}-y_{2}\|_{\Phi}^{2}.

(4.9)

The admissible weight function
\Phi
can be chosen to depend only on the lifting basis
(\mu,M_{b},M_{\sigma})
and the constants
C_{b,\mathrm{Lip}}
,
C_{\sigma,\mathrm{Lip}}
, and
C_{\mathrm{UE}}
in
1
.

Remark 4.3
.

The joint distribution on
\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{H}
of the pair of random variables
(Y^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t},\widehat{Y}^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t})
under
\mathbb{P}
can be regarded as a generalized coupling between two probability measures
P_{t}(y_{1},\cdot)\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})
and
P_{t}(y_{2},\cdot)\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})
in the sense that, although the first marginal
\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(Y^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t})
coincides with
P_{t}(y_{1},\cdot)
, the second marginal
\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(\widehat{Y}^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t})
does not necessarily coincide with
P_{t}(y_{2},\cdot)
. Instead,
\widehat{Y}^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t}
should satisfy the constraints (
4.7
), (
4.8
) and (
4.9
). We define
\widehat{Y}^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t}
as the solution to a
controlled SEE
(see (
4.10
) below), which involves a (finite-dimensional) control process in the drift term. Recall that the SEE (
1.2
) is a highly degenerate infinite-dimensional system. Hence, in order to meet the constraints (
4.7
), (
4.8
) and (
4.9
), we have to control the dynamics in the infinite-dimensional space
\mathcal{H}
by a finite-dimensional control process. This is a significant challenge for a general infinite-dimensional model with degenerate noise, and a model-dependent analysis is required. Our construction of a generalized coupling is based on the specific structure of the SEE (
1.2
), where a careful treatment of the integral operator
\mu[\cdot]\in L(\mathcal{V};\mathbb{R}^{n})
(which is not continuous in
\mathcal{H}
) is essential. In order to manage the influence of
\mu[\cdot]
, we perform a “change-of-norm” technique based on
2.17
. A key idea is to construct a suitable admissible weight function
\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}
depending on a control parameter
\mathfrak{a}
such that the operator
\mu_{\sigma,\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}[\cdot]
(see
2.17
) approximates
\mu[\cdot]
in some sense.

Proof of
4.2
.

Recall that
\kappa:=\inf{\mathrm{supp}\,\mu}>0
by
1
. Fix a tuple
\mathfrak{a}=(m,\delta,L,R)
of constants
m,\delta,L,R>0
, which will be determined later. Define
\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
by

\displaystyle\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}(\theta)

\displaystyle:=M_{\sigma}(\theta)^{-1}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[\kappa,m)\cap A_{L}\cap B_{R}}(\theta)+\left(\delta\theta^{1/2}I_{n\times n}+M_{\sigma}(\theta)\right)^{-1}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{([\kappa,m)\setminus A_{L})\cap B_{R}}(\theta)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+I_{n\times n}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[\kappa,m)\setminus B_{R}}(\theta)+m^{1/2}\theta^{-1/2}I_{n\times n}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[m,\infty)}(\theta)

for
\theta\in[0,\infty)
, where the sets
A_{L},B_{R}\in\mathcal{B}([0,\infty))
are defined by

\displaystyle A_{L}:=\left\{\theta\in[0,\infty)\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\text{$M_{\sigma}(\theta)\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}$ is symmetric, positive definite and satisfies $\big|M_{\sigma}(\theta)^{-1}\big|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq L$}\right\},

\displaystyle B_{R}:=\left\{\theta\in[0,\infty)\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\text{$M_{\sigma}(\theta)\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}$ is symmetric, positive definite and satisfies $\big|M_{\sigma}(\theta)\big|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq R$}\right\}.

By construction, we see that
\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}(\theta)\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is symmetric, positive definite and satisfies

\displaystyle\big|\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}(\theta)\big|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\max\{L,\delta^{-1}\kappa^{-1/2},1\}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[\kappa,m)}(\theta)+m^{1/2}\theta^{-1/2}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[m,\infty)}(\theta),

\displaystyle\big|\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}(\theta)^{-1}\big|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\max\{\delta m^{1/2}+R,1\}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[\kappa,m)}(\theta)+m^{-1/2}\theta^{1/2}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[m,\infty)}(\theta),

for any
\theta\geq\kappa:=\inf{\mathrm{supp}\,\mu}>0
. Hence, (
2.32
) holds for
\mu
-a.e.
\theta\in[0,\infty)
for some constant
C_{\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}>0
(which may depend on
\mathfrak{a}=(m,\delta,L,R)
), and thus
\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}
is an admissible weight function.

Let
W
be a
d
-dimensional Brownian motion defined on a complete probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
, with respect to a filtration
\mathbb{F}=(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
satisfying the usual conditions. In this proof, we denote by
\mathbb{E}[\cdot]
the expectation under the probability measure
\mathbb{P}
. Fix
y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathcal{H}
. Let
Y=Y^{y_{1},y_{2}}=Y^{y_{1}}
be the solution to the SEE (
1.2
) with the initial condition
Y_{0}=y_{1}
. Clearly, the relation (
4.6
) holds. Now we introduce the following
controlled SEE
defined on the same probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
as
Y
:

\begin{cases}\mathrm{d}\widehat{Y}_{t}(\theta)=-\theta\widehat{Y}_{t}(\theta)\,\mathrm{d}t+\Big\{M_{b}(\theta)b(\mu[\widehat{Y}_{t}])+\lambda_{\mathfrak{a}}M_{\sigma}(\theta)\mu_{\sigma,\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}\big[Y_{t}-\widehat{Y}_{t}\big]\Big\}\,\mathrm{d}t+M_{\sigma}(\theta)\sigma(\mu[\widehat{Y}_{t}])\,\mathrm{d}W_{t}\\
\hskip 170.71652ptt\geq 0,\ \theta\in[0,\infty),\\
\widehat{Y}_{0}(\theta)=y_{2}(\theta),\ \ \theta\in[0,\infty),\end{cases}

(4.10)

where
\lambda_{\mathfrak{a}}>0
is a constant depending on
\mathfrak{a}=(m,\delta,L,R)
, which will be also determined later. Here, the operator
\mu_{\sigma,\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}[\cdot]\in L(\mathcal{H};\mathbb{R}^{n})
is defined as in
2.17
. Analogously to the proof of
2.15
, applying the general result on monotone SPDEs
[
47
, Theorem 4.2.4]
to the controlled SEE (
4.10
), we see that there exists a unique solution
\widehat{Y}=\widehat{Y}^{y_{1},y_{2}}
of (
4.10
) (defined in the same manner as in
2.13
). Furthermore, for any
T>0
, we have

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\Big\{\big\|Y_{t}\big\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+\big\|\widehat{Y}_{t}\big\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\Big\}+\int^{T}_{0}\Big\{\big\|Y_{t}\big\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}+\big\|\widehat{Y}_{t}\big\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\Big\}\,\mathrm{d}t\right]<\infty.

(4.11)

Applying Itô’s formula for the squared norm
\|\cdot\|_{\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}^{2}
in
2.19
to the
\mathcal{H}
-valued continuous adapted process
Y-\widehat{Y}
shows that

\displaystyle\big\|Y_{t}-\widehat{Y}_{t}\big\|_{\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\big\|y_{1}-y_{2}\big\|_{\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}^{2}+2\int^{t}_{0}\Big\langle Y_{s}-\widehat{Y}_{s},\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}\big(\sigma(\mu[Y_{s}])-\sigma(\mu[\widehat{Y}_{s}])\big)\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}\Big\rangle_{\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\int^{t}_{0}\Big\{2\Big\langle\mathcal{A}\big(Y_{s}-\widehat{Y}_{s}\big)+\mathcal{M}_{b}\big(b(\mu[Y_{s}])-b(\mu[\widehat{Y}_{s}])\big)-\lambda_{\mathfrak{a}}\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}\mu_{\sigma,\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}\big[Y_{s}-\widehat{Y}_{s}\big],Y_{s}-\widehat{Y}_{s}\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{V}^{*},\mathcal{V},\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt+\Big\|\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}\big(\sigma(\mu[Y_{s}])-\sigma(\mu[\widehat{Y}_{s}])\big)\Big\|_{L_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};(\mathcal{H},\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}))}^{2}\Big\}\,\mathrm{d}s

for any
t\geq 0
a.s. Recalling
2.17
and the formulas (
2.33
), (
2.34
), (
2.35
) and (
2.36
), the above can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\big\|Y_{t}-\widehat{Y}_{t}\big\|_{\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}^{2}+2\int^{t}_{0}\mathopen{\big|\mkern-1.5mu\big|\mkern-1.5mu\big|}Y_{s}-\widehat{Y}_{s}\mathclose{\big|\mkern-1.5mu\big|\mkern-1.5mu\big|}_{\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s+2\lambda_{\mathfrak{a}}\int^{t}_{0}\big|\mu_{\sigma,\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}\big[Y_{s}-\widehat{Y}_{s}\big]\big|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\big\|y_{1}-y_{2}\big\|_{\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}^{2}+2\int^{t}_{0}\Big\langle\mu_{\sigma,\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}\big[Y_{s}-\widehat{Y}_{s}\big],\big(\sigma(\mu[Y_{s}])-\sigma(\mu[\widehat{Y}_{s}])\big)\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}\Big\rangle

\displaystyle\hskip 8.5359pt+\int^{t}_{0}\Big\{2\Big\langle b(\mu[Y_{s}])-b(\mu[\widehat{Y}_{s}]),\mu_{b,\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}\big[Y_{s}-\widehat{Y}_{s}\big]\Big\rangle+\mathrm{tr}\Big[\big(\sigma(\mu[Y_{s}])-\sigma(\mu[\widehat{Y}_{s}])\big)^{\top}Q_{\sigma,\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}\big(\sigma(\mu[Y_{s}])-\sigma(\mu[\widehat{Y}_{s}])\big)\Big]\Big\}\,\mathrm{d}s

for any
t\geq 0
a.s. Applying the usual Itô’s formula to the product of the function
e^{\kappa t}
and the real semimartingale
\|Y_{t}-\widehat{Y}_{t}\|_{\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}^{2}
, we get

\displaystyle e^{\kappa t}\big\|Y_{t}-\widehat{Y}_{t}\big\|_{\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}^{2}+2\int^{t}_{0}e^{\kappa s}\mathopen{\big|\mkern-1.5mu\big|\mkern-1.5mu\big|}Y_{s}-\widehat{Y}_{s}\mathclose{\big|\mkern-1.5mu\big|\mkern-1.5mu\big|}_{\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s+2\lambda_{\mathfrak{a}}\int^{t}_{0}e^{\kappa s}\big|\mu_{\sigma,\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}\big[Y_{s}-\widehat{Y}_{s}\big]\big|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\big\|y_{1}-y_{2}\big\|_{\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}^{2}+2\int^{t}_{0}e^{\kappa s}\Big\langle\mu_{\sigma,\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}\big[Y_{s}-\widehat{Y}_{s}\big],\big(\sigma(\mu[Y_{s}])-\sigma(\mu[\widehat{Y}_{s}])\big)\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}\Big\rangle

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\int^{t}_{0}e^{\kappa s}\Big\{\kappa\|Y_{s}-\widehat{Y}_{s}\|_{\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}^{2}+2\Big\langle b(\mu[Y_{s}])-b(\mu[\widehat{Y}_{s}]),\mu_{b,\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}\big[Y_{s}-\widehat{Y}_{s}\big]\Big\rangle

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt+\mathrm{tr}\Big[\big(\sigma(\mu[Y_{s}])-\sigma(\mu[\widehat{Y}_{s}])\big)^{\top}Q_{\sigma,\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}\big(\sigma(\mu[Y_{s}])-\sigma(\mu[\widehat{Y}_{s}])\big)\Big]\Big\}\,\mathrm{d}s

for any
t\geq 0
a.s. Since
\kappa=\inf{\mathrm{supp}\,\mu}
, we have
\kappa\|Y_{s}-\widehat{Y}_{s}\|_{\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}^{2}\leq\mathopen{|\mkern-1.5mu|\mkern-1.5mu|}Y_{s}-\widehat{Y}_{s}\mathclose{|\mkern-1.5mu|\mkern-1.5mu|}_{\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}^{2}
. By this estimate and the Lipschitz continuity of the maps
b:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\sigma:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
(see
1
), we obtain

\begin{split}&e^{\kappa t}\big\|Y_{t}-\widehat{Y}_{t}\big\|_{\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}^{2}+\int^{t}_{0}e^{\kappa s}\mathopen{\big|\mkern-1.5mu\big|\mkern-1.5mu\big|}Y_{s}-\widehat{Y}_{s}\mathclose{\big|\mkern-1.5mu\big|\mkern-1.5mu\big|}_{\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s+2\lambda_{\mathfrak{a}}\int^{t}_{0}e^{\kappa s}\big|\mu_{\sigma,\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}\big[Y_{s}-\widehat{Y}_{s}\big]\big|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\\
&\leq\big\|y_{1}-y_{2}\big\|_{\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}^{2}+2\int^{t}_{0}e^{\kappa s}\Big\langle\mu_{\sigma,\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}\big[Y_{s}-\widehat{Y}_{s}\big],\big(\sigma(\mu[Y_{s}])-\sigma(\mu[\widehat{Y}_{s}])\big)\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}\Big\rangle+\int^{t}_{0}e^{\kappa s}F_{\mathfrak{a}}\big(Y_{s}-\widehat{Y}_{s}\big)\,\mathrm{d}s\end{split}

(4.12)

for any
t\geq 0
a.s., where the function
F_{\mathfrak{a}}:\mathcal{V}\to[0,\infty)
is defined by

F_{\mathfrak{a}}(y):=2C_{b,\mathrm{Lip}}\big|\mu[y]\big|\big|\mu_{b,\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}[y]\big|+C_{\sigma,\mathrm{Lip}}^{2}\big|Q_{\sigma,\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}\big|_{\mathrm{op}}\big|\mu[y]\big|^{2},\ \ y\in\mathcal{V}.

We provide an estimate of
F_{\mathfrak{a}}(y)
in terms of
\mathopen{|\mkern-1.5mu|\mkern-1.5mu|}y\mathclose{|\mkern-1.5mu|\mkern-1.5mu|}_{\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}
and
|\mu_{\sigma,\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}[y]|
for each
y\in\mathcal{V}
. By the triangle inequality, we have

F_{\mathfrak{a}}(y)\leq 2C_{b,\mathrm{Lip}}\Big\{\big|\mu_{\sigma,\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}[y]\big|+\big|\overline{\mu}_{\sigma,\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}[y]\big|\Big\}\big|\mu_{b,\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}[y]\big|+2C_{\sigma,\mathrm{Lip}}^{2}\big|Q_{\sigma,\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}\big|_{\mathrm{op}}\Big\{\big|\mu_{\sigma,\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}[y]\big|^{2}+\big|\overline{\mu}_{\sigma,\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}[y]\big|^{2}\Big\},

(4.13)

where
\overline{\mu}_{\sigma,\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}[\cdot]\in L(\mathcal{V};\mathbb{R}^{n})
is defined by

\overline{\mu}_{\sigma,\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}[y]:=\mu[y]-\mu_{\sigma,\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}[y]=\int_{[0,\infty)}\big(I_{n\times n}-M_{\sigma}(\theta)^{\top}\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}(\theta)\big)y(\theta)\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta),\ \ y\in\mathcal{V}.

We estimate the three terms
|\overline{\mu}_{\sigma,\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}[y]|
,
|\mu_{b,\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}[y]|
and
|Q_{\sigma,\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}|_{\mathrm{op}}
. First, concerning
|\overline{\mu}_{\sigma,\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}[y]|
, observe that

\displaystyle\left|\left(I_{n\times n}-M_{\sigma}(\theta)^{\top}\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}(\theta)\right)\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}(\theta)^{-1/2}\right|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\left|\left(I_{n\times n}-M_{\sigma}(\theta)\left(\delta\theta^{1/2}I_{n\times n}+M_{\sigma}(\theta)\right)^{-1}\right)\left(\delta\theta^{1/2}I_{n\times n}+M_{\sigma}(\theta)\right)^{1/2}\right|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{([\kappa,m)\setminus A_{L})\cap B_{R}}(\theta)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\big|I_{n\times n}-M_{\sigma}(\theta)\big|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[\kappa,m)\setminus B_{R}}(\theta)+m^{-1/2}\theta^{1/2}\left|I_{n\times n}-m^{1/2}\theta^{-1/2}M_{\sigma}(\theta)\right|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[m,\infty)}(\theta)

\displaystyle\leq\delta\theta^{1/2}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[\kappa,\infty)\setminus A_{L}}(\theta)+\big(1+|M_{\sigma}(\theta)|_{\mathrm{op}}\big)^{2}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[\kappa,\infty)\setminus B_{R}}(\theta)+m^{-1/2}\theta^{1/2}\big(1+|M_{\sigma}(\theta)|_{\mathrm{op}}\big)^{2}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[m,\infty)}(\theta)

for any
\theta\geq\kappa:=\inf{\mathrm{supp}\,\mu}>0
. From this, together with the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, we have

\displaystyle\big|\overline{\mu}_{\sigma,\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}[y]\big|

\displaystyle\leq\left\{\int_{[\kappa,\infty)}\theta^{-1}\left|\left(I_{n\times n}-M_{\sigma}(\theta)^{\top}\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}(\theta)\right)\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}(\theta)^{-1/2}\right|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)\right\}^{1/2}\left\{\int_{[0,\infty)}\theta\left|\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}(\theta)^{1/2}y(\theta)\right|^{2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)\right\}^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\alpha(m,\delta,L,R)^{1/2}\mathopen{|\mkern-1.5mu|\mkern-1.5mu|}y\mathclose{|\mkern-1.5mu|\mkern-1.5mu|}_{\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}},

(4.14)

where
\alpha(m,\delta,L,R)\geq 0
is given by

\displaystyle\alpha(m,\delta,L,R)

\displaystyle:=\delta\int_{[\kappa,\infty)\setminus A_{L}}\theta^{-1/2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)+\int_{[\kappa,\infty)\setminus B_{R}}\theta^{-1}\big(1+|M_{\sigma}(\theta)|_{\mathrm{op}}\big)^{2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+m^{-1/2}\int_{[m,\infty)}\theta^{-1/2}\big(1+|M_{\sigma}(\theta)|_{\mathrm{op}}\big)^{2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta).

By the integrability conditions (
2.6
) and (
2.8
) on
\mu
and
M_{\sigma}
, combined with the assumption
\kappa:=\inf{\mathrm{supp}\,\mu}>0
, the constant
\alpha(m,\delta,L,R)
is finite. Next, concerning
|\mu_{b,\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}[y]|
, observe that

\displaystyle\left|M_{b}(\theta)^{\top}\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}(\theta)^{1/2}\right|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\big|M_{b}(\theta)^{\top}M_{\sigma}(\theta)^{-1/2}\big|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[\kappa,m)\cap A_{L}\cap B_{R}}(\theta)+\left|M_{b}(\theta)^{\top}\left(\delta\theta^{1/2}I_{n\times n}+M_{\sigma}(\theta)\right)^{-1/2}\right|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{([\kappa,m)\setminus A_{L})\cap B_{R}}(\theta)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+|M_{b}(\theta)|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[\kappa,m)\setminus B_{R}}(\theta)+m^{1/2}\theta^{-1/2}|M_{b}(\theta)|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[m,\infty)}(\theta)

\displaystyle\leq\beta(m,\delta,L)\theta^{-1/2}|M_{b}(\theta)|^{2}_{\mathrm{op}}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[\kappa,\infty)}(\theta)

for any
\theta\geq\kappa:=\inf{\mathrm{supp}\,\mu}>0
, where the constant
\beta(m,\delta,L)>0
is given by

\beta(m,\delta,L):=\max\Big\{Lm^{1/2},\delta^{-1},m^{1/2}\Big\}.

Hence, using the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, we have

\displaystyle\big|\mu_{b,\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}[y]\big|

\displaystyle\leq\left\{\int_{[\kappa,\infty)}\theta^{-1}\left|M_{b}(\theta)^{\top}\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}(\theta)^{1/2}\right|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)\right\}^{1/2}\left\{\int_{[0,\infty)}\theta\left|\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}(\theta)^{1/2}y(\theta)\right|^{2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)\right\}^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\{\beta(m,\delta,L)\int_{[\kappa,\infty)}\theta^{-3/2}|M_{b}(\theta)|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)\right\}^{1/2}\mathopen{|\mkern-1.5mu|\mkern-1.5mu|}y\mathclose{|\mkern-1.5mu|\mkern-1.5mu|}_{\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}.

(4.15)

Similarly, concerning
|Q_{\sigma,\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}|_{\mathrm{op}}
, we have

\displaystyle\left|M_{\sigma}(\theta)^{\top}\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}(\theta)M_{\sigma}(\theta)\right|_{\mathrm{op}}

\displaystyle=|M_{\sigma}(\theta)|_{\mathrm{op}}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[\kappa,m)\cap A_{L}\cap B_{R}}(\theta)+\left|M_{\sigma}(\theta)\left(\delta\theta^{1/2}I_{n\times n}+M_{\sigma}(\theta)\right)^{-1}M_{\sigma}(\theta)\right|_{\mathrm{op}}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{([\kappa,m)\setminus A_{L})\cap B_{R}}(\theta)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+|M_{\sigma}(\theta)|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[\kappa,m)\setminus B_{R}}(\theta)+m^{1/2}\theta^{-1/2}|M_{\sigma}(\theta)|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[m,\infty)}(\theta)

\displaystyle\leq\beta(m,\delta,L)\theta^{-1/2}|M_{\sigma}(\theta)|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[\kappa,\infty)}(\theta)

for any
\theta\geq\kappa:=\inf{\mathrm{supp}\,\mu}>0
. Hence, we have

\big|Q_{\sigma,\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}\big|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\beta(m,\delta,L)\int_{[\kappa,\infty)}\theta^{-1/2}|M_{\sigma}(\theta)|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta).

(4.16)

Combining (
4.13
) with (
4.14
), (
4.15
) and (
4.16
), and using Young’s inequality, we get

F_{\mathfrak{a}}(y)\leq\left(\varepsilon(m,\delta,L,R)+\frac{1}{2}\right)\mathopen{|\mkern-1.5mu|\mkern-1.5mu|}y\mathclose{|\mkern-1.5mu|\mkern-1.5mu|}_{\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}^{2}+C(m,\delta,L)\big|\mu_{\sigma,\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}[y]\big|^{2}

(4.17)

for any
y\in\mathcal{V}
, where the constants
\varepsilon(m,\delta,L,R)\geq 0
and
C(m,\delta,L)>0
are given by

\displaystyle\varepsilon(m,\delta,L,R)

\displaystyle:=2C_{b,\mathrm{Lip}}\left\{\alpha(m,\delta,L,R)\beta(m,\delta,L)\int_{[\kappa,\infty)}\theta^{-3/2}|M_{b}(\theta)|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)\right\}^{1/2}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+2C_{\sigma,\mathrm{Lip}}^{2}\alpha(m,\delta,L,R)\beta(m,\delta,L)\int_{[\kappa,\infty)}\theta^{-1/2}|M_{\sigma}(\theta)|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)

and

C(m,\delta,L):=2\beta(m,\delta,L)\left\{C_{b,\mathrm{Lip}}^{2}\int_{[\kappa,\infty)}\theta^{-3/2}|M_{b}(\theta)|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)+C_{\sigma,\mathrm{Lip}}^{2}\int_{[\kappa,\infty)}\theta^{-1/2}|M_{\sigma}(\theta)|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)\right\}.

By the integrability conditions (
2.7
) and (
2.8
) on
M_{b}
and
M_{\sigma}
, together with
\kappa:=\inf{\mathrm{supp}\,\mu}>0
, all the integrals appearing in the expressions of
\varepsilon(m,\delta,L,R)
and
C(m,\delta,L)
are finite.

The constant
\varepsilon(m,\delta,L,R)\geq 0
can be arbitrarily small by choosing suitable parameters
m,\delta,L,R>0
. Indeed, observe that

\displaystyle\alpha(m,\delta,L,R)\beta(m,\delta,L)

\displaystyle=\delta\max\Big\{Lm^{1/2},\delta^{-1},m^{1/2}\Big\}\int_{[\kappa,\infty)\setminus A_{L}}\theta^{-1/2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\max\Big\{Lm^{1/2},\delta^{-1},m^{1/2}\Big\}\int_{[\kappa,\infty)\setminus B_{R}}\theta^{-1}\big(1+|M_{\sigma}(\theta)|_{\mathrm{op}}\big)^{2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)
