\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+m^{-1/2}\max\Big\{Lm^{1/2},\delta^{-1},m^{1/2}\Big\}\int_{[m,\infty)}\theta^{-1/2}\big(1+|M_{\sigma}(\theta)|_{\mathrm{op}}\big)^{2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta).

Note that
\mu([\kappa,\infty)\setminus(\bigcup_{L>0}A_{L}))=0
and
\mu([\kappa,\infty)\setminus(\bigcup_{R>0}B_{R}))=0
by
1
. Recalling again the integrability conditions (
2.6
) and (
2.8
) on
\mu
and
M_{\sigma}
, as well as the assumption
\kappa:=\inf{\mathrm{supp}\,\mu}>0
, the dominated convergence theorem yields

\displaystyle\lim_{L\to\infty}\int_{[\kappa,\infty)\setminus A_{L}}\theta^{-1/2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)=0,

\displaystyle\lim_{R\to\infty}\int_{[\kappa,\infty)\setminus B_{R}}\theta^{-1}\big(1+|M_{\sigma}(\theta)|_{\mathrm{op}}\big)^{2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)=0,\ \ \text{and}

\displaystyle\lim_{m\to\infty}\int_{[m,\infty)}\theta^{-1/2}\big(1+|M_{\sigma}(\theta)|_{\mathrm{op}}\big)^{2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)=0.

Setting
\delta=\delta_{m}>0
and
L=L_{m}>0
according to
m>0
by

\displaystyle\delta_{m}:=m^{-1/2}\left\{\int_{[m,\infty)}\theta^{-1/2}\big(1+|M_{\sigma}(\theta)|_{\mathrm{op}}\big)^{2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)+\frac{1}{m}\right\}^{1/2}\ \ \text{and}

\displaystyle L_{m}:=\left\{\int_{[m,\infty)}\theta^{-1/2}\big(1+|M_{\sigma}(\theta)|_{\mathrm{op}}\big)^{2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)+\frac{1}{m}\right\}^{-1/2},

we see that

\lim_{m\to\infty}\lim_{R\to\infty}\alpha(m,\delta_{m},L_{m},R)\beta(m,\delta_{m},L_{m})=0.

Hence, we have

\lim_{m\to\infty}\lim_{R\to\infty}\varepsilon(m,\delta_{m},L_{m},R)=0.

Therefore, there exist constants
m,\delta,L,R>0
such that
\varepsilon(m,\delta,L,R)\leq\frac{1}{2}
. Fix such a set of parameters
\mathfrak{a}=(m,\delta,L,R)
, and set
\lambda_{\mathfrak{a}}=C(m,\delta,L)>0
. Then, (
4.12
) and (
4.17
) yield

\begin{split}&e^{\kappa t}\big\|Y_{t}-\widehat{Y}_{t}\big\|_{\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}^{2}+\lambda_{\mathfrak{a}}\int^{t}_{0}e^{\kappa s}\big|\mu_{\sigma,\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}\big[Y_{s}-\widehat{Y}_{s}\big]\big|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\\
&\leq\big\|y_{1}-y_{2}\big\|_{\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}^{2}+2\int^{t}_{0}e^{\kappa s}\Big\langle\mu_{\sigma,\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}\big[Y_{s}-\widehat{Y}_{s}\big],\big(\sigma(\mu[Y_{s}])-\sigma(\mu[\widehat{Y}_{s}])\big)\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}\Big\rangle\end{split}

(4.18)

for any
t\geq 0
a.s. We stress that the constant
\lambda_{\mathfrak{a}}>0
and the admissible weight function
\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}
depend only on the lifting basis
(\mu,M_{b},M_{\sigma})
and the constants
C_{b,\mathrm{Lip}},C_{\sigma,\mathrm{Lip}}>0
in
1
. By virtue of the a priori estimate (
4.11
), along with the facts that
\mu_{\sigma,\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}[\cdot]\in L(\mathcal{H};\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
\mu[\cdot]\in L(\mathcal{V};\mathbb{R}^{n})
, and the Lipschitz continuity of
\sigma:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
, the stochastic integral on the right-hand side of (
4.18
) is a martingale under the probability measure
\mathbb{P}
. Hence, taking the expectations on both sides of (
4.18
) with respect to
\mathbb{P}
, we obtain

\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\kappa t}\big\|Y_{t}-\widehat{Y}_{t}\big\|_{\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}^{2}+\lambda_{\mathfrak{a}}\int^{t}_{0}e^{\kappa s}\big|\mu_{\sigma,\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}\big[Y_{s}-\widehat{Y}_{s}\big]\big|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\right]\leq\big\|y_{1}-y_{2}\big\|_{\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}^{2}

(4.19)

for any
t\geq 0
.

For each
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, let
\sigma(x)^{\dagger}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times n}
be the pseudo-inverse of the matrix
\sigma(x)\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
. By
1
, we have
\sigma(x)^{\dagger}=\sigma(x)^{\top}(\sigma(x)\sigma(x)^{\top})^{-1}
,
\sigma(x)\sigma(x)^{\dagger}=I_{n\times n}
and
|\sigma(x)^{\dagger}|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq C_{\mathrm{UE}}^{1/2}
for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Define processes
u=u^{y_{1},y_{2}}
and
\mathscr{E}=\mathscr{E}^{y_{1},y_{2}}
by

u_{t}:=\lambda_{\mathfrak{a}}\sigma(\mu[\widehat{Y}_{t}])^{\dagger}\mu_{\sigma,\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}\big[Y_{t}-\widehat{Y}_{t}\big],\ \ t\geq 0,

and

\mathscr{E}_{t}:=\exp\left(-\int^{t}_{0}\langle u_{s},\mathrm{d}W_{s}\rangle-\frac{1}{2}\int^{t}_{0}|u_{s}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\right),\ \ t\geq 0.

Note that
u
is an
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-valued progressively measurable process such that
\int^{T}_{0}|u_{t}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty

\mathbb{P}
-a.s. for any
T>0
, and
\mathscr{E}
is a positive local martingale under
\mathbb{P}
. Now we show that the stochastic exponential
\mathscr{E}
is a martingale under the probability measure
\mathbb{P}
. To do so, it suffices to show that
\mathscr{E}
belongs to the class (DL), which means that the family of random variables
\{\mathscr{E}_{\tau}\,|\,\tau\in\mathcal{T}_{T}\}
is uniformly integrable with respect to
\mathbb{P}
for any
T>0
, where
\mathcal{T}_{T}
denotes the set of all stopping times
\tau
such that
\tau\leq T
a.s. Let
T>0
and
\tau\in\mathcal{T}_{T}
be fixed. For each
N\in\mathbb{N}
, define

\tau_{N}:=\tau\wedge\inf\left\{t\in[0,T]\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\int^{t}_{0}\big|\mu_{\sigma,\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}\big[Y_{s}-\widehat{Y}_{s}\big]\big|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\geq N\right\}.

Since
u\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[0,\tau_{N}]}
satisfies Novikov’s condition, the stopped process
\mathscr{E}_{\cdot\wedge\tau_{N}}
is a
\mathbb{P}
-martingale, and hence the measure
\widehat{\mathbb{P}}_{\tau_{N}}\sim\mathbb{P}
on
(\Omega,\mathcal{F})
defined by
\frac{\mathrm{d}\widehat{\mathbb{P}}_{\tau_{N}}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}:=\mathscr{E}_{\tau_{N}}
is a probability measure. By Girsanov’s theorem, the process

\widehat{W}^{\tau_{N}}_{t}:=W_{t}+\int^{t\wedge\tau_{N}}_{0}u_{s}\,\mathrm{d}s,\ \ t\in[0,T],

is a Brownian motion relative to
(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
under the probability measure
\widehat{\mathbb{P}}_{\tau_{N}}
. By (
4.18
), we have

\displaystyle e^{\kappa(t\wedge\tau_{N})}\big\|Y_{t\wedge\tau_{N}}-\widehat{Y}_{t\wedge\tau_{N}}\big\|_{\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}^{2}+\lambda_{\mathfrak{a}}\int^{t\wedge\tau_{N}}_{0}e^{\kappa s}\big|\mu_{\sigma,\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}\big[Y_{s}-\widehat{Y}_{s}\big]\big|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\big\|y_{1}-y_{2}\big\|_{\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}^{2}+2\int^{t\wedge\tau_{N}}_{0}e^{\kappa s}\Big\langle\mu_{\sigma,\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}\big[Y_{s}-\widehat{Y}_{s}\big],\big(\sigma(\mu[Y_{s}])-\sigma(\mu[\widehat{Y}_{s}])\big)\,\mathrm{d}\widehat{W}^{\tau_{N}}_{s}\Big\rangle

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt-2\lambda_{\mathfrak{a}}\int^{t\wedge\tau_{N}}_{0}e^{\kappa s}\Big\langle\mu_{\sigma,\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}\big[Y_{s}-\widehat{Y}_{s}\big],\big(\sigma(\mu[Y_{s}])-\sigma(\mu[\widehat{Y}_{s}])\big)\sigma(\mu[\widehat{Y}_{s}])^{\dagger}\mu_{\sigma,\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}\big[Y_{s}-\widehat{Y}_{s}\big]\Big\rangle\,\mathrm{d}s

for any
t\in[0,T]

\widehat{\mathbb{P}}_{\tau_{N}}
-a.s. Noting that
\|\sigma\|_{\mathrm{op},\infty}:=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\sigma(x)|_{\mathrm{op}}<\infty
by the (temporal) assumption in this lemma, we see that the stopped stochastic integral appearing in the right-hand side above is a martingale under the probability measure
\widehat{\mathbb{P}}_{\tau_{N}}
. Furthermore, we have

\displaystyle\left|\Big\langle\mu_{\sigma,\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}\big[Y_{s}-\widehat{Y}_{s}\big],\big(\sigma(\mu[Y_{s}])-\sigma(\mu[\widehat{Y}_{s}])\big)\sigma(\mu[\widehat{Y}_{s}])^{\dagger}\mu_{\sigma,\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}\big[Y_{s}-\widehat{Y}_{s}\big]\Big\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\leq 2\|\sigma\|_{\mathrm{op},\infty}C_{\mathrm{UE}}^{1/2}\big|\mu_{\sigma,\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}\big[Y_{s}-\widehat{Y}_{s}\big]\big|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\|\sigma\|_{\mathrm{op},\infty}C_{\mathrm{UE}}^{1/2}\int_{[0,\infty)}\big|M_{\sigma}(\theta)^{\top}\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}(\theta)^{1/2}\big|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)\big\|Y_{s}-\widehat{Y}_{s}\big\|^{2}_{\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}},

where we used the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality in the last line. Note that
\int_{[0,\infty)}|M_{\sigma}(\theta)^{\top}\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}(\theta)^{1/2}|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)<\infty
, which follows from the integrability condition (
2.8
) on
M_{\sigma}
and the bound (
2.32
) for the admissible weight function
\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}
. Hence, denoting by
\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\tau_{N}}[\cdot]
the expectation under
\widehat{\mathbb{P}}_{\tau_{N}}
, we have

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\tau_{N}}\left[e^{\kappa(t\wedge\tau_{N})}\big\|Y_{t\wedge\tau_{N}}-\widehat{Y}_{t\wedge\tau_{N}}\big\|_{\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}^{2}+\lambda_{\mathfrak{a}}\int^{t\wedge\tau_{N}}_{0}e^{\kappa s}\big|\mu_{\sigma,\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}\big[Y_{s}-\widehat{Y}_{s}\big]\big|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\right]

\displaystyle\leq\big\|y_{1}-y_{2}\big\|_{\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}^{2}+C\int^{t}_{0}\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\tau_{N}}\Big[e^{\kappa(s\wedge\tau_{N})}\big\|Y_{s\wedge\tau_{N}}-\widehat{Y}_{s\wedge\tau_{N}}\big\|_{\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}^{2}\Big]\,\mathrm{d}s

for any
t\in[0,T]
, where

C:=4\lambda_{\mathfrak{a}}\|\sigma\|_{\mathrm{op},\infty}C_{\mathrm{UE}}^{1/2}\int_{[0,\infty)}\big|M_{\sigma}(\theta)^{\top}\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}(\theta)^{1/2}\big|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)<\infty.

By Gronwall’s inequality, we get

\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\tau_{N}}\left[e^{\kappa\tau_{N}}\big\|Y_{\tau_{N}}-\widehat{Y}_{\tau_{N}}\big\|_{\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}^{2}+\lambda_{\mathfrak{a}}\int^{\tau_{N}}_{0}e^{\kappa t}\big|\mu_{\sigma,\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}\big[Y_{t}-\widehat{Y}_{t}\big]\big|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\right]\leq e^{CT}\big\|y_{1}-y_{2}\big\|_{\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}^{2}.

This estimate yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\mathscr{E}_{\tau_{N}}\log\mathscr{E}_{\tau_{N}}\big]

\displaystyle=\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\tau_{N}}\left[-\int^{\tau_{N}}_{0}\big\langle u_{t},\mathrm{d}\widehat{W}^{\tau_{N}}_{t}\big\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\int^{\tau_{N}}_{0}|u_{t}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{\mathrm{UE}}\lambda_{\mathfrak{a}}^{2}}{2}\widehat{\mathbb{E}}_{\tau_{N}}\left[\int^{\tau_{N}}_{0}\big|\mu_{\sigma,\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}\big[Y_{t}-\widehat{Y}_{t}\big]\big|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{\mathrm{UE}}\lambda_{\mathfrak{a}}}{2}e^{CT}\big\|y_{1}-y_{2}\big\|_{\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}^{2}.

Letting
N\to\infty
and utilizing Fatou’s lemma, we obtain

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathscr{E}_{\tau}\log\mathscr{E}_{\tau}\big]\leq\frac{C_{\mathrm{UE}}\lambda_{\mathfrak{a}}}{2}e^{CT}\big\|y_{1}-y_{2}\big\|_{\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}^{2}.

This bound holds for any
\tau\in\mathcal{T}_{T}
, and hence the family
\{\mathscr{E}_{\tau}\,|\,\tau\in\mathcal{T}_{T}\}
is uniformly integrable under
\mathbb{P}
. Since
T>0
is arbitrary, we see that the local martingale
\mathscr{E}
belongs to the class (DL). Thus,
\mathscr{E}
is a martingale under the probability measure
\mathbb{P}
.

For each
t\geq 0
, define a measure
\widehat{\mathbb{P}}_{t}=\widehat{\mathbb{P}}^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t}\sim\mathbb{P}
on
(\Omega,\mathcal{F})
by
\frac{\mathrm{d}\widehat{\mathbb{P}}_{t}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}:=\mathscr{E}_{t}
. The martingale property of the stochastic exponential
\mathscr{E}
under
\mathbb{P}
implies that
\widehat{\mathbb{P}}_{t}
is a probability measure. Therefore, by Girsanov’s theorem, the process

\widehat{W}_{s}:=W_{s}+\int^{s}_{0}u_{r}\,\mathrm{d}r,\ \ s\in[0,t],

is a Brownian motion relative to
(\mathcal{F}_{s})_{s\in[0,t]}
under
\widehat{\mathbb{P}}_{t}
. Note that the process
(\widehat{Y}_{s})_{s\in[0,t]}
solves the following SEE on
(\Omega,\mathcal{F}_{t},\widehat{\mathbb{P}}_{t})
driven by the Brownian motion
\widehat{W}
:

\begin{cases}\mathrm{d}\widehat{Y}_{s}(\theta)=-\theta\widehat{Y}_{s}(\theta)\,\mathrm{d}s+M_{b}(\theta)b(\mu[\widehat{Y}_{s}])\,\mathrm{d}s+M_{\sigma}(\theta)\sigma(\mu[\widehat{Y}_{s}])\,\mathrm{d}\widehat{W}_{s},\ \ s\in[0,t],\ \theta\in[0,\infty),\\
\widehat{Y}_{0}(\theta)=y_{2}(\theta),\ \ \theta\in[0,\infty).\end{cases}

Hence, by the uniqueness in law for the SEE (
1.2
), which is ensured by the pathwise uniqueness and a Yamada–Watanabe-type result for general SEEs (see, e.g.,
[
47
, Appendix E]
), we obtain
\mathrm{Law}_{\widehat{\mathbb{P}}_{t}}(\widehat{Y}_{t})=P_{t}(y_{2},\cdot)
, proving (
4.7
). Furthermore,

\displaystyle D_{\mathrm{KL}}\big(\mathbb{P}\big\|\widehat{\mathbb{P}}_{t}\big)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\log\frac{1}{\mathscr{E}_{t}}\right]=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{t}_{0}|u_{s}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{\mathrm{UE}}\lambda_{\mathfrak{a}}^{2}}{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{t}_{0}\big|\mu_{\sigma,\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}\big[Y_{s}-\widehat{Y}_{s}\big]\big|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{\mathrm{UE}}\lambda_{\mathfrak{a}}}{2}\big\|y_{1}-y_{2}\big\|_{\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}^{2},

where the last inequality follows from (
4.19
). On the other hand, another application of (
4.19
) shows that

\mathbb{E}\Big[\big\|Y_{t}-\widehat{Y}_{t}\big\|_{\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}\Big]\leq\mathbb{E}\Big[\big\|Y_{t}-\widehat{Y}_{t}\big\|_{\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}^{2}\Big]^{1/2}\leq e^{-\kappa t/2}\big\|y_{1}-y_{2}\big\|_{\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}.

Now we define an admissible weight function
\Phi:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
by
\Phi:=C_{\mathrm{UE}}\lambda_{\mathfrak{a}}\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}
, which depends only on the lifting basis
(\mu,M_{b},M_{\sigma})
and the constants
C_{b,\mathrm{Lip}}
,
C_{\sigma,\mathrm{Lip}}
and
C_{\mathrm{UE}}
in
1
. Noting that
C_{\mathrm{UE}}\lambda_{\mathfrak{a}}\|\cdot\|_{\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}^{2}=\|\cdot\|_{\Phi}^{2}
, we obtain the required estimates (
4.8
) and (
4.9
). This completes the proof.
∎

Now we can prove
3.2
.

Proof of
3.2
.

By
4.1
and its subsequent discussion, without loss of generality, we may assume that
\sigma:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
is bounded. Let
\Phi
,
\{(Y^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t},\widehat{Y}^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t})\}_{t\geq 0,y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathcal{H}}
and
\{\widehat{\mathbb{P}}^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0,y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathcal{H}}
be as constructed in
4.2
. Note that the admissible weight function
\Phi
depends only on the lifting basis
(\mu,M_{b},M_{\sigma})
and the constants
C_{b,\mathrm{Lip}}
,
C_{\sigma,\mathrm{Lip}}
and
C_{\mathrm{UE}}
, while remaining independent of the bound of
\sigma
.

For each
t\geq 0
and
y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathcal{H}
, by (
4.7
), we have

d_{\mathrm{TV}}\big(\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}\big(\widehat{Y}^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t}\big),P_{t}(y_{2},\cdot)\big)=d_{\mathrm{TV}}\big(\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}\big(\widehat{Y}^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t}\big),\mathrm{Law}_{\widehat{\mathbb{P}}^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t}}\big(\widehat{Y}^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t}\big)\big)\leq d_{\mathrm{TV}}\big(\mathbb{P},\widehat{\mathbb{P}}^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t}\big).

(4.20)

However, the well-known inequalities for the total variation distance (see, e.g.,
[
59
, Lemma 2.5 and Equation (2.25)]
) yield

d_{\mathrm{TV}}\big(\mathbb{P},\widehat{\mathbb{P}}^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t}\big)\leq\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}D_{\mathrm{KL}}\big(\mathbb{P}\big\|\widehat{\mathbb{P}}^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t}\big)}

and

d_{\mathrm{TV}}\big(\mathbb{P},\widehat{\mathbb{P}}^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t}\big)\leq 1-\frac{1}{2}\exp\left(-D_{\mathrm{KL}}\big(\mathbb{P}\big\|\widehat{\mathbb{P}}^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t}\big)\right).

Applying these inequalities to (
4.20
) and utilizing the estimate (
4.9
), we get

d_{\mathrm{TV}}\big(\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}\big(\widehat{Y}^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t}\big),P_{t}(y_{2},\cdot)\big)\leq\frac{1}{2}\|y_{1}-y_{2}\|_{\Phi}

(4.21)

and

d_{\mathrm{TV}}\big(\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}\big(\widehat{Y}^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t}\big),P_{t}(y_{2},\cdot)\big)\leq 1-\frac{1}{2}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\|y_{1}-y_{2}\|_{\Phi}^{2}\right)

(4.22)

for any
t\geq 0
and
y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathcal{H}
. Assertions (i) and (ii) now follow from (
4.6
), (
4.8
), (
4.21
), and (
4.22
) by
[
12
, Theorem 2.4]
. For the sake of completeness, we provide the details of their proofs below.

For each
t\geq 0
and
y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathcal{H}
, by the “coupling lemma” (see, e.g.,
[
61
, Theorem 4.1]
), there exists an
\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{H}
-valued random variable
(\widehat{\mathscr{Y}}^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t},\mathscr{Y}^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t})
on a probability space
(\Omega^{\prime},\mathcal{F}^{\prime},\mathbb{P}^{\prime})
such that
\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{\prime}}(\widehat{\mathscr{Y}}^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t})=\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(\widehat{Y}^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t})
,
\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{\prime}}(\mathscr{Y}^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t})=P_{t}(y_{2},\cdot)
, and

\mathbb{P}^{\prime}\big(\widehat{\mathscr{Y}}^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t}\neq\mathscr{Y}^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t}\big)=d_{\mathrm{TV}}\big(\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}\big(\widehat{Y}^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t}\big),P_{t}(y_{2},\cdot)\big).

Furthermore, by the “gluing lemma” (see, e.g.,
[
42
, Lemma 4.3.2]
), we can construct an
\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{H}
-valued random variable
(\xi^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t},\eta^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t},\zeta^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t})
on a probability space
(\Omega^{\prime\prime},\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime},\mathbb{P}^{\prime\prime})
such that
\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{\prime\prime}}(\xi^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t},\eta^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t})=\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(Y^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t},\widehat{Y}^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t})
and
\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{\prime\prime}}(\eta^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t},\zeta^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t})=\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{\prime}}(\widehat{\mathscr{Y}}^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t},\mathscr{Y}^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t})
. Note that

\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{\prime\prime}}(\xi^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t})=\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(Y^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t})=P_{t}(y_{1},\cdot)

by (
4.6
), and

\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{\prime\prime}}(\zeta^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t})=\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{\prime}}(\mathscr{Y}^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t})=P_{t}(y_{2},\cdot).

Hence, the joint distribution
\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}^{\prime\prime}}(\xi^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t},\zeta^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t})\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{H})
is a (true) coupling between the two probability measures
P_{t}(y_{1},\cdot)\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})
and
P_{t}(y_{2},\cdot)\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})
. Furthermore, we have

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\prime\prime}}\big[\big\|\xi^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t}-\eta^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t}\big\|_{\Phi}\big]=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}\big[\big\|Y^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t}-\widehat{Y}^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t}\big\|_{\Phi}\big]

and

\mathbb{P}^{\prime\prime}\big(\eta^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t}\neq\zeta^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t}\big)=\mathbb{P}^{\prime}\big(\widehat{\mathscr{Y}}^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t}\neq\mathscr{Y}^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t}\big)=d_{\mathrm{TV}}\big(\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}\big(\widehat{Y}^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t}\big),P_{t}(y_{2},\cdot)\big).

Recalling the definition of the metric
d_{\Phi}(y_{1},y_{2}):=\|y_{1}-y_{2}\|_{\Phi}\wedge 1
, and utilizing the above properties of
(\xi^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t},\eta^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t},\zeta^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t})
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{W}_{d_{\Phi}}(P_{t}(y_{1},\cdot),P_{t}(y_{2},\cdot))

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\prime\prime}}\big[d_{\Phi}(\xi^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t},\zeta^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t})\big]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\prime\prime}}\big[\big\|\xi^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t}-\eta^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t}\big\|_{\Phi}\big]+\mathbb{P}^{\prime\prime}\big(\eta^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t}\neq\zeta^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t}\big)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}\big[\big\|Y^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t}-\widehat{Y}^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t}\big\|_{\Phi}\big]+d_{\mathrm{TV}}\big(\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}\big(\widehat{Y}^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t}\big),P_{t}(y_{2},\cdot)\big).

Together with (
4.8
) and (
4.21
), this implies that

\mathbb{W}_{d_{\Phi}}(P_{t}(y_{1},\cdot),P_{t}(y_{2},\cdot))\leq\left(e^{-\kappa t/2}+\frac{1}{2}\right)\|y_{1}-y_{2}\|_{\Phi}

(4.23)

for all
t\geq 0
and all
y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathcal{H}
. Furthermore, using (
4.22
) instead of (
4.21
), we obtain

\mathbb{W}_{d_{\Phi}}(P_{t}(y_{1},\cdot),P_{t}(y_{2},\cdot))\leq e^{-\kappa t/2}\|y_{1}-y_{2}\|_{\Phi}+1-\frac{1}{2}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\|y_{1}-y_{2}\|_{\Phi}^{2}\right)

(4.24)

for all
t\geq 0
and all
y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathcal{H}
.

On the one hand, if
t\geq\frac{4\log 2}{\kappa}
and
d_{\Phi}(y_{1},y_{2})<1
, then the estimate (
4.23
), combined with
e^{-\kappa t/2}+\frac{1}{2}\leq\frac{3}{4}
and
\|y_{1}-y_{2}\|_{\Phi}=d_{\Phi}(y_{1},y_{2})
, yields (
3.1
). This shows that the distance function
d_{\Phi}
is contracting for
P_{t}
, thereby establishing assertion (i). On the other hand, for any
R>0
, if
t\geq\frac{4R^{2}+2\log(8R)}{\kappa}
and
y_{1},y_{2}\in\overline{B}_{\Phi}(R):=\{y\in\mathcal{H}\,|\,\|y\|_{\Phi}\leq R\}
, then the estimate (
4.24
), together with
\|y_{1}-y_{2}\|_{\Phi}\leq 2R
and
e^{-\kappa t/2}2R+1-\frac{1}{2}e^{-2R^{2}}\leq 1-\frac{1}{4}e^{-2R^{2}}
, implies the estimate (
3.2
). This indicates that the set
\overline{B}_{\Phi}(R)
is
d_{\Phi}
-small for
P_{t}
, proving assertion (ii).

It remains to prove assertion (iii). Let
t\geq 0
and
y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathcal{H}
be fixed, and let
f:\mathcal{H}\to[1,\infty)
be a bounded Borel measurable function with
\|\nabla_{\Phi}\log f\|_{\infty}<\infty
. By (
4.6
) and the Lipschitz continuity of
\log f
, we have

P_{t}\log f(y_{1})=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}\big[\log f(Y^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t})\big]\leq\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}\big[\log f\big(\widehat{Y}^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t}\big)\big]+\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}\big[\|Y^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t}-\widehat{Y}^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t}\|_{\Phi}\big]\|\nabla_{\Phi}\log f\|_{\infty}.

Furthermore, by Jensen’s inequality and (
4.7
), we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}\big[\log f\big(\widehat{Y}^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t}\big)\big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}\left[\log\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\widehat{\mathbb{P}}^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}f(\widehat{Y}^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t})\right)\right]+\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}\left[\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}{\mathrm{d}\widehat{\mathbb{P}}^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\log\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}\left[\frac{\mathrm{d}\widehat{\mathbb{P}}^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}f\big(\widehat{Y}^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t}\big)\right]+\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}\left[\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}{\mathrm{d}\widehat{\mathbb{P}}^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t}}\right]

\displaystyle=\log P_{t}f(y_{2})+D_{\mathrm{KL}}\big(\mathbb{P}\big\|\widehat{\mathbb{P}}^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t}\big).

Hence, we obtain

P_{t}\log f(y_{1})\leq\log P_{t}f(y_{2})+D_{\mathrm{KL}}\big(\mathbb{P}\big\|\widehat{\mathbb{P}}^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t}\big)+\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}\big[\|Y_{t}-\widehat{Y}^{y_{1},y_{2}}_{t}\|_{\Phi}\big]\|\nabla_{\Phi}\log f\|_{\infty}.

Combined with (
4.8
) and (
4.9
), this estimate yields the desired asymptotic log-Harnack inequality (
3.3
) in assertion (iii). Finally, according to
[
4
, Theorem 2.1]
, the asymptotic log-Harnack inequality ensures that an invariant probability measure for
\{P_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
, if it exists, must be unique. This completes the proof.
∎

4.2
Proofs of
3.4
and
3.6
: Construction of a Lyapunov function

Our next purpose is to construct a Lyapunov function for the SEE (
1.2
). First, we prove
3.4
under the abstract condition in
2
.

Proof of
3.4
.

Let
y\in\mathcal{H}
be fixed, and let
(Y,W,\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
be a weak solution of the SEE (
1.2
) with initial condition
y
, the existence and uniqueness in law of which are assumed in this theorem. Note that
P_{t}(y,\cdot)=\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(Y_{t})
for any
t\geq 0
. Let
\Psi:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
,
\delta\in(0,1)
,
\rho>0
and
C_{\mathrm{Lyap}}>0
be as in
2
. By applying Itô’s formula for the squared (equivalent) norm
\|\cdot\|_{\Psi}^{2}
in
2.19
, and utilizing the formulas (
2.33
), (
2.34
), (
2.35
), and (
2.36
), we obtain

\begin{split}\|Y_{t}\|_{\Psi}^{2}+2\int^{t}_{0}\mathopen{|\mkern-1.5mu|\mkern-1.5mu|}Y_{s}\mathclose{|\mkern-1.5mu|\mkern-1.5mu|}_{\Psi}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s&=\|y\|_{\Psi}^{2}+2\int^{t}_{0}\left\{\big\langle b(\mu[Y_{s}]),\mu_{b,\Psi}[Y_{s}]\big\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}\big[\sigma(\mu[Y_{s}])^{\top}Q_{\sigma,\Psi}\sigma(\mu[Y_{s}])\big]\right\}\,\mathrm{d}s\\
&\hskip 28.45274pt+2\int^{t}_{0}\big\langle\mu_{\sigma,\Psi}[Y_{s}],\sigma(\mu[Y_{s}])\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}\big\rangle\end{split}

(4.25)

for any
t\geq 0
a.s. Hence, by the condition (
3.4
) in
2
, we see that

\|Y_{t}\|_{\Psi}^{2}+2\int^{t}_{0}\Big\{\rho\|Y_{s}\|_{\Psi}^{2}+(1-\delta)\mathopen{|\mkern-1.5mu|\mkern-1.5mu|}Y_{s}\mathclose{|\mkern-1.5mu|\mkern-1.5mu|}_{\Psi}^{2}\Big\}\,\mathrm{d}s\leq\|y\|_{\Psi}^{2}+2C_{\mathrm{Lyap}}t+2\int^{t}_{0}\big\langle\mu_{\sigma,\Psi}[Y_{s}],\sigma(\mu[Y_{s}])\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}\big\rangle

(4.26)

for any
t\geq 0
a.s. We emphasize that
\rho>0
and
1-\delta>0
by the assumption. For each
N\in\mathbb{N}
, define a stopping time
\tau_{N}
by

\tau_{N}:=\inf\left\{t\geq 0\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\int^{t}_{0}\big|\sigma(\mu[Y_{s}])^{\top}\mu_{\sigma,\Psi}[Y_{s}]\big|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\geq N\right\}.

Note that
\lim_{N\to\infty}\tau_{N}=\infty
a.s. Clearly, the stochastic integral on the right-hand side of (
4.26
) stopped at
\tau_{N}
is a martingale. Hence, by taking the expectations on both sides of (
4.26
) with
t
replaced by
t\wedge\tau_{N}
, we obtain

\mathbb{E}\left[\|Y_{t\wedge\tau_{N}}\|_{\Psi}^{2}+2\int^{t\wedge\tau_{N}}_{0}\Big\{\rho\|Y_{s}\|_{\Psi}^{2}+(1-\delta)\mathopen{|\mkern-1.5mu|\mkern-1.5mu|}Y_{s}\mathclose{|\mkern-1.5mu|\mkern-1.5mu|}_{\Psi}^{2}\Big\}\,\mathrm{d}s\right]\leq\|y\|_{\Psi}^{2}+2C_{\mathrm{Lyap}}t.

Taking the limit
N\to\infty
and utilizing Fatou’s lemma, we obtain

\mathbb{E}\left[\|Y_{t}\|_{\Psi}^{2}+2\int^{t}_{0}\Big\{\rho\|Y_{s}\|_{\Psi}^{2}+(1-\delta)\mathopen{|\mkern-1.5mu|\mkern-1.5mu|}Y_{s}\mathclose{|\mkern-1.5mu|\mkern-1.5mu|}_{\Psi}^{2}\Big\}\,\mathrm{d}s\right]\leq\|y\|_{\Psi}^{2}+2C_{\mathrm{Lyap}}t

(4.27)

for any
t\geq 0
. On the other hand, applying Itô’s formula to
e^{2\rho t}\|Y_{t}\|_{\Psi}^{2}
and utilizing (
4.25
), (
3.4
), along with a stopping time argument similar to the one above, shows that

\mathbb{E}\left[e^{2\rho t}\|Y_{t}\|_{\Psi}^{2}+2(1-\delta)\int^{t}_{0}e^{2\rho s}\mathopen{|\mkern-1.5mu|\mkern-1.5mu|}Y_{s}\mathclose{|\mkern-1.5mu|\mkern-1.5mu|}_{\Psi}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\right]\leq\|y\|_{\Psi}^{2}+\frac{C_{\mathrm{Lyap}}(e^{2\rho t}-1)}{\rho}

(4.28)

for any
t\geq 0
.

The estimate (
4.28
) in particular implies that

\int_{\mathcal{H}}\|y^{\prime}\|_{\Psi}^{2}\,P_{t}(y,\mathrm{d}y^{\prime})\leq e^{-2\rho t}\|y\|_{\Psi}^{2}+\frac{C_{\mathrm{Lyap}}}{\rho}

for all
t\geq 0
and all
y\in\mathcal{H}
. Hence, the function
V=\|\cdot\|_{\Psi}^{2}
is a Lyapunov function for
\{P_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
.

Define a lower semi-continuous function
U:\mathcal{H}\to[0,\infty]
by
U(y):=\rho\|y\|_{\Psi}^{2}+(1-\delta)\mathopen{|\mkern-1.5mu|\mkern-1.5mu|}y\mathclose{|\mkern-1.5mu|\mkern-1.5mu|}_{\Psi}^{2}
for
y\in\mathcal{H}
. For each
t>0
, define a probability kernel
\nu_{t}:\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})\to[0,1]
by
\nu_{t}(y,A):=\frac{1}{t}\int^{t}_{0}P_{s}(y,A)\,\mathrm{d}s
for each
y\in\mathcal{H}
and
A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
; this probability kernel is well-defined thanks to the measurability assumption for the Markov semigroup
\{P_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
. Then, by the estimate (
4.27
), we have

\int_{\mathcal{H}}U(y^{\prime})\,\nu_{t}(y,\mathrm{d}y^{\prime})\leq\frac{\|y\|_{\Psi}^{2}}{2t}+C_{\mathrm{Lyap}}

(4.29)

for any
y\in\mathcal{H}
and
t>0
. Suppose that we are given an invariant probability measure
\pi\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})
for
\{P_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
. Note that
\pi
is invariant with respect to
\nu_{t}
for any
t>0
. Hence, using the estimate (
4.29
), for any
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
t>0
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{H}}\big\{U(y)\wedge N\big\}\,\pi(\mathrm{d}y)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{H}}\int_{\mathcal{H}}\big\{U(y^{\prime})\wedge N\big\}\,\nu_{t}(y,\mathrm{d}y^{\prime})\,\pi(\mathrm{d}y)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathcal{H}}\left\{\int_{\mathcal{H}}U(y^{\prime})\,\nu_{t}(y,\mathrm{d}y^{\prime})\,\wedge N\right\}\,\pi(\mathrm{d}y)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathcal{H}}\left\{\frac{\|y\|_{\Psi}^{2}}{2t}\wedge N\right\}\,\pi(\mathrm{d}y)+C_{\mathrm{Lyap}}.

The dominated convergence theorem ensures that the integral in the last line above tends to zero as
t\to\infty
for each fixed
N\in\mathbb{N}
. Hence, by taking the limit as
t\to\infty
, subsequently letting
N\to\infty
, and applying the monotone convergence theorem, we obtain
\int_{\mathcal{H}}U(y)\,\pi(\mathrm{d}y)\leq C_{\mathrm{Lyap}}
. This demonstrates that the estimate (
3.5
) holds. Since
y\mapsto(\rho\|y\|_{\Psi}^{2}+(1-\delta)\mathopen{|\mkern-1.5mu|\mkern-1.5mu|}y\mathclose{|\mkern-1.5mu|\mkern-1.5mu|}_{\Psi}^{2})^{1/2}
defines a norm on
\mathcal{V}
equivalent to
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{V}}
, the estimate (
3.5
) ensures that
\pi(\mathcal{V})=1
and
\int_{\mathcal{V}}\|y\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\,\pi(\mathrm{d}y)<\infty
. This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 4.4
.

Since the map
y\mapsto(\rho\|y\|_{\Psi}^{2}+(1-\delta)\mathopen{|\mkern-1.5mu|\mkern-1.5mu|}y\mathclose{|\mkern-1.5mu|\mkern-1.5mu|}_{\Psi}^{2})^{1/2}
defines a norm on
\mathcal{V}
equivalent to
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{V}}
, the estimate (
4.29
), together with Markov’s inequality, implies that

\lim_{R\to\infty}\sup_{t\geq 1}\nu_{t}\big(y,\mathcal{H}\setminus\overline{B}_{\mathcal{V}}(R)\big)=0\ \ \text{for any $y\in\mathcal{H}$},

(4.30)

where
\overline{B}_{\mathcal{V}}(R):=\{y\in\mathcal{V}\,|\,\|y\|_{\mathcal{V}}\leq R\}
. By
A.1
, if
{\mathrm{supp}\,\mu}\cap[0,m]
is a finite set for each
m>0
, then the embedding
\mathcal{V}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{H}
is compact, and hence the set
\overline{B}_{\mathcal{V}}(R)
is a compact subset of
\mathcal{H}
for any
R>0
. Therefore, in this case, (
4.30
) implies that the family of probability measures
\{\nu_{t}(y,\cdot)\}_{t\geq 1}
is tight on
\mathcal{H}
for any
y\in\mathcal{H}
, and hence (assuming that
\{P_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
satisfies the Feller property) the Krylov–Bogoliubov theorem (see, e.g.,
[
15
, Corollary 11.8]
) ensures the existence of an invariant probability measure
\pi\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})
for the Markov semigroup
\{P_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
. However, if
{\mathrm{supp}\,\mu}\cap[0,m]
is an infinite set for some
m>0
, which is a typical case we are interested in, then by
A.1
the embedding
\mathcal{V}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{H}
is not compact, and hence (
4.30
) does no longer result in the tightness of
\{\nu_{t}(y,\cdot)\}_{t\geq 1}
. In this case, the estimate (
4.29
) alone is not sufficient to conclude the existence of an invariant probability measure. See also the discussions in
3.5
and
A.2
.

Next, we prove
3.6
, which provides a verifiable sufficient condition for
2
. As in the proof of
4.2
, the “change-of-norm” technique plays a crucial role. A key idea is to construct an admissible weight function
\Psi_{m}
depending on a parameter
m>0
such that the operator
\mu_{b,\Psi_{m}}[\cdot]
approximates the integral operator
\mu[\cdot]
appearing in the SEE (
1.2
) in some sense. This strategy parallels that used in the proof of
4.2
, where an admissible weight function
\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}
was constructed to approximate
\mu[\cdot]
via the operator
\mu_{\sigma,\Phi_{\mathfrak{a}}}[\cdot]
.

Proof of
3.6
.

Assume that the lifting basis
(\mu,M_{b},M_{\sigma})
and the maps
b:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\sigma:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
satisfy the conditions in
3.6
. Fix a constant
m>\kappa:=\inf{\mathrm{supp}\,\mu}>0
, which will be determined later. Define

\Psi_{m}(\theta):=\left(m^{-1}\theta^{1/2}I_{n\times n}+M_{b}(\theta)\right)^{-1}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{A_{m}}(\theta)+\theta^{-1/2}I_{n\times n}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[\kappa,\infty)\setminus A_{m}}(\theta),\ \ \theta\in[0,\infty),

where

A_{m}:=\left\{\theta\in[\kappa,m)\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\text{$M_{b}(\theta)$ is symmetric, nonnegative definite and satisfies $|M_{b}(\theta)|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq m$}\right\}.

Analogously to the proof of
4.2
, we can show that
\Psi_{m}:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is an admissible weight function. Furthermore, we define a bounded linear operator
\overline{\mu}_{b,\Psi_{m}}[\cdot]:\mathcal{V}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
by

\overline{\mu}_{b,\Psi_{m}}[y]:=\mu[y]-\mu_{b,\Psi_{m}}[y]=\int_{[0,\infty)}\big(I_{n\times n}-M_{b}(\theta)^{\top}\Psi_{m}(\theta)\big)y(\theta)\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)

for
y\in\mathcal{V}
.

Let
y\in\mathcal{V}
be fixed. By the assumption, we have

\displaystyle\big\langle b(\mu[y]),\mu_{b,\Psi_{m}}[y]\big\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}\big[\sigma(\mu[y])^{\top}Q_{\sigma,\Psi_{m}}\sigma(\mu[y])\big]

\displaystyle=\big\langle b(\mu[y]),\mu[y]\big\rangle-\big\langle b(\mu[y]),\overline{\mu}_{b,\Psi_{m}}[y]\big\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}\big[\sigma(\mu[y])^{\top}Q_{\sigma,\Psi_{m}}\sigma(\mu[y])\big]

\displaystyle\leq\gamma|\mu[y]|^{2}+C_{b,\mathrm{LG}}^{\prime}+C_{b,\mathrm{LG}}\big(1+|\mu[y]|\big)\big|\overline{\mu}_{b,\Psi_{m}}[y]\big|+\frac{1}{2}C_{\sigma,\mathrm{subLG}}^{2}|Q_{\sigma,\Psi_{m}}|_{\mathrm{op}}\big(1+|\mu[y]|^{p}\big)^{2}.

Since
p\in(0,1)
, Young’s inequality yields that, for any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
,

\big\langle b(\mu[y]),\mu_{b,\Psi_{m}}[y]\big\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}\big[\sigma(\mu[y])^{\top}Q_{\sigma,\Psi_{m}}\sigma(\mu[y])\big]\leq(\gamma+\varepsilon)|\mu[y]|^{2}+\frac{C_{b,\mathrm{LG}}^{2}}{\varepsilon}\big|\overline{\mu}_{b,\Psi_{m}}[y]\big|^{2}+C_{m,\varepsilon},

(4.31)

where
C_{m,\varepsilon}>0
is a constant which depends only on
|Q_{\sigma,\Psi_{m}}|_{\mathrm{op}}
,
\varepsilon
,
C_{b,\mathrm{LG}}^{\prime}
,
C_{\sigma,\mathrm{subLG}}
and
p
. Now we estimate
|\mu[y]|^{2}
and
|\overline{\mu}_{b,\Psi_{m}}[y]|^{2}
in terms of
\mathopen{|\mkern-1.5mu|\mkern-1.5mu|}y\mathclose{|\mkern-1.5mu|\mkern-1.5mu|}_{\Psi_{m}}^{2}
. First, recalling the assumption that
\kappa:=\inf{\mathrm{supp}\,\mu}>0
, the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality and the definition of the admissible weight function
\Psi_{m}
yield

\displaystyle|\mu[y]|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\{\int_{[\kappa,\infty)}\theta^{-1}\big|\Psi_{m}(\theta)^{-1/2}\big|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)\right\}\left\{\int_{[0,\infty)}\theta\big|\Psi_{m}(\theta)^{1/2}y(\theta)\big|^{2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{m^{-1}\int_{A_{m}}\theta^{-1/2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)+\int_{A_{m}}\theta^{-1}|M_{b}(\theta)|_{\mathrm{op}}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)+\int_{[\kappa,\infty)\setminus A_{m}}\theta^{-1/2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)\right\}\mathopen{|\mkern-1.5mu|\mkern-1.5mu|}y\mathclose{|\mkern-1.5mu|\mkern-1.5mu|}_{\Psi_{m}}^{2}.

(4.32)

Second, noting that

\displaystyle\left|\big(I_{n\times n}-M_{b}(\theta)^{\top}\Psi_{m}(\theta)\big)\Psi_{m}(\theta)^{-1/2}\right|^{2}_{\mathrm{op}}

\displaystyle=\left|\left(I_{n\times n}-M_{b}(\theta)\left(m^{-1}\theta^{1/2}I_{n\times n}+M_{b}(\theta)\right)^{-1}\right)\left(m^{-1}\theta^{1/2}I_{n\times n}+M_{b}(\theta)\right)^{1/2}\right|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{A_{m}}(\theta)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\theta^{1/2}\big|I_{n\times n}-\theta^{-1/2}M_{b}(\theta)\big|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[\kappa,\infty)\setminus A_{m}}(\theta)

\displaystyle\leq m^{-1}\theta^{1/2}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{A_{m}}(\theta)+2\theta^{1/2}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[\kappa,\infty)\setminus A_{m}}(\theta)+2\theta^{-1/2}|M_{b}(\theta)|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{[\kappa,\infty)\setminus A_{m}}(\theta)

for any
\theta\geq\kappa:=\inf{\mathrm{supp}\,\mu}>0
, we have

\displaystyle\big|\overline{\mu}_{b,\Psi_{m}}[y]\big|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\{\int_{[\kappa,\infty)}\theta^{-1}\left|\big(I_{n\times n}-M_{b}(\theta)^{\top}\Psi_{m}(\theta)\big)\Psi_{m}(\theta)^{-1/2}\right|^{2}_{\mathrm{op}}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)\right\}\left\{\int_{[0,\infty)}\theta\big|\Psi_{m}(\theta)^{1/2}y(\theta)\big|^{2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\left\{m^{-1}\int_{A_{m}}\theta^{-1/2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)+2\int_{[\kappa,\infty)\setminus A_{m}}\theta^{-1/2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)+2\int_{[\kappa,\infty)\setminus A_{m}}\theta^{-3/2}|M_{b}(\theta)|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)\right\}\mathopen{|\mkern-1.5mu|\mkern-1.5mu|}y\mathclose{|\mkern-1.5mu|\mkern-1.5mu|}_{\Psi_{m}}^{2}.

(4.33)

Combining (
4.31
), (
4.32
) and (
4.33
), we get

\big\langle b(\mu[y]),\mu_{b,\Psi_{m}}[y]\big\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}\big[\sigma(\mu[y])^{\top}Q_{\sigma,\Psi_{m}}\sigma(\mu[y])\big]\leq\delta_{m,\varepsilon}\mathopen{|\mkern-1.5mu|\mkern-1.5mu|}y\mathclose{|\mkern-1.5mu|\mkern-1.5mu|}_{\Psi_{m}}^{2}+C_{m,\varepsilon},

where the constant
\delta_{m,\varepsilon}>0
is defined by

\displaystyle\delta_{m,\varepsilon}

\displaystyle:=(\gamma+\varepsilon)\left\{m^{-1}\int_{A_{m}}\theta^{-1/2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)+\int_{A_{m}}\theta^{-1}|M_{b}(\theta)|_{\mathrm{op}}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)+\int_{[\kappa,\infty)\setminus A_{m}}\theta^{-1/2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)\right\}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\frac{C_{b,\mathrm{LG}}^{2}}{\varepsilon}\left\{m^{-1}\int_{A_{m}}\theta^{-1/2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)+2\int_{[\kappa,\infty)\setminus A_{m}}\theta^{-1/2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)+2\int_{[\kappa,\infty)\setminus A_{m}}\theta^{-3/2}|M_{b}(\theta)|^{2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)\right\}.

By the integrability conditions (
2.6
) and (
2.7
) on
\mu
and
M_{b}
, together with the assumption that
\kappa:=\inf{\mathrm{supp}\,\mu}>0
, all the integrals appearing in the definition of
\delta_{m,\varepsilon}
are finite. Furthermore, we have
\gamma\int_{[\kappa,\infty)}\theta^{-1}|M_{b}(\theta)|_{\mathrm{op}}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)<1
and
\mu([\kappa,\infty)\setminus\bigcup_{m>\kappa}A_{m})=0
by the assumption. Hence, the dominated convergence theorem yields

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\lim_{m\to\infty}\delta_{m,\varepsilon}=\gamma\int_{[\kappa,\infty)}\theta^{-1}|M_{b}(\theta)|_{\mathrm{op}}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)<1.

Thus, we can take a sufficiently small
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and sufficiently large
m\in(\kappa,\infty)
such that
\delta_{m,\varepsilon}<1
. Finally, noting that
\mathopen{|\mkern-1.5mu|\mkern-1.5mu|}y\mathclose{|\mkern-1.5mu|\mkern-1.5mu|}_{\Psi_{m}}^{2}\geq\kappa\|y\|_{\Psi_{m}}^{2}
with
\kappa:=\inf{\mathrm{supp}\,\mu}>0
, we have

\big\langle b(\mu[y]),\mu_{b,\Psi_{m}}[y]\big\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}\big[\sigma(\mu[y])^{\top}Q_{\sigma,\Psi_{m}}\sigma(\mu[y])\big]\leq\frac{1+\delta_{m,\varepsilon}}{2}\mathopen{|\mkern-1.5mu|\mkern-1.5mu|}y\mathclose{|\mkern-1.5mu|\mkern-1.5mu|}_{\Psi_{m}}^{2}-\frac{\kappa(1-\delta_{m,\varepsilon})}{2}\|y\|_{\Psi_{m}}^{2}+C_{m,\varepsilon}.

This indicates that
2
holds for the admissible weight function
\Psi=\Psi_{m}
and constants
\delta=\frac{1+\delta_{m,\varepsilon}}{2}\in(0,1)
,
\rho=\frac{\kappa(1-\delta_{m,\varepsilon})}{2}>0
and
C_{\mathrm{Lyap}}=C_{m,\varepsilon}>0
. This completes the proof.
∎

5
Finite-dimensional approximation of the invariant probability measure

In this section, we establish approximation results for the invariant probability measure
\pi\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})
and the stationary law
\mathbf{P}^{\pi}\in\mathcal{P}(\Lambda)
associated with the (infinite-dimensional) SEE (
1.2
) via their counterparts of (finite-dimensional) SDEs. In particular, the results presented here extend previous studies on Markovian approximations of SVEs, such as
[
1
,
3
,
5
]
. Furthermore, our approach offers a mathematically rigorous framework that supports the validity of Markovian embedding-type procedures widely employed in statistical physics, as discussed in the
Introduction
.

Let
(\mu,M_{b},M_{\sigma})
be a lifting basis. We introduce a sequence
\{(\Delta_{k},a_{k},M_{b,k},M_{\sigma,k})\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
of finite families
(\Delta_{k},a_{k},M_{b,k},M_{\sigma,k})=\{(\Delta_{k}^{(i)},a_{k}^{(i)},M_{b,k}^{(i)},M_{\sigma,k}^{(i)})\}^{I_{k}}_{i=1}
for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
, where

\big(\Delta_{k}^{(i)},a_{k}^{(i)},M_{b,k}^{(i)},M_{\sigma,k}^{(i)}\big)\in\mathcal{B}([0,\infty))\times[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}

for
i\in\{1,\dots,I_{k}\}
with
I_{k}\in\mathbb{N}
, such that the following hold:

•

For each
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
i\in\{1,\dots,I_{k}\}
, the set
\Delta_{k}^{(i)}\in\mathcal{B}([0,\infty))
is bounded and satisfies
\mu(\Delta_{k}^{(i)})>0
.

•

For each
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
i,j\in\{1,\dots,I_{k}\}
with
i\neq j
, it holds that
\Delta_{k}^{(i)}\cap\Delta_{k}^{(j)}=\emptyset
.

•

Setting

\begin{split}\varepsilon_{k}&:=\max_{i\in\{1,\dots,I_{k}\}}\mu\text{-}\esssup_{\theta\in\Delta_{k}^{(i)}}\frac{|\theta-a_{k}^{(i)}|}{1+\theta}+\left(\int_{[0,\infty)}(1+\theta)^{-3/2}\left|M_{b}(\theta)-\sum^{I_{k}}_{i=1}M_{b,k}^{(i)}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{\Delta_{k}^{(i)}}(\theta)\right|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)\right)^{1/2}\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt+\left(\int_{[0,\infty)}(1+\theta)^{-1/2}\left|M_{\sigma}(\theta)-\sum^{I_{k}}_{i=1}M_{\sigma,k}^{(i)}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{\Delta_{k}^{(i)}}(\theta)\right|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)\right)^{1/2},\end{split}

(5.1)

it holds that
\lim_{k\to\infty}\varepsilon_{k}=0
.

Noting the integrability conditions (
2.7
) and (
2.8
) on
M_{b}
and
M_{\sigma}
, such a sequence always exists and can be easily constructed using a standard approximation argument for
L^{2}
-functions by simple functions. We call a sequence
\{(\Delta_{k},a_{k},M_{b,k},M_{\sigma,k})\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfying the above conditions an
approximating component
for the lifting basis
(\mu,M_{b},M_{\sigma})
.

Suppose that we are given measurable maps
b:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\sigma:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
. Let
W
be a
d
-dimensional Brownian motion defined on a complete probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
with respect to a filtration
\mathbb{F}=(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
satisfying the usual conditions. For each
k\in\mathbb{N}
, consider the following finite-dimensional SDE on
(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}}
for an Itô process
Z_{k}=(Z_{k,t})_{t\geq 0}=((Z_{k,t}^{(i)})^{I_{k}}_{i=1})_{t\geq 0}
:

\begin{split}&\mathrm{d}Z_{k,t}^{(i)}=-a_{k}^{(i)}Z_{k,t}^{(i)}\,\mathrm{d}t+M_{b,k}^{(i)}\,b\left(\sum^{I_{k}}_{j=1}\mu\big(\Delta_{k}^{(j)}\big)Z_{k,t}^{(j)}\right)\,\mathrm{d}t+M_{\sigma,k}^{(i)}\,\sigma\left(\sum^{I_{k}}_{j=1}\mu\big(\Delta_{k}^{(j)}\big)Z_{k,t}^{(j)}\right)\,\mathrm{d}W_{t},\\
&\hskip 199.16928ptt\geq 0,\ i\in\{1,\dots,I_{k}\}.\end{split}

(5.2)

If the coefficients
b:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\sigma:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
are Lipschitz continuous, then the above SDE is strongly well-posed, and the solution forms a time-homogeneous Markov process on
(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}}
.

Remark 5.1
.

Applying Itô’s formula to the process
e^{a_{k}^{(i)}t}Z_{k,t}^{(i)}
for each
i\in\{1,\dots,I_{k}\}
, we see that the solution
Z_{k}
of the SDE (
5.2
) satisfies

Z_{k,t}^{(i)}=e^{-a_{k}^{(i)}t}Z_{k,0}^{(i)}+\int^{t}_{0}e^{-a_{k}^{(i)}(t-s)}M_{b,k}^{(i)}\,b(X_{k,s})\,\mathrm{d}s+\int^{t}_{0}e^{-a_{k}^{(i)}(t-s)}M_{\sigma,k}^{(i)}\,\sigma(X_{k,s})\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}

for
t\geq 0
and
i\in\{1,\dots,I_{k}\}
, where
X_{k,t}:=\sum^{I_{k}}_{i=1}\mu(\Delta_{k}^{(i)})Z_{k,t}^{(i)}
. Multiplying both sides by
\mu(\Delta_{k}^{(i)})
and summing over
i\in\{1,\dots,I_{k}\}
shows that the
\mathbb{R}^{n}
-valued progressively measurable (and continuous) process
X_{k}=(X_{k,t})_{t\geq 0}
solves the following SVE:

X_{k,t}=x_{k}(t)+\int^{t}_{0}K_{b,k}(t-s)b(X_{k,s})\,\mathrm{d}s+\int^{t}_{0}K_{\sigma,k}(t-s)\sigma(X_{k,s})\,\mathrm{d}W_{s},\ \ t\geq 0,

(5.3)

where

x_{k}(t)=\sum^{I_{k}}_{i=1}e^{-a_{k}^{(i)}t}\mu\big(\Delta_{k}^{(i)}\big)Z_{k,0}^{(i)},\ \ K_{b,k}(t)=\sum^{I_{k}}_{i=1}e^{-a_{k}^{(i)}t}\mu\big(\Delta_{k}^{(i)}\big)M_{b,k}^{(i)},\ \ K_{\sigma,k}(t)=\sum^{I_{k}}_{i=1}e^{-a_{k}^{(i)}t}\mu\big(\Delta_{k}^{(i)}\big)M_{\sigma,k}^{(i)},

for
t\geq 0
. Note that the forcing term
x_{k}
and kernels
K_{b,k}
and
K_{\sigma,k}
are of the (finite) sum-of-exponentials type as in
2.9
(i).

For each
\nu_{k}\in\mathcal{P}((\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}})
, we denote by
\mathbf{P}_{k}^{\nu_{k}}\in\mathcal{P}(C([0,\infty);(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}}))
the law of a solution
Z_{k}=(Z_{k,t})_{t\geq 0}
to the approximating SDE (
5.2
) with initial distribution
\nu_{k}
, where
C([0,\infty);(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}})
is the Polish space of
(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}}
-valued continuous functions on
[0,\infty)
, which is equipped with the complete metric defined by (
2.1
). The associated Markov semigroup
\{P_{k,t}\}_{t\geq 0}
on
(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}}
is defined by

P_{k,t}(z_{k},A_{k}):=\mathbf{P}_{k}^{\delta_{z_{k}}}\left(\left\{\zeta_{k}\in C\big([0,\infty);(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}}\big)\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\zeta_{k,t}\in A_{k}\right\}\right)

for
z_{k}\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}}
,
A_{k}\in\mathcal{B}((\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}})
, and
t\geq 0
.

The purpose of this section is to show that the invariant probability measure
\pi_{k}\in\mathcal{P}((\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}})
for the Markov semigroup
\{P_{k,t}\}_{t\geq 0}
“converges” to the invariant probability measure
\pi\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})
for the Markov semigroup
\{P_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
associated with the SEE (
1.2
) in a suitable sense. Beyond the convergence of invariant probability measures, we investigate the “convergence” of the stationary laws
\mathbf{P}_{k}^{\pi_{k}}\in\mathcal{P}(C([0,\infty);(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}}))
for the finite-dimensional SDEs (
5.2
) to the limit stationary law
\mathbf{P}^{\pi}\in\mathcal{P}(\Lambda)
for the SEE (
1.2
). More precise statements are given in
5.3
below.

Since the SEE (
1.2
) and the approximating SDEs (
5.2
) are defined on different state spaces, in order to compare the solutions, we have to embed the finite-dimensional space
(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}}
to
\mathcal{H}
in a suitable manner. To this end, for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
, let us introduce a bounded linear operator
\Upsilon_{k}:(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}}\to\mathcal{V}
by

(\Upsilon_{k}z_{k})(\theta):=\sum^{I_{k}}_{i=1}z_{k}^{(i)}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{\Delta_{k}^{(i)}}(\theta),\ \ \theta\in[0,\infty),

for
z_{k}=(z_{k}^{(i)})^{I_{k}}_{i=1}\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}}
. Note that

\big\|\Upsilon_{k}z_{k}\big\|_{\mathcal{H}}=\left(\sum^{I_{k}}_{i=1}\int_{\Delta_{k}^{(i)}}(1+\theta)^{-1/2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)\,\big|z_{k}^{(i)}\big|^{2}\right)^{1/2}\ \ \text{and}\ \ \big\|\Upsilon_{k}z_{k}\big\|_{\mathcal{V}}=\left(\sum^{I_{k}}_{i=1}\int_{\Delta_{k}^{(i)}}(1+\theta)^{1/2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)\,\big|z_{k}^{(i)}\big|^{2}\right)^{1/2}

for each
z_{k}=(z_{k}^{(i)})^{I_{k}}_{i=1}\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}}
. Both the maps
z_{k}\mapsto\|\Upsilon_{k}z_{k}\|_{\mathcal{H}}
and
z_{k}\mapsto\|\Upsilon_{k}z_{k}\|_{\mathcal{V}}
define (equivalent) norms on the finite-dimensional space
(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}}
, as each
\Delta_{k}^{(i)}
is bounded and satisfies
\mu(\Delta_{k}^{(i)})>0
.

Lemma 5.2
.

Let a lifting basis
(\mu,M_{b},M_{\sigma})
and its approximating component
\{(\Delta_{k},a_{k},M_{b,k},M_{\sigma,k})\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be given. Assume that
b:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\sigma:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
are Lipschitz continuous.

(i)

Suppose that we are given a
d
-dimensional Brownian motion
W
defined on a complete probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
, with respect to a filtration
\mathbb{F}=(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
satisfying the usual conditions. Then, for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
and any
(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}}
-valued
\mathcal{F}_{0}
-measurable random variable
Z_{k,0}
, the SDE (
5.2
) has a unique solution
Z_{k}=(Z_{k,t})_{t\geq 0}
with the prescribed initial condition
Z_{k,0}
. This solution forms a time-homogeneous Markov process on
(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}}
, and its Markov semigroup
\{P_{k,t}\}_{t\geq 0}
is stochastically continuous and satisfies the Feller property.

(ii)

There exists a constant
C_{0}>0
such that, for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
, any solution
Z_{k}=(Z_{k,t})_{t\geq 0}
of the SDE (
5.2
) and any solution
Y=(Y_{t})_{t\geq 0}
of the SEE (
1.2
) driven by a common
d
-dimensional Brownian motion
W
on a complete probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
, we have

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\big\|\Upsilon_{k}Z_{k,t}\big\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}+\int^{T}_{0}\big\|\Upsilon_{k}Z_{k,t}\big\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\mathcal{F}_{0}\right]\leq C_{0}e^{C_{0}T}\left(1+\big\|\Upsilon_{k}Z_{k,0}\big\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right)

(5.4)

and

\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\big\|Y_{t}-\Upsilon_{k}Z_{k,t}\big\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}+\int^{T}_{0}\big\|Y_{t}-\Upsilon_{k}Z_{k,t}\big\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\mathcal{F}_{0}\right]\\
&\leq C_{0}e^{C_{0}T}\big\|Y_{0}-\Upsilon_{k}Z_{k,0}\big\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+C_{0}e^{C_{0}T}\varepsilon_{k}^{2}\left(1+\big\|\Upsilon_{k}Z_{k,0}\big\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right),\end{split}

(5.5)

for any
T>0
a.s., where
\varepsilon_{k}>0
is the constant defined in (
5.1
).

(iii)

If
2
further holds, then there exists a natural number
k_{0}
such that, for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
with
k\geq k_{0}
, the Markov semigroup
\{P_{k,t}\}_{t\geq 0}
associated with the SDE (
5.2
) possesses at least one invariant probability measure
\pi_{k}\in\mathcal{P}((\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}})
. Furthermore, it holds that

\sup_{k\geq k_{0}}\sup_{\pi_{k}\in\Pi_{k}}\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}}}\big\|\Upsilon_{k}z_{k}\big\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\,\pi_{k}(\mathrm{d}z_{k})<\infty,

(5.6)

where
\Pi_{k}\subset\mathcal{P}((\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}})
denotes the (non-empty) set of all invariant probability measures for
\{P_{k,t}\}_{t\geq 0}
.

Proof.

For each
k\in\mathbb{N}
, since the coefficients of the SDE (
5.2
) on
(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}}
are Lipschitz continuous, assertion (i) follows from standard results on finite-dimensional SDEs. Furthermore, there exists a constant
\widetilde{C}_{k}>0
(which may depend on
k\in\mathbb{N}
) such that

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\big|Z_{k,t}\big|^{2}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\mathcal{F}_{0}\right]\leq\widetilde{C}_{k}e^{\widetilde{C}_{k}T}\left(1+\big|Z_{k,0}\big|^{2}\right)<\infty

(5.7)

for any
T>0
a.s. Here,
|\cdot|
denotes the standard Euclidean norm on
(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}}
.

To prove assertions (ii) and (iii), let us introduce additional notation for use throughout this proof. For each
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we set
\mathcal{V}_{k}:=\{\Upsilon_{k}z_{k}\,|\,z_{k}\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}}\}
. Note that
\mathcal{V}_{k}\subset\mathcal{V}
. Furthermore, for any
y_{k}=\Upsilon_{k}z_{k}\in\mathcal{V}_{k}
with
z_{k}=(z_{k}^{(i)})^{I_{k}}_{i=1}\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}}
, we have

\mu[y_{k}]=\mu\big[\Upsilon_{k}z_{k}\big]=\sum^{I_{k}}_{i=1}\mu\big(\Delta_{k}^{(i)}\big)z_{k}^{(i)}.

We define
\mathcal{A}_{k}:\mathcal{V}_{k}\to\mathcal{V}_{k}
and
\mathcal{M}_{b,k},\mathcal{M}_{\sigma,k}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathcal{V}_{k}
by

\mathcal{A}_{k}y_{k}:=-\sum^{I_{k}}_{i=1}a_{k}^{(i)}z_{k}^{(i)}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{\Delta_{k}^{(i)}}

for
y_{k}=\Upsilon_{k}z_{k}
with
z_{k}=(z_{k}^{(i)})^{I_{k}}_{i=1}\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}}
, and

\mathcal{M}_{b,k}x:=\sum^{I_{k}}_{i=1}M_{b,k}^{(i)}x\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{\Delta_{k}^{(i)}},\ \ \mathcal{M}_{\sigma,k}x:=\sum^{I_{k}}_{i=1}M_{\sigma,k}^{(i)}x\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{\Delta_{k}^{(i)}},

for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Recalling the definition (
5.1
) of
\varepsilon_{k}
, for any
y_{k}=\Upsilon_{k}z_{k}\in\mathcal{V}_{k}
with
z_{k}=(z_{k}^{(i)})^{I_{k}}_{i=1}\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}}
, we have

\displaystyle\big\|(\mathcal{A}_{k}-\mathcal{A})y_{k}\big\|_{\mathcal{V}^{*}}

\displaystyle=\left(\sum^{I_{k}}_{i=1}\int_{\Delta_{k}^{(i)}}(1+\theta)^{-3/2}\big|\theta-a_{k}^{(i)}\big|^{2}\big|z_{k}^{(i)}\big|^{2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon_{k}\left(\sum^{I_{k}}_{i=1}\int_{\Delta_{k}^{(i)}}(1+\theta)^{1/2}\big|z_{k}^{(i)}\big|^{2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon_{k}\|y_{k}\|_{\mathcal{V}}.

(5.8)

Furthermore, for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we have

\big\|(\mathcal{M}_{b,k}-\mathcal{M}_{b})x\big\|_{\mathcal{V}^{*}}=\left(\int_{[0,\infty)}(1+\theta)^{-3/2}\left|\left(\sum^{I_{k}}_{i=1}M_{b,k}^{(i)}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{\Delta_{k}^{(i)}}(\theta)-M_{b}(\theta)\right)x\right|^{2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)\right)^{1/2}\leq\varepsilon_{k}|x|

(5.9)

and

\big\|(\mathcal{M}_{\sigma,k}-\mathcal{M}_{\sigma})x\big\|_{\mathcal{H}}=\left(\int_{[0,\infty)}(1+\theta)^{-1/2}\left|\left(\sum^{I_{k}}_{i=1}M_{\sigma,k}^{(i)}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{\Delta_{k}^{(i)}}(\theta)-M_{\sigma}(\theta)\right)x\right|^{2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)\right)^{1/2}\leq\varepsilon_{k}|x|.

(5.10)

Now we prove the estimate (
5.4
) in assertion (ii). Recalling (
5.7
), together with the fact that
\|\Upsilon_{k}\cdot\|_{\mathcal{H}}
,
\|\Upsilon_{k}\cdot\|_{\mathcal{V}}
and
|\cdot|
are equivalent norms on the finite-dimensional space
(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}}
, it suffices to show that there exists a constant
C_{0}>0
and a number
k_{1}\in\mathbb{N}
such that (
5.4
) holds for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
with
k\geq k_{1}
. For a while, we fix an arbitrary
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
Z_{k}=(Z_{k,t})_{t\geq 0}
be a solution to the SDE (
5.2
), and set
Y_{k,t}:=\Upsilon_{k}Z_{k,t}
for
t\geq 0
. By the above observations, the
\mathcal{V}_{k}
-valued process
Y_{k}=(Y_{k,t})_{t\geq 0}
satisfies

Y_{k,t}=Y_{k,0}+\int^{t}_{0}\big\{\mathcal{A}_{k}Y_{k,s}+\mathcal{M}_{b,k}b(\mu[Y_{k,s}])\big\}\,\mathrm{d}s+\int^{t}_{0}\mathcal{M}_{\sigma,k}\sigma(\mu[Y_{k,s}])\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}

(5.11)

for any
t\geq 0
a.s. Applying Itô’s formula for
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}
in
2.19
to
Y_{k}
yields

\big\|Y_{k,t}\big\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}=\big\|Y_{k,0}\big\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+\int^{t}_{0}f_{k}(Y_{k,s})\,\mathrm{d}s+2\int^{t}_{0}\big\langle Y_{k,s},\mathcal{M}_{\sigma,k}\sigma(\mu[Y_{k,s}])\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}\big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

(5.12)

for any
t\geq 0
a.s., where
f_{k}:\mathcal{V}_{k}\to\mathbb{R}
is defined by

f_{k}(y_{k}):=2\big\langle\mathcal{A}_{k}y_{k}+\mathcal{M}_{b,k}b(\mu[y_{k}]),y_{k}\big\rangle_{\mathcal{V}^{*},\mathcal{V}}+\big\|\mathcal{M}_{\sigma,k}\sigma(\mu[y_{k}])\big\|_{L_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathcal{H})}^{2},\ \ y_{k}\in\mathcal{V}_{k}.

Decompose
f_{k}
as
f_{k}=f_{k}^{(1)}+f_{k}^{(2)}
, where

\displaystyle f_{k}^{(1)}(y_{k}):=2\big\langle\mathcal{A}y_{k},y_{k}\big\rangle_{\mathcal{V}^{*},\mathcal{V}}+2\big\langle\mathcal{M}_{b}b(\mu[y_{k}]),y_{k}\big\rangle_{\mathcal{V}^{*},\mathcal{V}}+\big\|\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}\sigma(\mu[y_{k}])\big\|_{L_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathcal{H})}^{2},

\displaystyle f_{k}^{(2)}(y_{k}):=2\big\langle(\mathcal{A}_{k}-\mathcal{A})y_{k},y_{k}\big\rangle_{\mathcal{V}^{*},\mathcal{V}}+2\big\langle(\mathcal{M}_{b,k}-\mathcal{M}_{b})b(\mu[y_{k}]),y_{k}\big\rangle_{\mathcal{V}^{*},\mathcal{V}}

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt+\big\langle(\mathcal{M}_{\sigma,k}-\mathcal{M}_{\sigma})\sigma(\mu[y_{k}]),(\mathcal{M}_{\sigma,k}+\mathcal{M}_{\sigma})\sigma(\mu[y_{k}])\big\rangle_{L_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathcal{H})},

for
y_{k}\in\mathcal{V}_{k}
. On the one hand, analogously to the proof of
2.15
(ii), by employing (
2.13
),
\mathcal{M}_{b}\in L(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathcal{V})
,
\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}\in L(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathcal{H})
, the linear growth of
b:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\sigma:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
, and
2.10
, together with Young’s inequality, we see that there exists a constant
C_{1}>0
, independent of
k
, such that

f_{k}^{(1)}(y_{k})\leq C_{1}\|y_{k}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}-\frac{3}{2}\|y_{k}\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}+C_{1}

for any
y_{k}\in\mathcal{V}_{k}
. On the other hand, from (
5.8
), (
5.9
) and (
5.10
), along with the linear growth of
b
and
\sigma
and the fact that
\mu[\cdot]\in L(\mathcal{V};\mathbb{R}^{n})
, we see that there exists a constant
C_{2}>0
, also independent of
k
, such that

\displaystyle f_{k}^{(2)}(y_{k})\leq C_{2}\varepsilon_{k}\Big(1+\|y_{k}\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\Big)

for any
y_{k}\in\mathcal{V}_{k}
. Now, we choose
k_{1}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
C_{2}\varepsilon_{k}\leq\frac{1}{2}
for all
k\geq k_{1}
. This is possible since
\lim_{k\to\infty}\varepsilon_{k}=0
by assumption. Let
k\geq k_{1}
. Then, we have

f_{k}(y_{k})=f_{k}^{(1)}(y_{k})+f_{k}^{(2)}(y_{k})\leq C_{1}\|y_{k}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}-\|y_{k}\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}+C_{1}+\frac{1}{2}

(5.13)

for any
y_{k}\in\mathcal{V}_{k}
. By (
5.12
) and (
5.13
), we have

\displaystyle\big\|Y_{k,t}\big\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+\int^{t}_{0}\big\|Y_{k,s}\big\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\big\|Y_{k,0}\big\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+C_{1}\int^{t}_{0}\big\|Y_{k,s}\big\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s+\left(C_{1}+\frac{1}{2}\right)t+2\int^{t}_{0}\big\langle Y_{k,s},\mathcal{M}_{\sigma,k}\sigma(\mu[Y_{k,s}])\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}\big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

for any
t\geq 0
a.s. From this estimate, by arguments analogous to the proof of
2.15
(ii), it can be shown that

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\big\|Y_{k,t}\big\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+\int^{T}_{0}\big\|Y_{k,t}\big\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\mathcal{F}_{0}\right]\leq C_{0}e^{C_{0}T}\left(1+\big\|Y_{k,0}\big\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right)

for any
T>0
a.s., where
C_{0}>0
is a constant independent of
k
. Thus, (
5.4
) holds for all
k\geq k_{1}
. This shows assertion (i).

Next, we prove (
5.5
) in assertion (ii). Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
be fixed. In view of (
5.11
), applying Itô’s formula for
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}
in
2.19
to the process
Y-Y_{k}
yields

\begin{split}\big\|Y_{t}-Y_{k,t}\big\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}&=\big\|Y_{0}-Y_{k,0}\big\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+\int^{t}_{0}g_{k}(Y_{s},Y_{k,s})\,\mathrm{d}s\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt+2\int^{t}_{0}\Big\langle Y_{s}-Y_{k,s},\big(\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}\sigma(\mu[Y_{s}])-\mathcal{M}_{\sigma,k}\sigma(\mu[Y_{k,s}])\big)\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\end{split}

(5.14)

for any
t\geq 0
a.s., where the function
g_{k}:\mathcal{V}\times\mathcal{V}_{k}\to\mathbb{R}
is defined by

\displaystyle g_{k}(y,y_{k})

\displaystyle:=2\big\langle\mathcal{A}y+\mathcal{M}_{b}b(\mu[y])-\mathcal{A}_{k}y_{k}-\mathcal{M}_{b,k}b(\mu[y_{k}]),y-y_{k}\big\rangle_{\mathcal{V}^{*},\mathcal{V}}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+\big\|\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}\sigma(\mu[y])-\mathcal{M}_{\sigma,k}\sigma(\mu[y_{k}])\big\|_{L_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathcal{H})}^{2},\ \ (y,y_{k})\in\mathcal{V}\times\mathcal{V}_{k}.

Decompose
g_{k}
as
g_{k}=g_{k}^{(1)}+g_{k}^{(2)}
, where

\displaystyle g_{k}^{(1)}(y,y_{k}):=2\big\langle\mathcal{A}(y-y_{k}),y-y_{k}\big\rangle_{\mathcal{V}^{*},\mathcal{V}}+2\big\langle\mathcal{M}_{b}\big(b(\mu[y])-b(\mu[y_{k}])\big),y-y_{k}\big\rangle_{\mathcal{V}^{*},\mathcal{V}}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+\big\|\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}\big(\sigma(\mu[y])-\sigma(\mu[y_{k}])\big)\big\|_{L_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathcal{H})}^{2},

\displaystyle g_{k}^{(2)}(y,y_{k}):=2\big\langle(\mathcal{A}-\mathcal{A}_{k})y_{k},y-y_{k}\big\rangle_{\mathcal{V}^{*},\mathcal{V}}+2\big\langle(\mathcal{M}_{b}-\mathcal{M}_{b,k})b(\mu[y_{k}]),y-y_{k}\big\rangle_{\mathcal{V}^{*},\mathcal{V}}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+2\big\langle(\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}-\mathcal{M}_{\sigma,k})\sigma(\mu[y_{k}]),\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}\big(\sigma(\mu[y])-\sigma(\mu[y_{k}])\big)\Big\rangle_{L_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathcal{H})}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+\big\|(\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}-\mathcal{M}_{\sigma,k})\sigma(\mu[y_{k}])\big\|_{L_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathcal{H})}^{2},

for
(y,y_{k})\in\mathcal{V}\times\mathcal{V}_{k}
. Analogously to the proof of
2.15
(ii), by using (
2.13
),
\mathcal{M}_{b}\in L(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathcal{V})
,
\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}\in L(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathcal{H})
, Lipschitz continuity of
b:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\sigma:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
, and
2.10
, together with Young’s inequality, we see that there exists a constant
C_{3}>0
, independent of
k
, such that

\displaystyle g_{k}^{(1)}(y,y_{k})

\displaystyle\leq C_{3}\|y-y_{k}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}-\frac{3}{2}\|y-y_{k}\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}

for any
(y,y_{k})\in\mathcal{V}\times\mathcal{V}_{k}
. On the other hand, by utilizing (
5.8
), (
5.9
), (
5.10
), Lipschitz continuity of
b
and
\sigma
, and
\mu[\cdot]\in L(\mathcal{V};\mathbb{R}^{n})
, together with Young’s inequality, we see that there exists a constant
C_{4}>0
, independent of
k
, such that

\displaystyle g_{k}^{(2)}(y,y_{k})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\|y-y_{k}\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}+C_{4}\varepsilon_{k}^{2}\left\{1+\|y_{k}\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\right\}

for any
(y,y_{k})\in\mathcal{V}\times\mathcal{V}_{k}
. Combining the above estimates, we obtain

g_{k}(y,y_{k})=g_{k}^{(1)}(y,y_{k})+g_{k}^{(2)}(y,y_{k})\leq C_{3}\|y-y_{k}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}-\|y-y_{k}\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}+C_{4}\varepsilon_{k}^{2}\left\{1+\|y_{k}\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\right\}

(5.15)

for any
(y,y_{k})\in\mathcal{V}\times\mathcal{V}_{k}
. By (
5.14
) and (
5.15
), we have

\displaystyle\big\|Y_{t}-Y_{k,t}\big\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+\int^{t}_{0}\big\|Y_{s}-Y_{k,s}\big\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\big\|Y_{0}-Y_{k,0}\big\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+C_{3}\int^{t}_{0}\big\|Y_{s}-Y_{k,s}\big\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s+C_{4}\varepsilon_{k}^{2}\int^{t}_{0}\Big\{1+\big\|Y_{k,s}\big\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\Big\}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+2\int^{t}_{0}\Big\langle Y_{s}-Y_{k,s},\big(\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}\sigma(\mu[Y_{s}])-\mathcal{M}_{\sigma,k}\sigma(\mu[Y_{k,s}])\big)\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}\Big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

for any
t\geq 0
a.s. From this, by the same manner as in the proof of
2.15
(ii), we can show that there exists a constant
C_{5}>0
, independent
k
, such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\big\|Y_{t}-Y_{k,t}\big\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+\int^{T}_{0}\big\|Y_{t}-Y_{k,t}\big\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\mathcal{F}_{0}\right]

\displaystyle\leq C_{5}e^{C_{5}T}\big\|Y_{0}-Y_{k,0}\big\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+C_{5}e^{C_{5}T}\varepsilon_{k}^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\int^{T}_{0}\Big\{1+\big\|Y_{k,t}\big\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\Big\}\,\mathrm{d}t\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\mathcal{F}_{0}\right]

for any
T>0
. Combining this estimate with (
5.4
) ensures that (
5.5
) holds for any
T>0
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
for some constant
C_{0}>0
.

Lastly, we prove assertion (iii). We additionally assume that
2
holds. Recall the notations in
2.17
. Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
be fixed. Let
\Psi
be the admissible weight function in
2
. In view of (
5.11
), applying Itô’s formula for
\|\cdot\|_{\Psi}^{2}
in
2.19
to
Y_{k}
yields

\big\|Y_{k,t}\big\|_{\Psi}^{2}=\big\|Y_{k,0}\big\|_{\Psi}^{2}+\int^{t}_{0}h_{k}(Y_{k,s})\,\mathrm{d}s+2\int^{t}_{0}\big\langle Y_{k,s},\mathcal{M}_{\sigma,k}\sigma(\mu[Y_{k,s}])\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}\big\rangle_{\Psi}

(5.16)

for any
t\geq 0
a.s., where
h_{k}:\mathcal{V}_{k}\to\mathbb{R}
is defined by

h_{k}(y_{k}):=2\big\langle\mathcal{A}_{k}y_{k}+\mathcal{M}_{b,k}b(\mu[y_{k}]),y_{k}\big\rangle_{\mathcal{V}^{*},\mathcal{V},\Psi}+\big\|\mathcal{M}_{\sigma,k}\sigma(\mu[y_{k}])\big\|_{L_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};(\mathcal{H},\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\Psi}))}^{2},\ \ y_{k}\in\mathcal{V}_{k}.

Decompose
h_{k}
as
h_{k}=h_{k}^{(1)}+h_{k}^{(2)}
, where

\displaystyle h_{k}^{(1)}(y_{k}):=2\big\langle\mathcal{A}y_{k},y_{k}\big\rangle_{\mathcal{V}^{*},\mathcal{V},\Psi}+2\big\langle b(\mu[y_{k}]),\mu_{b,\Psi}[y_{k}]\big\rangle+\mathrm{tr}\big[\sigma(\mu[y_{k}])^{\top}Q_{\sigma,\Psi}\sigma(\mu[y_{k}])\big],

\displaystyle h_{k}^{(2)}(y_{k}):=2\big\langle(\mathcal{A}_{k}-\mathcal{A})y_{k},y_{k}\big\rangle_{\mathcal{V}^{*},\mathcal{V},\Psi}+2\big\langle(\mathcal{M}_{b,k}-\mathcal{M}_{b})b(\mu[y_{k}]),y_{k}\big\rangle_{\mathcal{V}^{*},\mathcal{V},\Psi}

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt+\big\langle(\mathcal{M}_{\sigma,k}-\mathcal{M}_{\sigma})\sigma(\mu[y_{k}]),(\mathcal{M}_{\sigma,k}+\mathcal{M}_{\sigma})\sigma(\mu[y_{k}])\big\rangle_{L_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};(\mathcal{H},\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\Psi}))}

for
y_{k}\in\mathcal{V}_{k}
. Combining the equality (
2.33
) and the estimate (
3.4
) in
2
, we have

h_{k}^{(1)}(y_{k})\leq-2\Big\{\rho\|y_{k}\|_{\Psi}^{2}+(1-\delta)\mathopen{|\mkern-1.5mu|\mkern-1.5mu|}y_{k}\mathclose{|\mkern-1.5mu|\mkern-1.5mu|}_{\Psi}^{2}\Big\}+2C_{\mathrm{Lyap}}

for any
y_{k}\in\mathcal{V}_{k}
. Since the map
y\mapsto\big(\rho\|y\|_{\Psi}^{2}+(1-\delta)\mathopen{|\mkern-1.5mu|\mkern-1.5mu|}y\mathclose{|\mkern-1.5mu|\mkern-1.5mu|}_{\Psi}^{2})^{1/2}
defines a norm on
\mathcal{V}
equivalent to
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{V}}
, by (
5.8
), (
5.9
) and (
5.10
), together with the linear growth of
b:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\sigma:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
and the fact that
\mu[\cdot]\in L(\mathcal{V};\mathbb{R}^{n})
, we see that there exists a constant
C_{6}>0
, independent of
k
, such that

h_{k}^{(2)}(y_{k})\leq C_{6}\varepsilon_{k}\Big\{1+\rho\|y_{k}\|_{\Psi}^{2}+(1-\delta)\mathopen{|\mkern-1.5mu|\mkern-1.5mu|}y_{k}\mathclose{|\mkern-1.5mu|\mkern-1.5mu|}_{\Psi}^{2}\Big\}

for any
y_{k}\in\mathcal{V}_{k}
. Now we take
k_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
C_{6}\varepsilon_{k}\leq 1
for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
with
k\geq k_{0}
, and fix such a
k
. Then, we have

h_{k}(y_{k})=h_{k}^{(1)}(y_{k})+h_{k}^{(2)}(y_{k})\leq-\Big\{\rho\|y_{k}\|_{\Psi}^{2}+(1-\delta)\mathopen{|\mkern-1.5mu|\mkern-1.5mu|}y_{k}\mathclose{|\mkern-1.5mu|\mkern-1.5mu|}_{\Psi}^{2}\Big\}+2C_{\mathrm{Lyap}}+1

(5.17)

for any
y_{k}\in\mathcal{V}_{k}
. By (
5.16
) and (
5.17
), we obtain

\begin{split}&\big\|Y_{k,t}\big\|_{\Psi}^{2}+\int^{t}_{0}\Big\{\rho\big\|Y_{k,s}\big\|_{\Psi}^{2}+(1-\delta)\mathopen{\big|\mkern-1.5mu\big|\mkern-1.5mu\big|}Y_{k,s}\mathclose{\big|\mkern-1.5mu\big|\mkern-1.5mu\big|}_{\Psi}^{2}\Big\}\,\mathrm{d}s\\
&\leq\big\|Y_{k,0}\big\|_{\Psi}^{2}+\big(2C_{\mathrm{Lyap}}+1\big)t+2\int^{t}_{0}\big\langle Y_{k,s},\mathcal{M}_{\sigma,k}\sigma(\mu[Y_{k,s}])\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}\big\rangle_{\Psi}\end{split}

(5.18)

for any
t\geq 0
a.s. Let
\{P_{k,t}\}_{t\geq 0}
be the Markov semigroup on
(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}}
associated with the SDE (
5.2
). For each
t>0
, define a probability kernel
\nu_{k,t}:(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}}\times\mathcal{B}((\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}})\to[0,1]
by
\nu_{k,t}(z_{k},A_{k}):=\frac{1}{t}\int^{t}_{0}P_{k,s}(z_{k},A_{k})\,\mathrm{d}s
for
z_{k}\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}}
and
A_{k}\in\mathcal{B}((\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}})
. Analogously to the proof of
3.4
(see
Section

4.2
), recalling
Y_{k,t}=\Upsilon_{k}Z_{k,t}
, the estimate (
5.18
) ensures that

\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}}}\Big\{\rho\big\|\Upsilon_{k}z_{k}^{\prime}\big\|_{\Psi}^{2}+(1-\delta)\mathopen{\big|\mkern-1.5mu\big|\mkern-1.5mu\big|}\Upsilon_{k}z_{k}^{\prime}\mathclose{\big|\mkern-1.5mu\big|\mkern-1.5mu\big|}_{\Psi}^{2}\Big\}\,\nu_{k,t}(z_{k},\mathrm{d}z_{k}^{\prime})\leq\frac{\big\|\Upsilon_{k}z_{k}\big\|_{\Psi}^{2}}{t}+2C_{\mathrm{Lyap}}+1

for all
t>0
and all
z_{k}\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}}
. Since
z_{k}\mapsto(\rho\|\Upsilon_{k}z_{k}\|_{\Psi}^{2}+(1-\delta)\mathopen{|\mkern-1.5mu|\mkern-1.5mu|}\Upsilon_{k}z_{k}\mathclose{|\mkern-1.5mu|\mkern-1.5mu|}_{\Psi}^{2})^{1/2}
defines a norm on the finite-dimensional space
(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}}
, the above estimate immediately implies that the family of probability measures
\{\nu_{k,t}(z_{k},\cdot)\}_{t\geq 1}
on
(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}}
is tight for any
z_{k}\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}}
; see
4.4
. Hence, by the Krylov–Bogoliubov theorem (see, e.g.,
[
15
, Corollary 11.8]
), we see that there exists at least one invariant probability measure
\pi_{k}\in\mathcal{P}((\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}})
for
\{P_{k,t}\}_{t\geq 0}
. Furthermore, based on the estimate for
\nu_{k,t}
obtained above, an argument analogous to the final part of the proof of
3.4
(see
Section

4.2
) implies that

\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}}}\Big\{\rho\big\|\Upsilon_{k}z_{k}\big\|_{\Psi}^{2}+(1-\delta)\mathopen{\big|\mkern-1.5mu\big|\mkern-1.5mu\big|}\Upsilon_{k}z_{k}\mathclose{\big|\mkern-1.5mu\big|\mkern-1.5mu\big|}_{\Psi}^{2}\Big\}\,\pi_{k}(\mathrm{d}z_{k})\leq 2C_{\mathrm{Lyap}}+1.

Since
y\mapsto(\rho\|y\|_{\Psi}^{2}+(1-\delta)\mathopen{|\mkern-1.5mu|\mkern-1.5mu|}y\mathclose{|\mkern-1.5mu|\mkern-1.5mu|}_{\Psi}^{2})^{1/2}
defines a norm on
\mathcal{V}
equivalent to
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{V}}
, we see that (
5.6
) holds. This completes the proof.
∎

Under the settings of
5.2
(iii), for each
\pi_{k}\in\Pi_{k}
with
k\geq k_{0}
, the law
\mathbf{P}_{k}^{\pi_{k}}\in\mathcal{P}(C([0,\infty);(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}})
of a solution
Z_{k}=(Z_{k,t})_{t\geq 0}
to the approximating SDE (
5.2
) with initial distribution
\pi_{k}
is invariant under the time-shifts on
C([0,\infty);(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}})
; see
3.10
(i) for the corresponding result on the stationary law
\mathbf{P}^{\pi}\in\mathcal{P}(\Lambda)
of the SEE (
1.2
).

With a slight abuse of notation, we continue to denote by
\Upsilon_{k}
the map that sends each
\zeta_{k}=(\zeta_{k,t})_{t\geq 0}\in C([0,\infty);(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}})
to
(\Upsilon_{k}\zeta_{k,t})_{t\geq 0}\in\Lambda
. Then, we can regard
\Upsilon_{k}
as a continuous map from
C([0,\infty);(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}})
to
\Lambda
. For each
T\in(0,\infty)
, define an extended pseudo-metric
\mathbb{W}_{\Lambda_{T}}:\mathcal{P}(\Lambda)\times\mathcal{P}(\Lambda)\to[0,\infty]
on
\mathcal{P}(\Lambda)
by

\mathbb{W}_{\Lambda_{T}}(\mathfrak{p}_{1},\mathfrak{p}_{2}):=\inf_{\mathfrak{p}\in\mathscr{C}(\mathfrak{p}_{1},\mathfrak{p}_{2})}\int_{\Lambda\times\Lambda}\|\eta_{1}-\eta_{2}\|_{\Lambda_{T}}\,\mathfrak{p}(\mathrm{d}\eta_{1},\mathrm{d}\eta_{2})

(5.19)

for
\mathfrak{p}_{1},\mathfrak{p}_{2}\in\mathcal{P}(\Lambda)
, where the seminorm
\|\cdot\|_{\Lambda_{T}}
on
\Lambda
is defined by (
2.31
).

The following theorem is the main result of this section, establishing the weak convergence of
\mathbf{P}_{k}^{\pi_{k}}\circ\Upsilon_{k}^{-1}
to
\mathbf{P}^{\pi}
as probability measures on the Polish space
\Lambda
.

Theorem 5.3
.

Suppose that we are given a lifting basis
(\mu,M_{b},M_{\sigma})
and measurable maps
b:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\sigma:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
satisfying both
1
and
2
. Let
\{(\Delta_{k},a_{k},M_{b,k},M_{\sigma,k})\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be an approximating component for
(\mu,M_{b},M_{\sigma})
. Let
k_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
be the number as given in
5.2
(iii). For each
k\in\mathbb{N}
with
k\geq k_{0}
, let
\Pi_{k}\subset\mathcal{P}((\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}})
be the (non-empty) set of all invariant probability measures for the Markov semigroup
\{P_{k,t}\}_{t\geq 0}
associated with the SDE (
5.2
). Furthermore, let
\pi\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})
be the (unique) invariant probability measure for the Markov semigroup
\{P_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
associated with the SEE (
1.2
). Then, for any given
T\in(0,\infty)
, we have

\lim_{k\to\infty}\sup_{\pi_{k}\in\Pi_{k}}\mathbb{W}_{\Lambda_{T}}\left(\mathbf{P}^{\pi},\mathbf{P}_{k}^{\pi_{k}}\circ\Upsilon_{k}^{-1}\right)=0.

(5.20)

Remark 5.4
.

(i)

Recall the definition (
2.30
) of the (bounded) metric
d_{\Lambda}:\Lambda\times\Lambda\to[0,1]
on the Polish space
\Lambda
. Denote by
\mathbb{W}_{\Lambda}:\mathcal{P}(\Lambda)\times\mathcal{P}(\Lambda)\to[0,1]
the associated Wasserstein metric, that is,

\mathbb{W}_{\Lambda}(\mathfrak{p}_{1},\mathfrak{p}_{2}):=\inf_{\mathfrak{p}\in\mathscr{C}(\mathfrak{p}_{1},\mathfrak{p}_{2})}\int_{\Lambda\times\Lambda}d_{\Lambda}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\,\mathfrak{p}(\mathrm{d}\eta_{1},\mathrm{d}\eta_{2})

for
\mathfrak{p}_{1},\mathfrak{p}_{2}\in\mathcal{P}(\Lambda)
. The convergence result (
5.20
) with arbitrary
T\in(0,\infty)
immediately implies that

\lim_{k\to\infty}\sup_{\pi_{k}\in\Pi_{k}}\mathbb{W}_{\Lambda}\left(\mathbf{P}^{\pi},\mathbf{P}_{k}^{\pi_{k}}\circ\Upsilon_{k}^{-1}\right)=0,

which is equivalent to the (uniform) weak convergence of
\mathbf{P}_{k}^{\pi_{k}}\circ\Upsilon_{k}^{-1}
to
\mathbf{P}^{\pi}
in
\mathcal{P}(\Lambda)
. The result (
5.20
) incorporates the weak convergence with a kind of uniform integrability of the convergent sequence.

(ii)

Since the evaluation map
\Lambda\ni\eta=(\eta_{t})_{t\geq 0}\mapsto\eta_{0}\in\mathcal{H}
is Lipschitz continuous with respect to the semi-norm
\|\cdot\|_{\Lambda_{T}}
, the convergence result (
5.20
) for the stationary laws on the path space
\Lambda
implies the following convergence result for the invariant probability measures with respect to the
L^{1}
-Wasserstein (extended) metric
\mathbb{W}_{\mathcal{H}}:\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})\times\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})\to[0,\infty]
defined by (
3.12
):

\lim_{k\to\infty}\sup_{\pi_{k}\in\Pi_{k}}\mathbb{W}_{\mathcal{H}}\big(\pi,\pi_{k}\circ\Upsilon_{k}^{-1}\big)=0,

(5.21)

which furthermore implies the (uniform) weak convergence of
\pi_{k}\circ\Upsilon_{k}^{-1}
to
\pi
in
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})
. However, our result does
not
imply the weak convergence of
\pi_{k}\circ\Upsilon_{k}^{-1}
to
\pi
in the topology of
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{V})
, although they can be seen as probability measures on
\mathcal{V}
. This is because the evaluation map is not necessarily continuous in
\mathcal{V}
; it is continuous only in
\mathcal{H}
in general.

Before going into the technical details, let us outline the main idea of the proof of
5.3
. As a first step, we will show the following convergence result with respect to the invariant probability measures:

\lim_{k\to\infty}\sup_{\pi_{k}\in\Pi_{k}}\mathbb{W}_{d_{\Phi,\Psi}}\big(\pi,\pi_{k}\circ\Upsilon_{k}^{-1}\big)=0,

(5.22)

where the distance-like function
d_{\Phi,\Psi}:\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{H}\to[0,\infty)
is defined by (
3.7
) in
3.8
(i). Recalling the standard inequalities in (
3.10
), we see that (
5.22
) implies the
L^{1}
-Wasserstein-convergence (
5.21
) for the invariant probability measures. In the next step, combining (
5.21
) with the stability estimate (
5.5
), we will show the convergence (
5.20
) of the stationary laws on the path space
\Lambda
with respect to the semi-norm
\|\cdot\|_{\Lambda_{T}}
for any
T\in(0,\infty)
. The most important step in the proof is to derive (
5.22
). Here, the “spectral gap” result for the Markov semigroup
\{P_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
with respect to the distance-like function
d_{\Phi,\Psi}
in
3.8
(i) plays a crucial role. In this step, we borrow the idea from
[
29
, Section 4.1]
.

Proof of
5.3
.

First, we prove the convergence result (
5.22
) for the invariant probability measures. Note that

\sup_{k\geq k_{0}}\sup_{\pi_{k}\in\Pi_{k}}\mathbb{W}_{d_{\Phi,\Psi}}\big(\pi,\pi_{k}\circ\Upsilon_{k}^{-1}\big)<\infty,

which follows from the definition (
3.7
) of the distance-like function
d_{\Phi,\Psi}
and the estimates (
3.8
) and (
5.6
). Furthermore, analogously to the proof of
[
29
, Lemma 4.14]
, we can easily check that there exists a constant
C_{1}>0
such that

d_{\Phi,\Psi}(y_{1},y_{3})\leq C_{1}\big\{d_{\Phi,\Psi}(y_{1},y_{2})+d_{\Phi,\Psi}(y_{2},y_{3})\big\}

for any
y_{1},y_{2},y_{3}\in\mathcal{H}
. This implies that

\mathbb{W}_{d_{\Phi,\Psi}}(\nu_{1},\nu_{3})\leq C_{1}\big\{\mathbb{W}_{d_{\Phi,\Psi}}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})+\mathbb{W}_{d_{\Phi,\Psi}}(\nu_{2},\nu_{3})\big\}

(5.23)

for any
\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu_{3}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
with
k\geq k_{0}
and
\pi_{k}\in\Pi_{k}
be fixed. Let
t_{1}:=t_{0}\vee\frac{\log(2C_{1})}{r}
, where
r>0
and
t_{0}>0
are the constants arising in
3.8
(i), and
C_{1}>0
is the constant appearing in (
5.23
). Then, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{W}_{d_{\Phi,\Psi}}\big(\pi,\pi_{k}\circ\Upsilon_{k}^{-1}\big)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{W}_{d_{\Phi,\Psi}}\big(P_{t_{1}}^{*}\pi,\big(P_{k,t_{1}}^{*}\pi_{k}\big)\circ\Upsilon_{k}^{-1}\big)

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}\mathbb{W}_{d_{\Phi,\Psi}}\big(P_{t_{1}}^{*}\pi,P_{t_{1}}^{*}\big(\pi_{k}\circ\Upsilon_{k}^{-1}\big)\big)+C_{1}\mathbb{W}_{d_{\Phi,\Psi}}\big(P_{t_{1}}^{*}\big(\pi_{k}\circ\Upsilon_{k}^{-1}\big),\big(P_{k,t_{1}}^{*}\pi_{k}\big)\circ\Upsilon_{k}^{-1}\big)

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}e^{-rt_{1}}\mathbb{W}_{d_{\Phi,\Psi}}\big(\pi,\pi_{k}\circ\Upsilon_{k}^{-1}\big)+C_{1}\mathbb{W}_{d_{\Phi,\Psi}}\big(P_{t_{1}}^{*}\big(\pi_{k}\circ\Upsilon_{k}^{-1}\big),\big(P_{k,t_{1}}^{*}\pi_{k}\big)\circ\Upsilon_{k}^{-1}\big),

where we used the invariance of
\pi
and
\pi_{k}
with respect to
P_{t_{1}}
and
P_{k,t_{1}}
, respectively, in the first line, the estimate (
5.23
) in the second line, and the spectral gap estimate (
3.6
) in the third line. Since
\mathbb{W}_{d_{\Phi,\Psi}}(\pi,\pi_{k}\circ\Upsilon_{k}^{-1})<\infty
and
C_{1}e^{-rt_{1}}\leq\frac{1}{2}
, we obtain

\mathbb{W}_{d_{\Phi,\Psi}}\big(\pi,\pi_{k}\circ\Upsilon_{k}^{-1}\big)\leq 2C_{1}\mathbb{W}_{d_{\Phi,\Psi}}\big(P_{t_{1}}^{*}\big(\pi_{k}\circ\Upsilon_{k}^{-1}\big),\big(P_{k,t_{1}}^{*}\pi_{k}\big)\circ\Upsilon_{k}^{-1}\big).

(5.24)

Now we estimate the right-hand side of (
5.24
). Let
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
be a complete probability space which supports an
(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}}
-valued random variable
Z_{k,0}
distributed according to
\pi_{k}
and a
d
-dimensional Brownian motion
W
independent of
Z_{k,0}
. Let
\mathbb{F}=(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
be the augmentation of the filtration generated by
Z_{k,0}
and
W
. Let
Y=(Y_{t})_{t\geq 0}
be the solution to the SEE (
1.2
) with the
\mathcal{H}
-valued initial condition
Y_{0}=\Upsilon_{k}Z_{k,0}
, and let
Z_{k}=(Z_{k,t})_{t\geq 0}
be the solution to the SDE (
5.2
) with the
(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}}
-valued initial condition
Z_{k,0}
, both of which are driven by the same Brownian motion
W
. Then, the joint distribution of the pair of random variables
(Y_{t_{1}},\Upsilon_{k}Z_{k,t_{1}})
on
\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{H}
under
\mathbb{P}
constitutes a coupling between the probability measures
P_{t_{1}}^{*}(\pi_{k}\circ\Upsilon_{k}^{-1})\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})
and
(P_{k,t_{1}}^{*}\pi_{k})\circ\Upsilon_{k}^{-1}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})
. Hence, we have

\mathbb{W}_{d_{\Phi,\Psi}}\big(P_{t_{1}}^{*}\big(\pi_{k}\circ\Upsilon_{k}^{-1}\big),\big(P_{k,t_{1}}^{*}\pi_{k}\big)\circ\Upsilon_{k}^{-1}\big)\leq\mathbb{E}\big[d_{\Phi,\Psi}\left(Y_{t_{1}},\Upsilon_{k}Z_{k,t_{1}}\right)\big].

Recalling the definition (
3.7
) of the distance-like function
d_{\Phi,\Psi}
, by the above estimate and the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{W}_{d_{\Phi,\Psi}}\big(P_{t_{1}}^{*}\big(\pi_{k}\circ\Upsilon_{k}^{-1}\big),\big(P_{k,t_{1}}^{*}\pi_{k}\big)\circ\Upsilon_{k}^{-1}\big)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\big\|Y_{t_{1}}-\Upsilon_{k}Z_{k,t_{1}}\big\|_{\Phi}\wedge 1\right]^{1/2}\left\{1+\mathbb{E}\left[\big\|Y_{t_{1}}\big\|_{\Psi}^{2}\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[\big\|\Upsilon_{k}Z_{k,t_{1}}\big\|_{\Psi}^{2}\right]\right\}^{1/2}.

Recall that
\|\cdot\|_{\Phi}
and
\|\cdot\|_{\Psi}
are norms on
\mathcal{H}
and equivalent to
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{H}}
. Since
Y_{0}=\Upsilon_{k}Z_{k,0}
and
\mathrm{Law}_{\mathbb{P}}(Z_{k,0})=\pi_{k}\in\mathcal{P}((\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}})
, by using the estimates (
2.15
), (
5.4
) and (
5.5
), we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{W}_{d_{\Phi,\Psi}}\big(P_{t_{1}}^{*}\big(\pi_{k}\circ\Upsilon_{k}^{-1}\big),\big(P_{k,t_{1}}^{*}\pi_{k}\big)\circ\Upsilon_{k}^{-1}\big)

\displaystyle\leq C_{2}\varepsilon_{k}^{1/2}\left(1+\mathbb{E}\left[\big\|\Upsilon_{k}Z_{k,0}\big\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\right]\right)^{3/4}

\displaystyle=C_{2}\varepsilon_{k}^{1/2}\left(1+\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}}}\big\|\Upsilon_{k}z_{k}\big\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\,\pi_{k}(\mathrm{d}z_{k})\right)^{3/4},

(5.25)

where
C_{2}>0
is a constant which does not depend on
k
or
\pi_{k}
.

By (
5.24
) and (
5.25
), together with (
5.6
), we obtain

\sup_{\pi_{k}\in\Pi_{k}}\mathbb{W}_{d_{\Phi,\Psi}}\left(\pi,\pi_{k}\circ\Upsilon_{k}^{-1}\right)\leq C_{3}\varepsilon_{k}^{1/2}

for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
with
k\geq k_{0}
, where
C_{3}>0
is a constant independent of
k
. Since
\lim_{k\to\infty}\varepsilon_{k}=0
, we obtain (
5.22
). Recalling the standard estimate (
3.10
), the convergence (
5.22
) for the distance-like function
d_{\Phi,\Psi}
implies the
L^{1}
-Wasserstein-convergence (
5.21
) for the invariant probability measures.

Fix an arbitrary
T\in(0,\infty)
. Our next purpose is to prove (
5.20
). To this end, let
k\geq k_{0}
,
\pi_{k}\in\Pi_{k}
and
\nu_{k}\in\mathscr{C}(\pi,\pi_{k}\circ\Upsilon_{k}^{-1})
be fixed. Define
\Gamma_{k}:\mathcal{H}\times(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}}\to\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{H}
by
\Gamma_{k}(y,z_{k}):=(y,\Upsilon_{k}z_{k})
for
(y,z_{k})\in\mathcal{H}\times(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}}
. Clearly, the map
\Gamma_{k}
is continuous and injective. Hence, by the Lusin–Suslin theorem (see, e.g.,
[
39
, Theorem 15.1]
), we obtain
\Gamma_{k}(\Xi)\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{H})
for any
\Xi\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}\times(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}})
