. Consequently we can define a set function
\widetilde{\nu}_{k}:\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}\times(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}})\to[0,1]
by
\widetilde{\nu}_{k}(\Xi):=\nu_{k}(\Gamma_{k}(\Xi))
for
\Xi\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}\times(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}})
. Again by the injectivity of
\Gamma_{k}
, we see that the set function
\widetilde{\nu}_{k}
is a
\sigma
-additive measure. Furthermore, since

\widetilde{\nu}_{k}(\mathcal{H}\times(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}})=\nu_{k}(\mathcal{H}\times\Upsilon_{k}((\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}}))=\pi_{k}(\Upsilon_{k}^{-1}(\Upsilon_{k}((\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}})))=\pi_{k}((\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}})=1,

we have
\widetilde{\nu}_{k}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}\times(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}})
. Observe that

\displaystyle\widetilde{\nu}_{k}(A\times(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}})=\nu_{k}(A\times\Upsilon_{k}((\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}}))=\nu_{k}(A\times\mathcal{H})=\pi(A)\ \ \text{for any $A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})$},

(5.26)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\nu}_{k}(\mathcal{H}\times B)=\nu_{k}(\mathcal{H}\times\Upsilon_{k}(B))=\pi_{k}(\Upsilon_{k}^{-1}(\Upsilon_{k}(B)))=\pi_{k}(B)\ \ \text{for any $B\in\mathcal{B}\big((\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}}\big)$, and}

(5.27)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\nu}_{k}(\Gamma_{k}^{-1}(C))=\nu_{k}(\Gamma_{k}(\Gamma_{k}^{-1}(C)))=\nu_{k}(C\cap(\mathcal{H}\times\Upsilon_{k}((\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}})))=\nu_{k}(C)\ \ \text{for any $C\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{H})$}.

(5.28)

Consider a complete probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
which supports an
\mathcal{H}\times(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}}
-valued random variable
(Y_{0},Z_{k,0})
distributed according to
\widetilde{\nu}_{k}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}\times(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}})
and a
d
-dimensional Brownian motion
W
independent of
(Y_{0},Z_{k,0})
. We equip
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
with the augmented filtration
\mathbb{F}
generated by
(Y_{0},Z_{k,0})
and
W
. Under this setting, let
Y=(Y_{t})_{t\geq 0}
be the solution to the SEE (
1.2
) with the prescribed
\mathcal{H}
-valued initial condition
Y_{0}
, and let
Z_{k}=(Z_{k,t})_{t\geq 0}
be the solution to the approximating SDE (
5.2
) with the prescribed
(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}}
-valued initial condition
Z_{k,0}
. By (
5.26
) and (
5.27
), the joint distribution of the paths of
(Y_{t})_{t\geq 0}
and
(\Upsilon_{k}Z_{k,t})_{t\geq 0}
on
\Lambda\times\Lambda
constitutes a coupling between the probability measures
\mathbf{P}^{\pi}\in\mathcal{P}(\Lambda)
and
\mathbf{P}^{\pi_{k}}_{k}\circ\Upsilon_{k}^{-1}\in\mathcal{P}(\Lambda)
. Thus, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{W}_{\Lambda_{T}}\big(\mathbf{P}^{\pi},\mathbf{P}^{\pi_{k}}_{k}\circ\Upsilon_{k}^{-1}\big)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\big\|Y_{t}-\Upsilon_{k}Z_{k,t}\big\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+\int^{T}_{0}\big\|Y_{t}-\Upsilon_{k}Z_{k,t}\big\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{1/2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[C_{0}^{1/2}e^{C_{0}T/2}\big\|Y_{0}-\Upsilon_{k}Z_{k,0}\big\|_{\mathcal{H}}+C_{0}^{1/2}e^{C_{0}T/2}\varepsilon_{k}\left(1+\big\|\Upsilon_{k}Z_{k,0}\big\|_{\mathcal{H}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{H}\times(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}}}\left\{C_{0}^{1/2}e^{C_{0}T/2}\big\|y-\Upsilon_{k}z_{k}\big\|_{\mathcal{H}}+C_{0}^{1/2}e^{C_{0}T/2}\varepsilon_{k}\left(1+\big\|\Upsilon_{k}z_{k}\big\|_{\mathcal{H}}\right)\right\}\,\widetilde{\nu}_{k}(\mathrm{d}y,\mathrm{d}z_{k})

\displaystyle=C_{0}^{1/2}e^{C_{0}T/2}\int_{\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{H}}\big\|y-y^{\prime}\big\|_{\mathcal{H}}\,\nu_{k}(\mathrm{d}y,\mathrm{d}y^{\prime})+C_{0}^{1/2}e^{C_{0}T/2}\varepsilon_{k}\left(1+\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}}}\big\|\Upsilon_{k}z_{k}\big\|_{\mathcal{H}}\,\pi_{k}(\mathrm{d}z_{k})\right),

where we used the estimate (
5.5
) and the conditional Jensen’s inequality in the second inequality and the relations (
5.27
) and (
5.28
) in the final equality. Taking the infimum over
\nu_{k}\in\mathscr{C}(\pi,\pi_{k}\circ\Upsilon_{k}^{-1})
and then the supremum over
\pi_{k}\in\Pi_{k}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\sup_{\pi_{k}\in\Pi_{k}}\mathbb{W}_{\Lambda_{T}}\big(\mathbf{P}^{\pi},\mathbf{P}^{\pi_{k}}_{k}\circ\Upsilon_{k}^{-1}\big)

\displaystyle\leq C_{0}^{1/2}e^{C_{0}T/2}\sup_{\pi_{k}\in\Pi_{k}}\mathbb{W}_{\mathcal{H}}\big(\pi,\pi_{k}\circ\Upsilon_{k}^{-1}\big)+C_{0}e^{C_{0}T/2}\varepsilon_{k}\left(1+\sup_{\pi_{k}\in\Pi_{k}}\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}}}\big\|\Upsilon_{k}z_{k}\big\|_{\mathcal{H}}\,\pi_{k}(\mathrm{d}z_{k})\right).

By (
5.21
) and (
5.6
), we see that the right-hand side above tends to zero as
k\to\infty
, and hence (
5.20
) holds. This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 5.5
.

The proof of
5.3
also yields a quantitative estimate for the convergence (
5.20
). Specifically, we obtain a convergence order of
O(\varepsilon_{k}^{1/2})
, where
\varepsilon_{k}
is defined by (
5.1
). The exponent
1/2
stems from the square root in the definition (
3.7
) of the distance-like function
d_{\Phi,\Psi}
. Note, however, that this order is not necessarily optimal.

An important consequence of the convergence result (
5.20
) for the stationary laws on the path space
\Lambda
, rather than merely the convergence (
5.21
) for the invariant probability measures, is that it enables us to derive the corresponding convergence for the original SVE (
1.1
), as we will show in
5.6
below. Recall the observations in
2.16
(ii),
3.10
(ii) and
3.11
, where the stationary laws
\mathbf{Q}^{\pi}\in\mathcal{P}(L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(0,\infty;\mathbb{R}^{n}))
and
\widetilde{\mathbf{Q}}^{\pi}\in\mathcal{P}((\mathbb{R}^{n})^{[0,\infty)})
for the original SVE (
1.1
) were constructed from
\mathbf{P}^{\pi}\in\mathcal{P}(\Lambda)
; the former characterizes the distribution of the
\mathrm{d}t
-equivalence class of the stationary solution, while the latter characterizes the finite-dimensional distributions
\widetilde{\mathbf{Q}}^{\pi}_{(t_{1},\dots,t_{\ell})}\in\mathcal{P}((\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\ell})
of the stationary solution itself. Recall also that
\mathbf{Q}^{\pi}
and
\widetilde{\mathbf{Q}}^{\pi}
are independent of the choice of the lifting basis
(\mu,M_{b},M_{\sigma})
in the sense of
3.12
.

To establish the corresponding results on approximations of
\mathbf{Q}^{\pi}
and
\widetilde{\mathbf{Q}}^{\pi}
by means of the finite-dimensional SDEs (
5.2
), let us introduce the following additional notations. As in
2.16
(ii), for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
\nu_{k}\in\mathcal{P}((\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}})
, we set
\mathbf{Q}_{k}^{\nu_{k}}:=(\mathbf{P}_{k}^{\nu_{k}}\circ\Upsilon_{k}^{-1})\circ\mu[\cdot]^{-1}\in\mathcal{P}(L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(0,\infty;\mathbb{R}^{n}))
, where
\mu[\cdot]
is understood as a continuous map from
\Lambda
to
L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(0,\infty;\mathbb{R}^{n})
. This represents the law of the
\mathrm{d}t
-equivalence class of the
\mathbb{R}^{n}
-valued process
X_{k,t}:=\sum^{I_{k}}_{i=1}\mu(\Delta_{k}^{(i)})Z_{k,t}^{(i)}
,
t\geq 0
, where the
(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}}
-valued process
Z_{k}=(Z_{k,t})_{t\geq 0}
is a solution to the approximating SDE (
5.2
) with the initial distribution
\nu_{k}
. Recall that
X_{k}=(X_{k,t})_{t\geq 0}
solves the approximating SVE (
5.3
) with sum-of-exponentials type kernels; see
5.1
. Furthermore, we denote by
\widetilde{\mathbf{Q}}_{k}^{\nu_{k}}\in\mathcal{P}((\mathbb{R}^{n})^{[0,\infty)})
the law of
X_{k}=(X_{k,t})_{t\geq 0}
(not its
\mathrm{d}t
-equivalence class). This probability measure is consistent to the family of the finite-dimensional distributions
\widetilde{\mathbf{Q}}_{k,(t_{1},\dots,t_{\ell})}^{\nu_{k}}\in\mathcal{P}((\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\ell})
with
t_{1},\dots,t_{\ell}\geq 0
and
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, where

\widetilde{\mathbf{Q}}_{k,(t_{1},\dots,t_{\ell})}^{\nu_{k}}(A_{1}\times\cdots\times A_{\ell})=\mathbf{P}_{k}^{\nu_{k}}\left(\left\{\zeta_{k}=(\zeta_{k,t})_{t\geq 0}\in C([0,\infty);(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}})\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\mu[\Upsilon_{k}\zeta_{k,t_{1}}]\in A_{1},\dots,\mu[\Upsilon_{k}\zeta_{k,t_{\ell}}]\in A_{\ell}\right\}\right)

for any
A_{1},\dots,A_{\ell}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. These notations are comparable to the ones introduced in
3.11
. Note that, given an invariant probability measure
\pi_{k}\in\mathcal{P}((\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}})
for the Markov semigroup
\{P_{k,t}\}_{t\geq 0}
, the laws
\mathbf{Q}_{k}^{\pi_{k}}\in\mathcal{P}(L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(0,\infty;\mathbb{R}^{n}))
and
\widetilde{\mathbf{Q}}_{k}^{\pi_{k}}\in\mathcal{P}((\mathbb{R}^{n})^{[0,\infty)})
are invariant under the time-shifts on
L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(0,\infty;\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{[0,\infty)}
, respectively.

Similarly to (
5.19
), for each
T\in(0,\infty)
, we define an extended pseudo-metric
\mathbb{W}_{L^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{n})}:\mathcal{P}(L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(0,\infty;\mathbb{R}^{n}))\times\mathcal{P}(L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(0,\infty;\mathbb{R}^{n}))\to[0,\infty]
on
\mathcal{P}(L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(0,\infty;\mathbb{R}^{n}))
by

\mathbb{W}_{L^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{n})}(\mathfrak{q}_{1},\mathfrak{q}_{2}):=\inf_{\mathfrak{q}\in\mathscr{C}(\mathfrak{q}_{1},\mathfrak{q}_{2})}\int_{L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(0,\infty;\mathbb{R}^{n})\times L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(0,\infty;\mathbb{R}^{n})}\big\|\xi_{1}-\xi_{2}\big\|_{L^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{n})}\,\mathfrak{q}(\mathrm{d}\xi_{1},\mathrm{d}\xi_{2})

for
\mathfrak{q}_{1},\mathfrak{q}_{2}\in\mathcal{P}(L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(0,\infty;\mathbb{R}^{n}))
. Furthermore, for each
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, we define an extended metric
\mathbb{W}_{(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\ell}}:\mathcal{P}((\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\ell})\times\mathcal{P}((\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\ell})\to[0,\infty]
on
\mathcal{P}((\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\ell})
by

\mathbb{W}_{(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\ell}}\big(\widetilde{\mathfrak{q}}_{1},\widetilde{\mathfrak{q}}_{2}\big):=\inf_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{q}}\in\mathscr{C}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{q}}_{1},\widetilde{\mathfrak{q}}_{2})}\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\ell}\times(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\ell}}\max_{j\in\{1,\dots,\ell\}}\big|x_{1,j}-x_{2,j}\big|\,\widetilde{\mathfrak{q}}\big(\mathrm{d}(x_{1,j})^{\ell}_{j=1},\mathrm{d}(x_{2,j})^{\ell}_{j=1}\big)

for
\widetilde{\mathfrak{q}}_{1},\widetilde{\mathfrak{q}}_{2}\in\mathcal{P}((\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\ell})
, where
|\cdot|
denotes the standard Euclidean norm on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Now we are ready to state a corollary of
5.3
.

Corollary 5.6
.

Under the same setting of
5.3
, for any
T\in(0,\infty)
and
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

\lim_{k\to\infty}\sup_{\pi_{k}\in\Pi_{k}}\mathbb{W}_{L^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{n})}\big(\mathbf{Q}^{\pi},\mathbf{Q}^{\pi_{k}}_{k}\big)=0,

(5.29)

and

\lim_{k\to\infty}\sup_{\pi_{k}\in\Pi_{k}}\sup_{t_{1},\dots,t_{\ell}\in[0,T]}\mathbb{W}_{(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\ell}}\left(\widetilde{\mathbf{Q}}^{\pi}_{(t_{1},\dots,t_{\ell})},\widetilde{\mathbf{Q}}^{\pi_{k}}_{k,(t_{1},\dots,t_{\ell})}\right)=0.

(5.30)

Remark 5.7
.

The convergence result (
5.29
) for the laws of the
\mathrm{d}t
-equivalence classes is an immediate consequence of (
5.20
), by virtue of the continuity of the map
\mu[\cdot]:\Lambda\to L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(0,\infty;\mathbb{R}^{n})
, as demonstrated in the first part of the proof below. In contrast, the convergence result (
5.30
) for the finite-dimensional distributions is less trivial, as the pointwise evaluation
\eta\mapsto(\mu[\eta_{t_{1}}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{\mathcal{V}}(\eta_{t_{1}})],\dots,\mu[\eta_{t_{\ell}}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{\mathcal{V}}(\eta_{t_{\ell}})])
is not necessarily continuous from
\Lambda
to
(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\ell}
, and (
5.20
) does not necessarily imply weak convergence in
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{V})
for a fixed time parameter; see also
5.4
(ii). Nevertheless, by leveraging the property of the map
\mu[\cdot]:\mathcal{V}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
in
2.10
, combined with the integrability results (
3.8
) and (
5.6
) for the invariant probability measures with respect to the
\mathcal{V}
-norm, we can derive (
5.30
) from (
5.20
). This derivation is detailed in the second part of the proof.

Proof of
5.6
.

The convergence (
5.29
) immediately follows from (
5.20
) and the following estimate:

\|\mu[\eta]\|_{L^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq\|\mu[\cdot]\|_{L(\mathcal{V};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\left(\int^{T}_{0}\|\eta_{t}\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{1/2}\leq\|\mu[\cdot]\|_{L(\mathcal{V};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\|\eta\|_{\Lambda_{T}}

for any
\eta\in\Lambda
. Indeed, for any
k\geq k_{0}
,
\pi_{k}\in\Pi_{k}
and
\mathfrak{p}_{k}\in\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{P}^{\pi},\mathbf{P}^{\pi_{k}}_{k}\circ\Upsilon_{k}^{-1})
, notice that the probability measure
\mathfrak{q}_{k}\in\mathcal{P}(L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(0,\infty;\mathbb{R}^{n})\times L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(0,\infty;\mathbb{R}^{n}))
given by

\mathfrak{q}_{k}(D):=\mathfrak{p}_{k}\left(\left\{(\eta,\eta^{\prime})\in\Lambda\times\Lambda\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\big(\mu[\eta],\mu[\eta^{\prime}]\big)\in D\right\}\right),\ \ D\in\mathcal{B}\big(L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(0,\infty;\mathbb{R}^{n})\times L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(0,\infty;\mathbb{R}^{n})\big),

constitutes a coupling between the probability measures
\mathbf{Q}^{\pi}\in\mathcal{P}(L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(0,\infty;\mathbb{R}^{n}))
and
\mathbf{Q}_{k}^{\pi_{k}}\in\mathcal{P}(L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(0,\infty;\mathbb{R}^{n}))
. Hence,

\displaystyle\mathbb{W}_{L^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{n})}\big(\mathbf{Q}^{\pi},\mathbf{Q}_{k}^{\pi_{k}}\big)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(0,\infty;\mathbb{R}^{n})\times L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(0,\infty;\mathbb{R}^{n})}\big\|\xi-\xi^{\prime}\big\|_{L^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{n})}\,\mathfrak{q}_{k}(\mathrm{d}\xi,\mathrm{d}\xi^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int_{\Lambda\times\Lambda}\big\|\mu[\eta-\eta^{\prime}]\big\|_{L^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{n})}\,\mathfrak{p}_{k}(\mathrm{d}\eta,\mathrm{d}\eta^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq\|\mu[\cdot]\|_{L(\mathcal{V};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\int_{\Lambda\times\Lambda}\|\eta-\eta^{\prime}\|_{\Lambda_{T}}\,\mathfrak{p}_{k}(\mathrm{d}\eta,\mathrm{d}\eta^{\prime}).

Taking the infimum over
\mathfrak{p}_{k}\in\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{P}^{\pi},\mathbf{P}^{\pi_{k}}_{k}\circ\Upsilon_{k}^{-1})
and then the supremum over
\pi_{k}\in\Pi_{k}
, we obtain

\sup_{\pi_{k}\in\Pi_{k}}\mathbb{W}_{L^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{n})}\big(\mathbf{Q}^{\pi},\mathbf{Q}_{k}^{\pi_{k}}\big)\leq\|\mu[\cdot]\|_{L(\mathcal{V};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\sup_{\pi_{k}\in\Pi_{k}}\mathbb{W}_{\Lambda_{T}}\big(\mathbf{P}^{\pi},\mathbf{P}^{\pi_{k}}_{k}\circ\Upsilon_{k}^{-1}\big).

Since
\|\mu[\cdot]\|_{L(\mathcal{V};\mathbb{R}^{n})}<\infty
, by (
5.20
), we obtain (
5.29
).

To prove the convergence (
5.30
) of finite-dimensional distributions, let
k\geq k_{0}
,
\pi_{k}\in\Pi_{k}
,
t_{1},\dots,t_{\ell}\in[0,T]
and
\mathfrak{p}_{k}\in\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{P}^{\pi},\mathbf{P}^{\pi_{k}}_{k}\circ\Upsilon_{k}^{-1})
be fixed. Noting
3.11
and
\Upsilon_{k}:(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}}\to\mathcal{V}
, we see that

\mathfrak{p}_{k}\left(\left\{(\eta,\eta^{\prime})\in\Lambda\times\Lambda\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\eta_{t_{1}},\dots,\eta_{t_{\ell}},\eta^{\prime}_{t_{1}},\dots,\eta^{\prime}_{t_{\ell}}\in\mathcal{V}\right\}\right)=1.

Define
\widetilde{\mathfrak{q}}_{k}\in\mathcal{P}((\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\ell}\times(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\ell})
by

\widetilde{\mathfrak{q}}_{k}(E):=\mathfrak{p}_{k}\left(\left\{(\eta,\eta^{\prime})\in\Lambda\times\Lambda\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\begin{aligned} &\eta_{t_{1}},\dots,\eta_{t_{\ell}},\eta^{\prime}_{t_{1}},\dots,\eta^{\prime}_{t_{\ell}}\in\mathcal{V}\ \text{and}\\
&\left(\big(\mu\big[\eta_{t_{j}}\big]\big)^{\ell}_{j=1},\big(\mu\big[\eta^{\prime}_{t_{j}}\big]\big)^{\ell}_{j=1}\right)\in E\end{aligned}\right\}\right),\ \ E\in\mathcal{B}\big((\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\ell}\times(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\ell}\big).

Then, we see that
\widetilde{\mathfrak{q}}_{k}
is a coupling between the probability measures
\widetilde{\mathbf{Q}}^{\pi}_{(t_{1},\dots,t_{\ell})}\in\mathcal{P}((\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\ell})
and
\widetilde{\mathbf{Q}}^{\pi_{k}}_{k,(t_{1},\dots,t_{\ell})}\in\mathcal{P}((\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\ell})
. Hence, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{W}_{(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\ell}}\left(\widetilde{\mathbf{Q}}^{\pi}_{(t_{1},\dots,t_{\ell})},\widetilde{\mathbf{Q}}^{\pi_{k}}_{k,(t_{1},\dots,t_{\ell})}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\ell}\times(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\ell}}\max_{j\in\{1,\dots,\ell\}}\big|x_{j}-x^{\prime}_{j}\big|\,\widetilde{\mathfrak{q}}_{k}\big(\mathrm{d}(x_{j})^{\ell}_{j=1},\mathrm{d}(x^{\prime}_{j})^{\ell}_{j=1}\big)

\displaystyle=\int_{\Lambda\times\Lambda}\max_{j\in\{1,\dots,\ell\}}\big|\mu\big[\eta_{t_{j}}-\eta^{\prime}_{t_{j}}\big]\big|\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{\mathcal{V}^{2\ell}}\big(\eta_{t_{1}},\dots,\eta_{t_{\ell}},\eta^{\prime}_{t_{1}},\dots,\eta^{\prime}_{t_{\ell}}\big)\,\mathfrak{p}_{k}(\mathrm{d}\eta,\mathrm{d}\eta^{\prime}).

Fix an arbitrary number
\varepsilon>0
, and let
C_{\mu,\varepsilon}>0
be the constant arising in
2.10
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{W}_{(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\ell}}\left(\widetilde{\mathbf{Q}}^{\pi}_{(t_{1},\dots,t_{\ell})},\widetilde{\mathbf{Q}}^{\pi_{k}}_{k,(t_{1},\dots,t_{\ell})}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\Lambda\times\Lambda}\max_{j\in\{1,\dots,\ell\}}\Big(\varepsilon\big\|\eta_{t_{j}}-\eta^{\prime}_{t_{j}}\big\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}+C_{\mu,\varepsilon}\big\|\eta_{t_{j}}-\eta^{\prime}_{t_{j}}\big\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\Big)^{1/2}\,\mathfrak{p}_{k}(\mathrm{d}\eta,\mathrm{d}\eta^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon^{1/2}\sum^{\ell}_{j=1}\left\{\int_{\Lambda}\|\eta_{t_{j}}\|_{\mathcal{V}}\,\mathbf{P}^{\pi}(\mathrm{d}\eta)+\int_{C([0,\infty);(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}})}\big\|\Upsilon_{k}\zeta_{k,t_{j}}\big\|_{\mathcal{V}}\,\mathbf{P}^{\pi_{k}}_{k}(\mathrm{d}\zeta_{k})\right\}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+C_{\mu,\varepsilon}^{1/2}\int_{\Lambda\times\Lambda}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\eta_{t}-\eta^{\prime}_{t}\|_{\mathcal{H}}\,\mathfrak{p}_{k}(\mathrm{d}\eta,\mathrm{d}\eta^{\prime}),

where the first inequality follows from
2.10
, while the second is a consequence of the fact that
\mathfrak{p}_{k}\in\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{P}^{\pi},\mathbf{P}^{\pi_{k}}_{k}\circ\Upsilon_{k}^{-1})
. By the invariance of
\pi\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H})
and
\pi_{k}\in\mathcal{P}((\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}})
with respect to
\{P_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
and
\{P_{k,t}\}_{t\geq 0}
, respectively, and the inequality
\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\eta_{t}-\eta^{\prime}_{t}\big\|_{\mathcal{H}}\leq\|\eta-\eta^{\prime}\|_{\Lambda_{T}}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{W}_{(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\ell}}\left(\widetilde{\mathbf{Q}}^{\pi}_{(t_{1},\dots,t_{\ell})},\widetilde{\mathbf{Q}}^{\pi_{k}}_{k,(t_{1},\dots,t_{\ell})}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon^{1/2}\ell\left\{\int_{\mathcal{H}}\|y\|_{\mathcal{V}}\,\pi(\mathrm{d}y)+\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}}}\big\|\Upsilon_{k}z_{k}\big\|_{\mathcal{V}}\,\pi_{k}(\mathrm{d}z_{k})\right\}+C_{\mu,\varepsilon}^{1/2}\int_{\Lambda\times\Lambda}\|\eta-\eta^{\prime}\|_{\Lambda_{T}}\,\mathfrak{p}_{k}(\mathrm{d}\eta,\mathrm{d}\eta^{\prime}).

Taking the infimum over
\mathfrak{p}_{k}\in\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{P}^{\pi},\mathbf{P}^{\pi_{k}}_{k}\circ\Upsilon_{k}^{-1})
and then the supremum over
t_{1},\dots,t_{\ell}\in[0,T]
and
\pi_{k}\in\Pi_{k}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\sup_{\pi_{k}\in\Pi_{k}}\sup_{t_{1},\dots,t_{\ell}\in[0,T]}\mathbb{W}_{(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\ell}}\left(\widetilde{\mathbf{Q}}^{\pi}_{(t_{1},\dots,t_{\ell})},\widetilde{\mathbf{Q}}^{\pi_{k}}_{k,(t_{1},\dots,t_{\ell})}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon^{1/2}\ell\left\{\int_{\mathcal{H}}\|y\|_{\mathcal{V}}\,\pi(\mathrm{d}y)+\sup_{\pi_{k}\in\Pi_{k}}\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}}}\big\|\Upsilon_{k}z_{k}\big\|_{\mathcal{V}}\,\pi_{k}(\mathrm{d}z_{k})\right\}+C_{\mu,\varepsilon}^{1/2}\sup_{\pi_{k}\in\Pi_{k}}\mathbb{W}_{\Lambda_{T}}\big(\mathbf{P}^{\pi},\mathbf{P}^{\pi_{k}}_{k}\circ\Upsilon_{k}^{-1}\big).

By using (
5.20
), we have

\displaystyle\limsup_{k\to\infty}\sup_{\pi_{k}\in\Pi_{k}}\sup_{t_{1},\dots,t_{\ell}\in[0,T]}\mathbb{W}_{(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\ell}}\left(\widetilde{\mathbf{Q}}^{\pi}_{(t_{1},\dots,t_{\ell})},\widetilde{\mathbf{Q}}^{\pi_{k}}_{k,(t_{1},\dots,t_{\ell})}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon^{1/2}\ell\left\{\int_{\mathcal{H}}\|y\|_{\mathcal{V}}\,\pi(\mathrm{d}y)+\sup_{k\geq k_{0}}\sup_{\pi_{k}\in\Pi_{k}}\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{I_{k}}}\big\|\Upsilon_{k}z_{k}\big\|_{\mathcal{V}}\,\pi_{k}(\mathrm{d}z_{k})\right\}.

In view of the integrability conditions (
3.8
) and (
5.6
), taking the limit
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
yields the desired assertion (
5.30
). This completes the proof.
∎

Appendix A
Appendix

In the context of monotone SPDEs defined on a Gelfand triplet
\mathcal{V}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{H}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{V}^{*}
, it is essential to determine whether the embedding
\mathcal{V}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{H}
is compact. Indeed, while various general theories exist, several fundamental results—including the well-posedness for SPDEs with fully local monotone coefficients
[
47
,
56
]
and the ergodicity derived from ultimate boundedness
[
21
, Chapter 7]
—require this compactness property. The following lemma provides a characterization of the compactness of the embedding in our framework. It reveals that, in typical cases of interest, the embedding
\mathcal{V}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{H}
is not compact, which means that the aforementioned results cannot be directly applied to our setting.

Lemma A.1
.

For the Banach spaces
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{V}
defined in
Section

2
, the embedding
\mathcal{V}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{H}
is compact if and only if
{\mathrm{supp}\,\mu}\cap[0,m]
is a finite set for any
m\in(0,\infty)
.

Remark A.2
.

By virtue of the Lebesgue decomposition of the Borel measure
\mu
on
[0,\infty)
, the condition that
{\mathrm{supp}\,\mu}\cap[0,m]
is a finite set for every
m\in(0,\infty)
is equivalent to
\mu
being of the form
\mu=\sum^{\infty}_{i=1}c_{i}\delta_{\theta_{i}}
for some
c_{i},\theta_{i}\geq 0
with
\lim_{i\to\infty}\theta_{i}=\infty
. Specifically, for the lifting basis generating the sum-of-exponentials type kernels in
2.9
(i), the embedding
\mathcal{V}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{H}
is indeed compact. In contrast, in typical cases of interest, such as the tempered fractional kernels in
2.9
(ii), the embedding
\mathcal{V}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{H}
is never compact.

Proof of
A.1
.

To prove the “if part”, suppose that
{\mathrm{supp}\,\mu}\cap[0,m]
is a finite set for any
m\in(0,\infty)
. If
{\mathrm{supp}\,\mu}
itself is a finite set, then the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
is finite-dimensional, making the embedding
\mathcal{V}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{H}
trivially compact. Otherwise, let
{\mathrm{supp}\,\mu}=\{\theta_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
be an infinite set, where
\{\theta_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\subset[0,\infty)
is a strictly increasing sequence such that
\lim_{i\to\infty}\theta_{i}=\infty
. In this case, the measure
\mu
is of the form
\mu=\sum^{\infty}_{i=1}c_{i}\delta_{\theta_{i}}
with
c_{i}>0
. Let
\overline{B}_{\mathcal{V}}:=\{y\in\mathcal{V}\,|\,\|y\|_{\mathcal{V}}\leq 1\}
be the unit ball in
\mathcal{V}
, and take an arbitrary sequence
\{y_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\overline{B}_{\mathcal{V}}
. Noting that
\{y_{k}(\theta_{i})\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is bounded for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
, by Cantor’s diagonal argument, we can find an increasing sequence
\{k_{\ell}\}_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathbb{N}
and a sequence
\{e_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
\lim_{\ell\to\infty}y_{k_{\ell}}(\theta_{i})=e_{i}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
for any
i\in\mathbb{N}
. Define
y:=\sum^{\infty}_{i=1}e_{i}\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{\{\theta_{i}\}}
. By Fatou’s lemma, we have

\|y\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}=\sum^{\infty}_{i=1}c_{i}(1+\theta_{i})^{1/2}|e_{i}|^{2}\leq\liminf_{\ell\to\infty}\sum^{\infty}_{i=1}c_{i}(1+\theta_{i})^{1/2}|y_{k_{\ell}}(\theta_{i})|^{2}=\liminf_{\ell\to\infty}\big\|y_{k_{\ell}}\big\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\leq 1,

and hence
y\in\overline{B}_{\mathcal{V}}
. Moreover, since the sequence
\{\theta_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\subset[0,\infty)
is increasing, we have

\|y_{k_{\ell}}-y\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}=\sum^{\infty}_{j=1}c_{j}(1+\theta_{j})^{-1/2}\big|y_{k_{\ell}}(\theta_{j})-e_{j}\big|^{2}\leq\sum^{i}_{j=1}c_{j}(1+\theta_{j})^{-1/2}\big|y_{k_{\ell}}(\theta_{j})-e_{j}\big|^{2}+(1+\theta_{i+1})^{-1}\|y_{k_{\ell}}-y\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}

for any
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
and
i\in\mathbb{N}
. For each
i\in\mathbb{N}
, the first term in the right-hand side tends to zero as
\ell\to\infty
. The second term tends to zero as
i\to\infty
uniformly in
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, since
\|y_{k_{\ell}}-y\|_{\mathcal{V}}\leq 2
and
\lim_{i\to\infty}\theta_{i}=\infty
. Thus,
\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\|y_{k_{\ell}}-y\|_{\mathcal{H}}=0
, proving that the set
\overline{B}_{\mathcal{V}}
is sequentially compact in
\mathcal{H}
. Consequently, the embedding
\mathcal{V}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{H}
is compact.

To prove the “only if” part, suppose that there exists an
m\in(0,\infty)
such that
{\mathrm{supp}\,\mu}\cap[0,m]
is an infinite set. We can then choose a strictly increasing sequence
\{\theta_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\subset{\mathrm{supp}\,\mu}\cap(0,m)
. Let
\theta_{0}=0
, and define
A_{i}:=(\frac{\theta_{i-1}+\theta_{i}}{2},\frac{\theta_{i}+\theta_{i+1}}{2})
for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
. By construction,
\{A_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is a sequence of disjoint open intervals contained in
[0,m]
such that
\mu(A_{i})\in(0,\infty)
for any
i\in\mathbb{N}
. Take an
e\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
|e|=1
. For each
i\in\mathbb{N}
, define
c_{i}:=\int_{A_{i}}(1+\theta)^{1/2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)\in(0,\infty)
and
y_{i}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{c_{i}}}e\mbox{\rm{1}}\hskip-2.5pt\mbox{\rm{l}}_{A_{i}}\in\mathcal{V}
. Then, we have
\|y_{i}\|_{\mathcal{V}}=1
for any
i\in\mathbb{N}
. However, for any
i,j\in\mathbb{N}
with
i\neq j
, we have

\|y_{i}-y_{j}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}=\frac{\int_{A_{i}}(1+\theta)^{-1/2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)}{\int_{A_{i}}(1+\theta)^{1/2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)}+\frac{\int_{A_{j}}(1+\theta)^{-1/2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)}{\int_{A_{j}}(1+\theta)^{1/2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}\theta)}\geq\frac{2}{1+m}.

Thus,
\{y_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
does not have a Cauchy subsequence in
\mathcal{H}
, implying that the embedding
\mathcal{V}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{H}
is not compact.
∎

The following lemma establishes some fundamental properties of the path space of the Markovian lift. More generally, it addresses the path space of solutions to general monotone SPDEs defined on a Gelfand triplet. Here, we consider a general setting that goes beyond the specific framework of Markovian lifts.

Lemma A.3
.

Let
(\mathcal{H},\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{H}},\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathcal{H}})
be a separable Hilbert space and
\mathcal{V}
be a dense subspace of
\mathcal{H}
. Assume that
\mathcal{V}
is equipped with a norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{V}}
such that
(\mathcal{V},\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{V}})
is a reflexive separable Banach space continuously embedded into
\mathcal{H}
. Define

\Lambda:=\left\{\eta:[0,\infty)\to\mathcal{H}\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\text{$t\mapsto\eta_{t}$ is strongly continuous in $\mathcal{H}$ and}\ \int^{T}_{0}\|\eta_{t}\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty\ \text{for any $T>0$}\right\}

and

d_{\Lambda}(\eta,\eta^{\prime}):=\sum_{T\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{1}{2^{T}}\big\{\|\eta-\eta^{\prime}\|_{\Lambda_{T}}\wedge 1\big\}\ \ \text{for $\eta,\eta^{\prime}\in\Lambda$},

where

\|\eta\|_{\Lambda_{T}}:=\left(\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\eta_{t}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+\int^{T}_{0}\|\eta_{t}\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{1/2}\ \ \text{for $\eta\in\Lambda$ and $T\in(0,\infty)$}.

Then,
(\Lambda,d_{\Lambda})
is a complete separable metric space, and
\{\|\cdot\|_{\Lambda_{T}}\}_{T\in\mathbb{N}}
is a countable family of seminorms on
\Lambda
generating the topology of
(\Lambda,d_{\Lambda})
. Furthermore, the Borel
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{B}(\Lambda)
coincides with the
\sigma
-algebra generated by the family
\{\{\eta\in\Lambda\,|\,\eta_{t}\in A\}\,|\,t\in[0,\infty),\,A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})\}
.

Remark A.4
.

(i)

Since
\mathcal{V}
and
\mathcal{H}
are separable Banach spaces with continuous embedding
\mathcal{V}\subset\mathcal{H}
, the Lusin–Suslin theorem (see, e.g.,
[
39
, Theorem 15.1]
) implies that
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{V})=\{A\cap\mathcal{V}\,|\,A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})\}\subset\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
. Furthermore, by defining
\|y\|_{\mathcal{V}}=\infty
for
y\in\mathcal{H}\setminus\mathcal{V}
, the map
\mathcal{H}\ni y\mapsto\|y\|_{\mathcal{V}}\in[0,\infty]
is lower semi-continuous on
\mathcal{H}
and hence
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
-measurable (see, e.g.,
[
47
, Exercise 4.2.3]
). Hence, the integral
\int^{T}_{0}\|\eta_{t}\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\in[0,\infty]
is well-defined for all
T>0
for every
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
-measurable function
\eta:[0,\infty)\to\mathcal{H}
.

(ii)

By the final assertion of
A.3
, for every
\mathcal{H}
-valued measurable process
Y=(Y_{t})_{t\geq 0}
on a complete probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
such that
t\mapsto Y_{t}
is strongly continuous in
\mathcal{H}
and
\int^{T}_{0}\|Y_{t}\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty
a.s. for any
T>0
, the sample-path map
\omega\mapsto(Y_{t}(\omega))_{t\geq 0}
defines a Borel-measurable random variable taking values in the Polish space
\Lambda
, and its law on
\Lambda
is uniquely characterized by the “finite-dimensional distributions”
\mathbb{P}((Y_{t_{1}},\dots,Y_{t_{\ell}})\in A)
with
t_{1},\dots,t_{\ell}\in[0,\infty)
,
A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}^{\ell})
and
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
.

Although the results above are expected to be well-known and have been implicitly invoked in studies on monotone SPDEs (e.g.,
[
47
, Appendix E]
and
[
33
]
), a formal proof is often omitted. For completeness, we provide a proof below. Recall that for a separable Banach space
(\mathcal{X},\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{X}})
, the space
C([0,\infty);\mathcal{X})
of
\mathcal{X}
-valued continuous functions on
[0,\infty)
, equipped with the metric defined by (
2.1
), is a complete separable metric space.

Proof of
A.3
.

Obviously,
d_{\Lambda}
defines a metric on
\Lambda
, and
\{\|\cdot\|_{\Lambda_{T}}\}_{T\in\mathbb{N}}
is a countable family of seminorms on
\Lambda
generating the topology of
(\Lambda,d_{\Lambda})
. In the following, we prove the completeness and separability of
\Lambda
, as well as the identification of its associated Borel
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{B}(\Lambda)
. To this end, we observe from the embedding
\mathcal{V}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{H}
and the definition of the metric
d_{\Lambda}
that

C([0,\infty);\mathcal{V})\hookrightarrow\Lambda\hookrightarrow C([0,\infty);\mathcal{H}).

(A.1)

To prove the completeness of
\Lambda
, let
\{\eta^{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be a Cauchy sequence in
\Lambda
. By the continuous embedding (
A.1
),
\{\eta^{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is also a Cauchy sequence in the complete metric space
C([0,\infty);\mathcal{H})
. Hence there exists an
\eta\in C([0,\infty);\mathcal{H})
such that
\lim_{k\to\infty}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\eta^{k}_{t}-\eta_{t}\|_{\mathcal{H}}=0
for any
T\in(0,\infty)
. Furthermore, the lower semi-continuity of the map
\mathcal{H}\ni y\mapsto\|y\|_{\mathcal{V}}\in[0,\infty]
and Fatou’s lemma yield

\int^{T}_{0}\|\eta_{t}\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq\int^{T}_{0}\liminf_{k\to\infty}\big\|\eta^{k}_{t}\big\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq\liminf_{k\to\infty}\int^{T}_{0}\big\|\eta^{k}_{t}\big\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty

and

\limsup_{k\to\infty}\int^{T}_{0}\big\|\eta^{k}_{t}-\eta_{t}\big\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq\limsup_{k\to\infty}\int^{T}_{0}\liminf_{\ell\to\infty}\big\|\eta^{k}_{t}-\eta^{\ell}_{t}\big\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq\lim_{k,\ell\to\infty}\int^{T}_{0}\big\|\eta^{k}_{t}-\eta^{\ell}_{t}\big\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t=0

for any
T\in(0,\infty)
. Hence, we have
\eta\in\Lambda
and
\lim_{k\to\infty}d_{\Lambda}(\eta^{k},\eta)=0
. This demonstrates that the metric space
(\Lambda,d_{\Lambda})
is complete.

To prove the separability of
\Lambda
, recalling the continuous embedding (
A.1
) and the separability of
C([0,\infty);\mathcal{V})
, it suffices to show that
C([0,\infty);\mathcal{V})
is dense in
\Lambda
. Let
\eta\in\Lambda
be fixed. We extend the domain of
\eta
to
\mathbb{R}
by setting
\eta_{t}:=\eta_{-t}
for
t\in(-\infty,0)
. Then, we can regard
\eta
as a strongly continuous map from
\mathbb{R}
to
\mathcal{H}
such that
\int_{[-T,T]}\|\eta_{t}\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty
for any
T\in(0,\infty)
. For each
\varepsilon>0
, let
\phi_{\varepsilon}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be the standard mollifier supported on
[-\varepsilon,\varepsilon]
, and define
\eta^{\varepsilon}_{t}:=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\phi_{\varepsilon}(t-s)\eta_{s}\,\mathrm{d}s
for
t\in[0,\infty)
, where the integral is well-defined as a Bochner integral on
\mathcal{H}
. On the one hand, thanks to the integrability
\int_{[-T,T]}\|\eta_{t}\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty
for each
T>0
, we see that
\eta^{\varepsilon}\in C([0,\infty);\mathcal{V})
for each
\varepsilon>0
and
\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\int_{[0,T]}\|\eta^{\varepsilon}_{t}-\eta_{t}\|_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t=0
for any
T>0
. On the other hand, thanks to the strong continuity of
\mathbb{R}\ni t\mapsto\eta_{t}
in
\mathcal{H}
, we have
\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\eta^{\varepsilon}_{t}-\eta_{t}\|_{\mathcal{H}}=0
for any
T>0
. Thus,
\eta^{\varepsilon}
converges to
\eta
in
\Lambda
as
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
, showing the density of
C([0,\infty);\mathcal{V})
in
\Lambda
. Hence,
\Lambda
is separable.

We prove the last assertion for the Borel
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{B}(\Lambda)
on
\Lambda
. First, since both
\Lambda
and
C([0,\infty);\mathcal{H})
are Polish spaces and
\Lambda\hookrightarrow C([0,\infty);\mathcal{H})
, the Lusin–Suslin theorem (see, e.g.,
[
39
, Theorem 15.1]
) implies that
\Lambda\in\mathcal{B}(C([0,\infty);\mathcal{H}))
and
\mathcal{B}(\Lambda)=\{B\cap\Lambda\,|\,B\in\mathcal{B}(C([0,\infty);\mathcal{H}))\}
. Denote by
\mathcal{G}(C([0,\infty);\mathcal{H}))
the
\sigma
-algebra on
C([0,\infty);\mathcal{H})
generated by the family of cylinder sets
\{\{\eta\in C([0,\infty);\mathcal{H})\,|\,\eta_{t}\in A\}\,|\,t\in[0,\infty),\,A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})\}
. As a next step, we show that
\mathcal{B}(C([0,\infty);\mathcal{H}))=\mathcal{G}(C([0,\infty);\mathcal{H}))
. On the one hand, since every evaluation map
\eta\mapsto\eta_{t}
for
t\in[0,\infty)
is continuous from
C([0,\infty);\mathcal{H})
to
\mathcal{H}
, we have
\mathcal{G}(C([0,\infty);\mathcal{H}))\subset\mathcal{B}(C([0,\infty);\mathcal{H}))
. On the other hand, for any
\check{\eta}\in C([0,\infty);\mathcal{H})
,
T>0
and
r>0
, we have

\left\{\eta\in C([0,\infty);\mathcal{H})\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\big\|\eta_{t}-\check{\eta}_{t}\big\|_{\mathcal{H}}<r\right\}=\bigcup_{q\in(0,r)\cap\mathbb{Q}}\bigcap_{s\in[0,T]\cap\mathbb{Q}}\left\{\eta\in C([0,\infty);\mathcal{H})\mathrel{}\middle|\mathrel{}\big\|\eta_{s}-\check{\eta}_{s}\big\|_{\mathcal{H}}\leq q\right\},

which belongs to
\mathcal{G}(C([0,\infty);\mathcal{H}))
. Since the topology of
C([0,\infty);\mathcal{H})
is generated by the family of sets of the above forms, we obtain
\mathcal{B}(C([0,\infty);\mathcal{H}))\subset\mathcal{G}(C([0,\infty);\mathcal{H}))
, concluding that
\mathcal{B}(C([0,\infty);\mathcal{H}))=\mathcal{G}(C([0,\infty);\mathcal{H}))
. Therefore, the Borel
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{B}(\Lambda)
on
\Lambda
, which is equal to
\{B\cap\Lambda\,|\,B\in\mathcal{B}(C([0,\infty);\mathcal{H}))\}
, coincides with
\{B\cap\Lambda\,|\,B\in\mathcal{G}(C([0,\infty);\mathcal{H}))\}
. By the standard monotone-class argument, we see that the latter is also equal to the
\sigma
-algebra generated by the family
\{\{\eta\in\Lambda\,|\,\eta_{t}\in A\}\,|\,t\in[0,\infty),\,A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})\}
. This completes the proof.
∎
